An ancient English Cathedral Tower—massive, grey, square, planted like a stubborn old root in the landscape—rose where it had no right to be. It stood behind everything the eye could name, behind the daybreak haze, behind the very thought that summoned it. Yet between the watcher and that familiar tower there thrust up a spike: thin, rusty, upright, insolent.

Who had put the spike there?

In the same breath, the air broke into clangour. Cymbals struck hard as tin trays slapped in a scullery. A Sultan went by in long procession, as if the alley outside had become a parade-ground; scimitars winked like knives lifted to a kitchen lamp; dancing-girls scattered flowers that might have been herbs torn up too roughly—mint and marigold, bruised to scent. White elephants followed, caparisoned in colours like spilled spice—saffron, paprika, verdigris—endless, attended by endless feet. The Cathedral Tower would not yield its place in the background. The spike waited for a writhing body that never came.

Stay. The spike was lower, meaner. Not a stake for impalement at all, but the rusty iron upright of a broken bedstead—one corner kicked awry, as if some heavy sleeper had fought his own dreams and lost.

There should have been laughter at that discovery; there was, somewhere in the fog of thought, a vague period of drowsy mirth. Then the man—shaking from head to foot as if he had been held too long over a fire that gives no warmth—pushed himself up, braced on his arms, and looked about.

He was in the meanest, closest of small rooms: a box of stale air and stained plaster. Through a ragged curtain the early light seeped in, not clean and gold, but thin and grudging, like broth poured out after the meat is gone. It came from a miserable court below, where damp lay in corners and the day’s first sounds were not birdsong but the scuffle of rats and the clack of a distant latch.

He lay dressed, across a large unseemly bed, upon the very bedstead that had given way. The iron post he had dreamed as a murderous spike was beside him indeed, jutting up with the dull red-brown of old rust.

Not longwise across the bed but athwart it too, dressed as he was, were three others: a Chinaman; a Lascar; and a haggard woman whose thinness made her seem made of string and ash. The two men were sunk in sleep or stupor. The woman bent over a little pipe, blowing at it to kindle it, shading the ember with her lean hand so that the red spark glowed brighter—an ugly kind of hearth, a small private dawn. With every breath she drew the room’s sourness through her lungs and sent it back out with a sickly sweetness that clung to the throat and hair and clothes.

“Another?” she whispered—querulous, rattling, as if her voice had been kept too long in a cupboard with broken crockery. “Have another?”

He stared, his hand to his forehead, trying to gather the scattered pieces of himself as one might try to gather spilt flour with wet fingers.

“Ye’ve smoked as many as five since ye come in at midnight,” the woman went on in her chronic complaint. “Poor me, poor me, my head is so bad. Them two come in after ye. Ah, poor me, the business is slack, is slack! Few Chinamen about the Docks, and fewer Lascars, and no ships coming in, these say! Here’s another ready for ye, deary. Ye’ll remember like a good soul, won’t ye, that the market price is dreffle high just now? More nor three shillings and sixpence for a thimbleful! And ye’ll remember that nobody but me—(and Jack Chinaman t’other side the court; but he can’t do it as well as me)—has the true secret of mixing it? Ye’ll pay up accordingly, deary, won’t ye?”

As she spoke she blew again, and now and then bubbled at the mouthpiece, taking in the opiate vapour herself. It was a strange domestic industry: a cookery of poison, carefully portioned, pride taken in the “true secret” as if it were a family pickle handed down. The smell had layers—burnt sweetness, damp ash, an undernote like old oranges left too long in a drawer—yet it did not belong to any honest kitchen. It stole appetite by imitation, tricked the belly into silence, as if hunger could be smothered like a flame under a lid.

“O me, O me, my lungs is weak, my lungs is bad!” she continued, half-coughing, half-whispering. “It’s nearly ready for ye, deary. Ah, poor me, poor me, my poor hand shakes like to drop off! I see ye coming-to, and I ses to my poor self, ‘I’ll have another ready for him, and he’ll bear in mind the market price of opium, and pay according.’ O my poor head! I makes my pipes of old penny ink-bottles, ye see, deary—this is one—and I fits-in a mouthpiece, this way, and I takes my mixter out of this thimble with this little horn spoon; and so I fills, deary. Ah, my poor nerves! I got Heavens-hard drunk for sixteen year afore I took to this; but this don’t hurt me, not to speak of. And it takes away the hunger as well as wittles, deary.”

She held the nearly-emptied pipe towards him, and as she did the little coal at its heart winked, lighting the hollows of her face with a subterranean glow, like a lantern shown up through a skull. Then, as if her words had wound her down, she sank back and rolled upon her face.

The man rose unsteadily, his joints protesting like hinges that have not been oiled. He set the pipe down upon the hearth-stone as one might set down a dirty utensil one cannot bear to wash, drew the ragged curtain aside, and looked with a fastidious repugnance at the three bodies strewn across the bed.

He noticed, with the startled precision of a mind that can still take measurements, that the woman had smoked herself into a strange likeness of the Chinaman. The form of cheek and eye and temple, and even the colour that should have belonged to different blood and different weather, were repeated in her. It was as though the drug had translated her into another face—but poorly, inaccurately, leaving the original meaning behind. No language could carry what she had been into what she had become. The room itself seemed full of such mistranslations: words half-heard through thin walls; foreign tongues turned to noise; names worn away into nicknames until no one knew what anything was called.

The Chinaman wrestled convulsively with one of his many Gods or Devils, perhaps, and snarled horribly. The sound did not fit the place—too fierce for the little room—yet it was muffled by the low ceiling and filthy hangings, made small and ugly as a caged animal’s rage. The Lascar laughed and dribbled at the mouth, a soft wet chuckle that made the skin shrink. The hostess was still.

“What visions can she have?” the waking man mused, and turned the woman’s face towards him. He stood looking down at it as if it were evidence of some crime he could not name. “Visions of many butchers’ shops, and public-houses, and much credit? Of an increase of hideous customers, and this horrible bedstead set upright again, and this horrible court swept clean? What can she rise to, under any quantity of opium, higher than that!—Eh?”

He bent his ear to her mouth to catch her mutterings. Her lips moved; the sounds were shapes without meaning, as if her tongue had forgotten its own dialect and could only mouth the dominant babble of the street—unowned, unrooted, unintelligible.

“Unintelligible!” he said under his breath.

As he watched the spasmodic shoots and darts that broke out of her face and limbs—fitful lightning in a dark sky—some contagion seized on him. It was not pity; it was a wretched sympathy of nerves, a twitching echo. He had to withdraw to a lean arm-chair by the hearth, placed there, perhaps, for such emergencies, and grip the arms of it hard, holding tight until the unclean spirit of imitation passed.

When he had got the better of it, he came back sharply, pounced upon the Chinaman, and seizing him with both hands by the throat turned him violently on the bed. The Chinaman clutched at the hands, resisted, gasped, protested in a rapid stream of sounds that might have carried sense in another room, another world.

“What do you say?” the man demanded, pausing watchfully.

The answer poured out—jargon to his ear, syllables without foothold.

“Unintelligible!” he concluded again, with a listening frown, slowly loosening his grasp as if the effort were not in the hands but in the mind.

He turned then to the Lascar and fairly dragged him down upon the floor. As he fell, the Lascar started up half-risen, eyes glaring, arms lashing, and drew a phantom knife. For an instant the room’s soundscape changed: not the soft bubbling of the pipe, not the rat-scratch beneath the boards, but a sudden sharpness—an imagined blade cutting air. Then it became apparent that the woman had taken possession of the real knife for safety’s sake; for she too started up, restraining and expostulating with him in her harsh whisper, and the knife was visible in her dress, not in his, when they drowsily dropped back side by side. Their chattering and clattering—different accents, different cadences, the drowned remnants of far coasts—filled the room and came to nothing. When any distinct word flew out it had no sense or sequence, as if the room itself ate meaning.

Wherefore “unintelligible!” was again the watcher’s comment, with some reassured nodding of his head and a gloomy smile, as though he had verified that no confession could be had here, no coherent clue, nothing to carry forward into daylight.

He laid certain silver money on the table—payment rendered with the same exactness as he had listened—found his hat, groped down broken stairs, and gave a good morning to a rat-ridden doorkeeper who lay in bed in a black hutch beneath the steps, breathing the close air like an animal penned. Then he passed out.

That same afternoon the massive grey square tower of an old Cathedral rose before the eyes of a jaded traveller, not as dream-work now but as masonry and age and authority set upon the earth. The town around it hummed with its ordinary commerce—carts, boots on cobbles, voices advertising wares—yet the Tower held a different register, a deep note in the day’s hearing. Above all the smaller noises, bells were going for daily vesper service: measured bronze speech rolling through streets and over roofs, calling the scattered mind to a single place. The sound did not clang like cymbals; it did not rattle like a whisper in a foul room; it held its own air cleanly and asked the listener to match it.

He must needs attend, one would say, from his haste to reach the open Cathedral door. He went in among the choristers as they were getting on their sullied white robes in a hurry, their hands busy with ties and buttons as if this too were a kind of preparation of food—a daily ritual done whether the heart is in it or not. He took his own robe, put it on with practiced speed, and fell into the procession filing in to service.

Then the Sacristan locked the iron-barred gates that divided the sanctuary from the chancel; and when all the procession had scuttled into place, they hid their faces; and then the intoned words—

“WHEN THE WICKED MAN—”

—rose among groins of arches and beams of roof, waking a muttered thunder that was not the street’s noise at all but the Cathedral’s long memory speaking in a tongue older than any single man’s.

When the last chord of service had been coaxed into silence beneath the old square tower, the Cathedral began to empty with the orderly unhurriedness of creatures long trained to routine. The choir boys scuffled away, their soles rasping the stone like brushes on a drum-skin; the canons and dignitaries drifted apart with the same measured decorum as rooks making for their roost. And as with those clerical birds, two figures detached themselves from the flock and doubled back, as if some private current of meaning ran counter to the general flight.

The Close received them with its own voice: the damp flags held shallow pools that trembled when the wind passed; the great elms shed leaves in sighing showers, their wet boughs ticking softly as beads. Not only the day waned, but the year. A low sun, fierce in colour yet cold to the skin, burned behind the broken monastery wall; half the Virginia creeper’s deep red had let go and lay like spilt spice on the paving-stones. The rain of the afternoon still hung in the air, giving the stone that particular smell—old lime and moss and soot—that rises when water wakes the pores of ancient masonry.

Some timid leaves, hustled by a gust, tried to slip inside the low arched door of the Cathedral, seeking warmth like small creatures. Two men came out and, without thinking, brushed them back with their feet, casting them to the wet again. One of the two turned a broad key with a competent clink; the other vanished with a folio music-book hugged to his breast.

“Mr. Jasper was that, Tope?” asked the Dean.

“Yes, Mr. Dean.”

“He has stayed late.”

“Yes, Mr. Dean. I have stayed for him, your Reverence. He has been took a little poorly.”

“Say ‘taken,’ Tope—to the Dean,” murmured the younger clergyman at the Dean’s elbow; and he said it with the neatness of a man who hears language as other men hear a sour note.

Mr. Tope—Chief Verger and showman, accustomed to be lofty with excursion parties—refused with silence to notice that any correction had been offered.

“And when and how has Mr. Jasper been taken—taken—” repeated the Dean, tasting the corrected word as if it were a better seasoning; “when and how has Mr. Jasper been Taken—”

“Taken, sir,” Tope murmured, deferential.

“—poorly, Tope?”

“Why, sir, Mr. Jasper was that breathed—”

“I wouldn’t say ‘that breathed,’ Tope,” interposed Mr. Crisparkle, with the same gentle touch of trimness. “Not English—to the Dean.”

“Breathed to that extent,” the Dean suggested, not sorry to be thus served as standard.

“Mr. Jasper’s breathing was so remarkably short,” Tope said, working round the obstacle as a practised guide will lead you past the worn corner of a tomb, “when he came in, that it distressed him mightily to get his notes out: which was perhaps the cause of his having a kind of fit on him after a little. His memory grew DAZED.”

He launched DAZED with a pointed look at Mr. Crisparkle, as if daring him to better it.

“And a dimness and giddiness crept over him as strange as ever I saw: though he didn’t seem to mind it particularly, himself. However, a little time and a little water brought him out of his DAZE.”

He repeated it—DAZE—with the satisfied air of a man who has managed a word as he might manage a reluctant visitor through a narrow doorway.

“And Mr. Jasper has gone home quite himself, has he?” asked the Dean.

“Your Reverence, he has gone home quite himself. And I’m glad to see he’s having his fire kindled up, for it’s chilly after the wet, and the Cathedral had both a damp feel and a damp touch this afternoon, and he was very shivery.”

All three looked towards the old stone gatehouse that crossed the Close like a relic of fortification turned domestic. Through its latticed window a fire shone out on the fast-darkening scene, throwing ivy and creeper into heavy shadow. The great Cathedral bell struck the hour; and at the sound a ripple of wind went through those hanging leaves as if the air itself were a skin, stirred by the solemn vibration. The Close heard it and held it; the stone made an echo of it; and the sound seemed to go on humming through tomb and tower, broken niche and defaced statue, like a remembered tune no one could quite translate into words.

“Is Mr. Jasper’s nephew with him?” asked the Dean.

“No, sir,” replied the Verger. “But expected. There’s his own solitary shadow betwixt his two windows—the one looking this way, and the one looking down into the High Street—drawing his own curtains now.”

“Well, well,” said the Dean, briskly breaking up the little conference as his dinner-bell called him by stronger claims than any bell in the tower. “I hope Mr. Jasper’s heart may not be too much set upon his nephew. Our affections, however laudable, in this transitory world, should never master us; we should guide them, guide them. I find I am not disagreeably reminded of my dinner, by hearing my dinner-bell. Perhaps, Mr. Crisparkle, you will, before going home, look in on Jasper?”

“Certainly, Mr. Dean. And tell him that you had the kindness to desire to know how he was?”

“Ay; do so, do so. Certainly. Wished to know how he was. By all means. Wished to know how he was.”

With a pleasant air of patronage, the Dean—his quaint hat as nearly cocked as a Dean in good spirits may—walked off towards the ruddy dining-room of the snug red-brick house where he was “in residence” with Mrs. Dean and Miss Dean.

Mr. Septimus Crisparkle, Minor Canon—fair, rosy, early-rising, musical and classical; cheerful, kind, good-natured, boy-like; one who flung himself head-foremost into every deep running water in the surrounding country as gladly as if it were a duty—went his own way towards the gatehouse, thinking of his early tea, and carrying in his ears the last clean cadence of the service like a well-baked crust that still crackles when you break it.

Jasper’s door admitted him to a room that seemed always half in eclipse. The air was warmed, yes, by the fire that had just been coaxed to brightness; yet the corners held their shadow as if they preferred it. Even in full sun, the light seldom touched the grand piano in its recess, or the folio music-books on the stand, or the shelves of books; it seldom reached the unfinished portrait over the chimneypiece: a blooming schoolgirl with flowing brown hair tied with a blue riband, her beauty remarkable for an almost babyish saucy discontent, comically conscious of itself. The picture had no artistic merit—mere daub—but it had been made humorously, almost revengefully, like the original.

Jasper, dark, thick-haired, well-arranged in whisker and manner, stood by the fire as if he were listening to it. He was six-and-twenty, yet looked older, as dark men often do. His voice, when he spoke, was deep and good; his manner, though courteous, carried a habitual shade, like a room built to keep the sun out.

“Sorry to hear from Tope that you have not been well, Jasper,” said Crisparkle.

“O, it was nothing, nothing!”

“You look a little worn.”

“Do I? O, I don’t think so. What is better, I don’t feel so. Tope has made too much of it, I suspect. It’s his trade to make the most of everything appertaining to the Cathedral, you know.”

“I may tell the Dean—I call expressly from the Dean—that you are all right again?”

“Certainly; with my respects and thanks to the Dean,” Jasper replied, with a slight smile that sat on his face as carefully as a note held in tune.

“I’m glad to hear that you expect young Drood.”

“I expect the dear fellow every moment.”

“Ah! He will do you more good than a doctor, Jasper.”

“More good than a dozen doctors. For I love him dearly, and I don’t love doctors, or doctors’ stuff.”

Crisparkle, whose geniality often overflowed into song as naturally as a kettle sings when it comes to the boil, took his leave. “We shall miss you, Jasper, at the ‘Alternate Musical Wednesdays’ to-night; but no doubt you are best at home. Good-night. God bless you! ‘Tell me, shep-herds, te-e-ell me; tell me-e-e, have you seen (have you seen, have you seen, have you seen) my-y-y Flo-o-ora-a pass this way!’”

With that melodious delivery—half music, half affectionate nonsense—he withdrew his amiable face from the doorway and went down-stairs. There came sounds of recognition at the stair-foot, the brief quick exchange of greeting that makes a house, for an instant, ring like a bowl struck with a spoon.

Jasper listened. Something tightened in him—an ear pricked, a nerve touched. He started from his chair, and in the next moment had a young fellow in his arms.

“My dear Edwin!”

“My dear Jack! So glad to see you!”

“Get off your greatcoat, bright boy, and sit down here in your own corner. Your feet are not wet? Pull your boots off. Do pull your boots off.”

“My dear Jack, I am as dry as a bone. Don’t moddley-coddley, there’s a good fellow. I like anything better than being moddley-coddleyed.”

Checked, Jasper stood still and watched him strip away coat, hat, gloves with brisk youthfulness, as if shedding weather were no more than tossing aside a napkin. And once for all there was on Jasper’s face, whenever it turned this way, a look not merely fond but hungry: exacting, watchful, devoted, concentrated—never divided.

“Now I am right, and now I’ll take my corner, Jack. Any dinner, Jack?”

Jasper opened a door at the upper end of the room and disclosed a small inner chamber pleasantly lighted and ready, where a comely dame was setting dishes on table with that efficient clatter which is the domestic counterpoint to cathedral music. The room breathed of hot gravy and browned edges, of pepper and honest fat, of plate warmed by steam. Outside, the wind worried the creeper; inside, the hearth and kitchen did their older work of making a shelter in sound as well as warmth—the soft chink of spoon against dish, the sizzle and sigh when something is set down, the comfortable mutter of a household that has fed men before and means to feed them again.

“What a jolly old Jack it is!” cried Edwin, clapping his hands. “Look here, Jack; tell me; whose birthday is it?”

“Not yours, I know,” Jasper answered, pausing to consider.

“Not mine, you know? No; not mine, I know! Pussy’s!”

As Edwin spoke the name, Jasper’s fixed look, still fixed on the boy, had in it a strange power of suddenly taking in the sketch over the chimneypiece too, as if the name had rung a bell that made the painted face answer.

“Pussy’s, Jack! We must drink Many happy returns to her. Come, uncle; take your dutiful and sharp-set nephew in to dinner.”

As the boy—little more than a boy—laid a hand on Jasper’s shoulder, Jasper gaily laid a hand on his; and so, arm to shoulder, they went in with that comradely step which is half march, half dance.

“And, Lord! here’s Mrs. Tope!” cried Edwin. “Lovelier than ever!”

“Never you mind me, Master Edwin,” retorted the Verger’s wife; “I can take care of myself.”

“You can’t. You’re much too handsome. Give me a kiss because it’s Pussy’s birthday.”

“I’d Pussy you, young man, if I was Pussy, as you call her,” Mrs. Tope retorted, blushing after being saluted. “Your uncle’s too much wrapt up in you, that’s where it is. He makes so much of you, that it’s my opinion you think you’ve only to call your Pussys by the dozen, to make ’em come.”

“You forget, Mrs. Tope,” Jasper interposed, taking his place with a genial smile that did not reach the depth of his gaze, “and so do you, Ned, that Uncle and Nephew are words prohibited here by common consent and express agreement. For what we are going to receive His holy name be praised!”

“Done like the Dean! Witness, Edwin Drood! Please to carve, Jack, for I can’t.”

The dinner went forward with the practical solemnity of appetite. The knives made their small bright music; the plates warmed the palms; the scents rose—roast and onion, pepper and browned crust—till even the damp of the afternoon seemed driven from the bones. Little was said while mouths were busy, as if speech would only spoil the honest work; and the house, content in its routine, made those low domestic sounds that mend the mind: a chair leg shifted, a dish nudged, the fire settling, the kettle’s faint hiss far away.

At length the cloth was drawn, and walnuts set down, and a decanter of rich-coloured sherry that held the light like dark amber.

“I say! Tell me, Jack,” Edwin began, the talk now flowing as naturally as wine. “Do you really and truly feel as if the mention of our relationship divided us at all? I don’t.”

“Uncles as a rule, Ned, are so much older than their nephews,” Jasper returned, “that I have that feeling instinctively.”

“As a rule! Ah, may-be! But what is a difference in age of half-a-dozen years or so? And some uncles, in large families, are even younger than their nephews. By George, I wish it was the case with us!”

“Why?”

“Because if it was, I’d take the lead with you, Jack, and be as wise as Begone, dull Care! that turned a young man gray, and Begone, dull Care! that turned an old man to clay.—Halloa, Jack! Don’t drink.”

“Why not?”

“Asks why not, on Pussy’s birthday, and no Happy returns proposed! Pussy, Jack, and many of ’em! Happy returns, I mean.”

Jasper took the toast in silence, laying an affectionate, laughing touch on Edwin’s extended hand as if steadying not merely fingers but giddy spirits too.

“Hip, hip, hip, and nine times nine, and one to finish with, and all that, understood. Hooray, hooray, hooray!—And now, Jack, let’s have a little talk about Pussy. Two pairs of nut-crackers? Pass me one, and take the other.”

The first crack rang out, sharp as a snapped twig.

“How’s Pussy getting on, Jack?”

“With her music? Fairly.”

“What a dreadfully conscientious fellow you are, Jack! But I know, Lord bless you! Inattentive, isn’t she?”

“She can learn anything, if she will.”

“If she will! Egad, that’s it. But if she won’t?”

Crack—slow, on Jasper’s part.

“How’s she looking, Jack?”

Jasper’s concentrated face again took in the portrait as he answered, “Very like your sketch indeed.”

“I am a little proud of it,” Edwin said, glancing up with complacency, then shutting one eye and sighting it over the nut-crackers held level like a surveying instrument. “Not badly hit off from memory. But I ought to have caught that expression pretty well, for I have seen it often enough.”

Crack—on Edwin’s part. Crack—on Jasper’s.

“In point of fact,” Edwin went on after dipping among walnut fragments with a piqued air, “I see it whenever I go to see Pussy. If I don’t find it on her face, I leave it there.—You know I do, Miss Scornful Pert. Booh!”

He twirled the nut-crackers at the painted girl as if teasing her into life.

Crack, crack, crack—fast now on Edwin’s side. Slowly, one crack on Jasper’s. Silence followed, and in it the fire made a low inward roar like an animal sleeping.

“Have you lost your tongue, Jack?”

“Have you found yours, Ned?”

“No, but really;—isn’t it, you know, after all—”

Jasper lifted his dark eyebrows.

“Isn’t it unsatisfactory to be cut off from choice in such a matter? There, Jack! I tell you! If I could choose, I would choose Pussy from all the pretty girls in the world.”

“But you have not got to choose.”

“That’s what I complain of. My dead and gone father and Pussy’s dead and gone father must needs marry us together by anticipation. Why the—Devil, I was going to say, if it had been respectful to their memory—couldn’t they leave us alone?”

“Tet, tet, dear boy,” Jasper remonstrated gently.

“Tet, tet? Yes, Jack, it’s all very well for you. Your life is not laid down to scale, and lined and dotted out for you, like a surveyor’s plan. You have no uncomfortable suspicion that you are forced upon anybody, nor has anybody an uncomfortable suspicion that she is forced upon you, or that you are forced upon her. You can choose for yourself. Life, for you, is a plum with the natural bloom on; it hasn’t been over-carefully wiped off for you—”

“Don’t stop, dear fellow. Go on.”

“Can I anyhow have hurt your feelings, Jack?”

“How can you have hurt my feelings?”

“Good Heaven, Jack, you look frightfully ill! There’s a strange film come over your eyes.”

Jasper, with a forced smile, put out his right hand as if to quiet Edwin and gain time at once. For a little while he seemed to fight for breath, and the sound in the room changed: the fire’s murmur grew loud; the wind outside rose and fell along the gatehouse ivy like a distant surf; even the Cathedral bell’s last vibration seemed to have left an ache in the air.

At length Jasper said faintly, “I have been taking opium for a pain—an agony—that sometimes overcomes me. The effects of the medicine steal over me like a blight or a cloud, and pass. You see them in the act of passing; they will be gone directly. Look away from me. They will go all the sooner.”

Edwin, scared, obeyed and stared down at the ashes on the hearth, as if staring could keep the moment from becoming a memory. Jasper did not look away; he stared into the fire with a fierce steadiness, gripping the elbow-chair until his hand seemed welded to it. Thick drops stood on his forehead. His breath caught sharply—once, twice—then he subsided, and in an instant was as he had been, as if the cloud had blown through and left no mark but damp.

Edwin rose and tended him with a careful, almost fussy devotion—bringing water, adjusting, waiting for colour and ease to return.

When Jasper was fully restored, he laid a tender hand upon Edwin’s shoulder, and, strangely, his tone carried a faint raillery, as if to make light of what had shown too much.

“There is said to be a hidden skeleton in every house; but you thought there was none in mine, dear Ned.”

“Upon my life, Jack, I did think so. However, when I come to consider that even in Pussy’s house—if she had one—and in mine—if I had one—”

“You were going to say,” Jasper put in, “(but that I interrupted you in spite of myself) what a quiet life mine is. No whirl and uproar around me, no distracting commerce or calculation, no risk, no change of place; myself devoted to the art I pursue, my business my pleasure.”

“I really was going to say something of the kind, Jack; but you see, you, speaking of yourself, almost necessarily leave out much that I should have put in. For instance: I should have put in the foreground your being so much respected as Lay Precentor, or Lay Clerk, or whatever you call it, of this Cathedral; your enjoying the reputation of having done such wonders with the choir; your choosing your society, and holding such an independent position in this queer old place; your gift of teaching (why, even Pussy, who don’t like being taught, says there never was such a Master as you are!), and your connexion.”

“Yes; I saw what you were tending to. I hate it.”

“Hate it, Jack?” Edwin asked, bewildered.

“I hate it. The cramped monotony of my existence grinds me away by the grain. How does our service sound to you?”

“Beautiful! Quite celestial!”

“It often sounds to me quite devilish. I am so weary of it. The echoes of my own voice among the arches seem to mock me with my daily drudging round. No wretched monk who droned his life away in that gloomy place, before me, can have been more tired of it than I am. He could take for relief (and did take) to carving demons out of the stalls and seats and desks. What shall I do? Must I take to carving them out of my heart?”

Edwin leaned forward and laid a sympathetic hand on Jasper’s knee, searching his face. “I thought you had so exactly found your niche in life, Jack.”

“I know you thought so. They all think so.”

“Well, I suppose they do,” Edwin said slowly. “Pussy thinks so.”

“When did she tell you that?”

“The last time I was here. You remember when. Three months ago.”

“How did she phrase it?”

“O, she only said that she had become your pupil, and that you were made for your vocation.”

Edwin glanced at the portrait. Jasper saw the glance as surely as if it had been a spoken word.

“Anyhow, my dear Ned,” Jasper resumed, shaking his head with grave cheerfulness as if putting a lid on something that wanted to boil over, “I must subdue myself to my vocation: which is much the same thing outwardly. It’s too late to find another now. This is a confidence between us.”

“It shall be sacredly preserved, Jack.”

“I have reposed it in you, because—”

“I feel it, I assure you. Because we are fast friends, and because you love and trust me, as I love and trust you. Both hands, Jack.”

They stood, hand in hand, looking into one another’s eyes. Jasper held Edwin’s hands as if he would keep him steady not merely on his feet but in his future.

“You know now, don’t you,” Jasper said, “that even a poor monotonous chorister and grinder of music—in his niche—may be troubled with some stray sort of ambition, aspiration, restlessness, dissatisfaction, what shall we call it?”

“Yes, dear Jack.”

“And you will remember?”

“My dear Jack, I only ask you, am I likely to forget what you have said with so much feeling?”

“Take it as a warning, then.”

Edwin paused with his hands just released, considering what application a warning could have to him. Then he spoke, touched in earnest.

“I am afraid I am but a shallow, surface kind of fellow, Jack, and that my headpiece is none of the best. But I needn’t say I am young; and perhaps I shall not grow worse as I grow older. At all events, I hope I have something impressible within me, which feels—deeply feels—the disinterestedness of your painfully laying your inner self bare, as a warning to me.”

Jasper became so still that he seemed for a moment not to breathe.

“I couldn’t fail to notice, Jack,” Edwin went on, “that it cost you a great effort, and that you were very much moved, and very unlike your usual self. Of course I knew that you were extremely fond of me, but I really was not prepared for your, as I may say, sacrificing yourself to me in that way.”

Then Jasper, without any visible transition, breathed again, lifted his shoulders, laughed, and waved his right arm as if brushing away sentiment like smoke.

“No; don’t put the sentiment away, Jack; please don’t; for I am very much in earnest. I have no doubt that that unhealthy state of mind which you have so powerfully described is attended with some real suffering, and is hard to bear. But let me reassure you, Jack, as to the chances of its overcoming me. I don’t think I am in the way of it. In some few months less than another year, you know, I shall carry Pussy off from school as Mrs. Edwin Drood. I shall then go engineering into the East, and Pussy with me. And although we have our little tiffs now, arising out of a certain unavoidable flatness that attends our love-making, owing to its end being all settled beforehand, still I have no doubt of our getting on capitally then, when it’s done and can’t be helped. In short, Jack—who knows old songs better than you?—my wife shall dance, and I will sing, so merrily pass the day. Of Pussy’s being beautiful there cannot be a doubt;—and when you are good besides, Little Miss Impudence,” he added, apostrophising the portrait again, “I’ll burn your comic likeness, and paint your music-master another.”

Jasper, with his hand to his chin and an expression of musing benevolence, watched every animated look and gesture—attentively, steadily—as if the boy’s very breath and phrasing were a music he meant to remember note for note. He stayed so, held by a fascination of interest, and then said quietly, smiling:

“You won’t be warned, then?”

“No, Jack.”

“You can’t be warned, then?”

“No, Jack; not by you. Besides that I don’t really consider myself in danger, I don’t like your putting yourself in that position.”

“Shall we go and walk in the churchyard?”

“By all means. You won’t mind my slipping out of it for half a moment to the Nuns’ House, and leaving a parcel there? Only gloves for Pussy; as many pairs of gloves as she is years old to-day. Rather poetical, Jack?”

Still with his chin on his hand, Jasper murmured, “‘Nothing half so sweet in life,’ Ned!”

“Here’s the parcel in my greatcoat-pocket. They must be presented to-night, or the poetry is gone. It’s against regulations for me to call at night, but not to leave a packet. I am ready, Jack!”

Jasper let his attitude dissolve as if it had been a posture of listening. Together they went out into the Close again, where the wet stone held the day’s last cold, the elms whispered, and the Cathedral, vast and breathing in its own echoes, kept its ancient soundscape around them like a language that outlives its speakers.

For reasons that would come to matter, though not yet, the old Cathedral town must wear a name that is not upon any map. Let it be Cloisterham.

Names, in such a place, shed like skins. One tongue calls it this; another, that; and each century lays its label down over the last as a cook lays a fresh bay-leaf on yesterday’s broth—fragrant for a moment, then swallowed by the deeper, older taste. The town itself remains: ancient, earthen, steeped in crypt-damp and the slow chemistry of time. You smell it before you quite see it, an odour like cold stone rinsed by perpetual shade, mingled with the mossy sweetness of graveyard grass and the faint soot of a bell that has been hoarse for hundreds of years.

It is not a place for those who crave the loud world. Cloisterham keeps its soundscape close and narrow: the hush between footfalls; the thin clap of a shop-blind that hardly dares to flap; the sudden echo of a single voice turning a corner and coming back altered, as if the town itself is repeating you in an older dialect. Overhead, rooks circle the Cathedral tower—black commas in the sky—and their cawing is answered, far below, by other rooks and other rasping cries from the carved stalls, less distinct, as though spoken through cloth.

Here the past is not a story, but a soil. Children plant small salad in dust that was abbots and abbesses; they pat dirt into pies in which nuns and friars are kneaded without knowing. In fields beyond, the plough goes on with the blunt persistence of appetite, turning up what once bore titles—Lord Treasurers, Bishops, Archbishops—and grinding them back into the bread of common days. A man may eat without thinking what old authority has become his flour.

The town’s single thoroughfare is little more than a narrow line to enter by and escape by; all other ways are disappointing yards with pumps that clank in a tired key and lead nowhere. There is, it is true, the Cathedral Close, and a paved Quaker settlement tucked into a shaded corner, demure in colour and confirmation like a Quakeress’s bonnet. But even these seem more like preserved relics than living streets. Cloisterham’s pawnbroker has ceased to take pledges; he sits among unredeemed objects that sweat their age—dim watches in slow perspiration, sugar-tongs with ineffectual legs, odd dismal volumes that smell of dust and mould. One might say the liveliest signs of growth are in gardens: beans climbing their strings; cabbages thickening; herbs giving back, in crushed leaf, a sharp green answer to the Cathedral’s stony breath. Even the drooping little theatre, poor as it is, keeps a strip of earth where scarlet-beans or oyster-shells receive the foul fiend when he ducks from stage-hell into the real ground.

In the midst of Cloisterham stands the Nuns’ House: a venerable brick building whose name clings like a legend you cannot quite scrape off. Its low ceilings and jutting gables seem made to make a woman bow her head—whether in prayer, or in habit, or merely to save her brow from oak beams. Whatever lives there now, the walls remember what once murmured there; and if there are ghosts, they have the patience of those who have listened to the same bell note for centuries.

On the trim gate enclosing its old courtyard a bright brass plate flashes, too modern for the brick behind it, as if a new, staring eye had been stuck into an old, battered face:

“Seminary for Young Ladies. Miss Twinkleton.”

Miss Twinkleton’s accounts—half-yearly, meticulous, regular—contain no line for beads told at long windows, no extra for mortification, no fee for being walled up alive in some odd angle for having too much of busy Nature in you. Such questions would never appear in the poetical department at so much a quarter, and no recitation list would include a profitable piece on unprofitable suffering.

Miss Twinkleton herself, however, carries within her a curious division, like a kitchen with two doors leading to two different households. By day she is the seminary: composed, proper, a person of angles and schedules, dispensing education with a face that suggests she might at any moment be compelled—by duty, naturally—to set a terrestrial globe spinning or point to a celestial one, and range the earth and sky in search of knowledge for her pupils.

By night, the moment the young ladies have retired, she seems to unpin herself. Her curls smarten; her eyes brighten; she becomes a sprightlier Miss Twinkleton than any pupil ever sees. Then, in that other consciousness, she resumes without a stumble the tender scandal of Cloisterham which by day she would seem never to have heard; and she returns, too, to a certain season at Tunbridge Wells—airily “The Wells”—and to a certain finished gentleman, compassionately “Foolish Mr. Porters,” who in that season offered homage of the heart of which the seminary Miss Twinkleton is as ignorant as a granite pillar.

In both states her companion is Mrs. Tisher: a deferential widow with a weak back, a chronic sigh, and a voice trained to be present without being loud. She attends to young ladies’ wardrobes with a rustle like subdued ceremony, letting it be understood that she has seen better days. Perhaps for that reason the servants hold, as an article of faith handed down from one generation of kitchen to the next, that the departed Mr. Tisher (when he departed) must have been a hairdresser.

The pet pupil of the Nuns’ House is Miss Rosa Bud—Rosebud to everybody—wonderfully pretty, wonderfully childish, wonderfully whimsical. An awkward interest hangs about her among the other girls, awkward because romantic: it is known that by will and bequest a husband has been chosen for her, and that her guardian is bound to bestow her upon that husband when he comes of age. Miss Twinkleton, by day, combats the romance by shaking her head behind Rosebud’s dimpled shoulders and brooding aloud upon the unhappy lot of the doomed little victim. Yet the result, whether from the undermining memory of foolish Mr. Porters or from the girls’ instinct for hypocrisy, is only the unanimous bedchamber cry: “O, what a pretending old thing Miss Twinkleton is, my dear!”

The Nuns’ House is never in such a flutter as when the allotted husband calls. It is understood by the young ladies, unanimously, that he is lawfully entitled to appear; and that if Miss Twinkleton disputed it, she would be instantly taken up and transported. When his ring at the gate-bell is expected—or when it actually happens—every girl who can contrive, under any pretence, to look out of window, looks out; every girl who is “practising” practises out of time; the French class becomes so demoralised that the mark goes round briskly, like a bottle at a last-century supper.

On the afternoon of the day after a certain dinner of two at the gatehouse, the bell is rung, and the usual small earthquake runs through the establishment. The parlour-maid in chief announces, with the gravity of a court proclamation:

“Mr. Edwin Drood to see Miss Rosa.”

Miss Twinkleton, wearing exemplary melancholy like a shawl, turns to the sacrifice. “You may go down, my dear.”

Miss Bud goes down, followed by all eyes—eyes that can scent romance as quickly as a kitchen can scent a pie beginning to scorch. Mr. Edwin Drood waits in Miss Twinkleton’s own parlour, a dainty room whose only directly scholastic furniture is a terrestrial and a celestial globe, those expressive machines implying to parents and guardians that even in privacy Miss Twinkleton may, at any moment, be compelled by Duty to traverse continents and constellations on behalf of her pupils.

The newest maid—who has never yet seen the young gentleman to whom Miss Rosa is engaged—takes her first taste of him between the hinges of the open door left open for the purpose, and then tumbles guiltily down the kitchen stairs.

Into the parlour glides a charming little apparition, her face concealed by a little silk apron thrown over her head as if she were playing at being invisible.

“O! it is so ridiculous!” she cries, stopping and shrinking. “Don’t, Eddy!”

“Don’t what, Rosa?”

“Don’t come any nearer, please. It is so absurd.”

“What is absurd, Rosa?”

“The whole thing is. It is so absurd to be an engaged orphan, and it is so absurd to have the girls and the servants scuttling about after one like mice in the wainscot; and it is so absurd to be called upon!” The muffled voice has a childish thickness in it, as if a thumb were tucked into the corner of her mouth while she complains.

“You give me an affectionate reception, Pussy, I must say.”

“Well, I will in a minute, Eddy, but I can’t just yet. How are you?” She asks it very shortly, as if “how are you” were a mere button to press.

“I am unable to reply that I am much the better for seeing you, Pussy, inasmuch as I see nothing of you.”

A dark, bright, pouting eye peeps out from the apron’s edge and vanishes again, as she exclaims, “O good gracious! you have had half your hair cut off!”

“I should have done better to have had my head cut off, I think,” says Edwin, rumpling what remains with a fierce glance at the looking-glass, and stamping as if the boards had offended him.

“Shall I go?”

“No; you needn’t go just yet, Eddy. The girls would all be asking questions why you went.”

“Once for all, Rosa, will you uncover that ridiculous little head of yours and give me a welcome?”

She pulls off the apron. Her face is all dimples and wilfulness, with colour in it like something you might bite—like a candied cherry. “You’re very welcome, Eddy. There! I’m sure that’s nice. Shake hands. No, I can’t kiss you, because I’ve got an acidulated drop in my mouth.”

“Are you at all glad to see me, Pussy?”

“O yes, I’m dreadfully glad.—Go and sit down.—Miss Twinkleton.”

This is the signal for Propriety to pay its tithe. It is Miss Twinkleton’s custom, during these visits, to appear every three minutes either in her own person or in that of Mrs. Tisher, and lay a little offering on the shrine by pretending to seek a necessary article. On the present occasion Miss Twinkleton glides in and out and says, in passing, “How do you do, Mr. Drood? Very glad indeed to have the pleasure. Pray excuse me. Tweezers. Thank you!”—and is gone again, leaving behind a faint scent of lavender-water and supervision.

“I got the gloves last evening, Eddy, and I like them very much. They are beauties.”

“Well, that’s something,” he returns, half grumbling, as if praise were rationed. “The smallest encouragement thankfully received. And how did you pass your birthday, Pussy?”

“Delightfully! Everybody gave me a present. And we had a feast. And we had a ball at night.”

“A feast and a ball, eh? These occasions seem to go off tolerably well without me, Pussy.”

“De-lightfully!” Rosa cries, with such spontaneous relief that it pricks him.

“Hah! And what was the feast?”

“Tarts, oranges, jellies, and shrimps.”

The list comes out like the opening of a pantry: the bright peel-perfume of oranges, the trembling sweetness of jellies catching the light, the briny little shrimps with their seaside tang—foods that linger on the tongue and in the mind, much as Cloisterham’s old words linger in old mouths. Rosa’s breath, when she speaks, carries the sharp sugar-and-lemon of the acidulated drop.

“Any partners at the ball?”

“We danced with one another, of course, sir. But some of the girls made game to be their brothers. It was so droll!”

“Did anybody make game to be—”

“To be you? O dear yes!” Rosa laughs, enjoying it as if she were tasting it again. “That was the first thing done.”

“I hope she did it pretty well,” Edwin says, doubtfully, as if he suspects himself to be easy to caricature.

“O, it was excellent!—I wouldn’t dance with you, you know.”

He looks as if he cannot find the point, and asks why he may not take the liberty to inquire.

“Because I was so tired of you,” Rosa returns, and then, seeing displeasure gather in him, adds quickly and pleadingly, “Dear Eddy, you were just as tired of me, you know.”

“Did I say so, Rosa?”

“Say so! Do you ever say so? No, you only showed it. O, she did it so well!” she cries, in sudden ecstasy over the counterfeit.

“It strikes me that she must be a devilish impudent girl,” Edwin says.

“And so, Pussy, you have passed your last birthday in this old house.”

“Ah, yes!” Rosa clasps her hands, looks down with a sigh, and shakes her head.

“You seem to be sorry, Rosa.”

“I am sorry for the poor old place. Somehow, I feel as if it would miss me, when I am gone so far away, so young.”

“Perhaps we had better stop short, Rosa?”

She looks up swiftly, brightness like a spark, then drops her eyes again and shakes her head with another sigh.

“That is to say, is it, Pussy, that we are both resigned?”

She nods; then, after a short silence, bursts out quaintly: “You know we must be married, and married from here, Eddy, or the poor girls will be so dreadfully disappointed!”

For a moment there is more compassion on his face—compassion for her, and for himself—than there is love. He checks it and asks, “Shall I take you out for a walk, Rosa dear?”

Rosa dear seems uncertain, her mind making a comical little circuit; then her face brightens. “O yes, Eddy; let us go for a walk! And I tell you what we’ll do. You shall pretend that you are engaged to somebody else, and I’ll pretend that I am not engaged to anybody, and then we shan’t quarrel.”

“Do you think that will prevent our falling out, Rosa?”

“I know it will. Hush! Pretend to look out of window—Mrs. Tisher!”

By a fortuitous concourse of accidents, Mrs. Tisher appears, heaving into sight and rustling through the room like the ghost of a dowager condemned to silken skirts. “I hope I see Mr. Drood well; though I needn’t ask, if I may judge from his complexion. I trust I disturb no one; but there was a paper-knife—O, thank you, I am sure!” She disappears with her prize and a sigh that seems part of her clothing.

“One other thing you must do, Eddy, to oblige me,” says Rosebud. “The moment we get into the street, you must put me outside, and keep close to the house yourself—squeeze and graze yourself against it.”

“By all means, Rosa, if you wish it. Might I ask why?”

“O! because I don’t want the girls to see you.”

“It’s a fine day; but would you like me to carry an umbrella up?”

“Don’t be foolish, sir. You haven’t got polished leather boots on,” she pouts, with one shoulder raised like a small wing.

“Perhaps that might escape the notice of the girls, even if they did see me,” Edwin remarks, looking down at his boots with a sudden distaste for them.

“Nothing escapes their notice, sir. And then I know what would happen. Some of them would begin reflecting on me by saying (for they are free) that they never will on any account engage themselves to lovers without polished leather boots. Hark! Miss Twinkleton. I’ll ask for leave.”

Miss Twinkleton is indeed heard without, advancing in a blandly conversational tone as if speaking to the air: “Eh? Indeed! Are you quite sure you saw my mother-of-pearl button-holder on the work-table in my room?” Rosa is at once granted walking leave, and soon the young couple are out of the Nuns’ House, taking all precautions against discovery of the vitally defective boots of Mr. Edwin Drood—precautions which, for the peace of Mrs. Edwin Drood that is to be, let us hope are effective.

“Which way shall we take, Rosa?”

“I want to go to the Lumps-of-Delight shop.”

“To the—?”

“A Turkish sweetmeat, sir. My gracious me, don’t you understand anything? Call yourself an Engineer, and not know that?”

“Why, how should I know it, Rosa?”

“Because I am very fond of them. But O! I forgot what we are to pretend. No, you needn’t know anything about them; never mind.”

So he is borne off, gloomily enough, to the shop. There, amid jars and trays, Rosa buys her Lumps-of-Delight—sticky, dusted, perfumed with spice and rosewater, clinging to the paper like memories clinging to an old word. The sugar-dust rises when the parcel is opened, and the air takes on that faint foreign sweetness, as if a far country has been ground fine and sifted over Cloisterham’s grey day.

She offers him some. He declines rather indignantly, as though sweetness were an insult.

She begins to eat with great zest. First she takes off and rolls up her little pink gloves, like rose-leaves folded for keeping. Now and again she touches her little pink fingers to her rosy lips to clean them of the Dust of Delight. The sound of it—soft tackiness, a tiny pull and release—goes oddly against the town’s habitual hush, like a private, domestic noise refusing to be ashamed.

“Now, be a good-tempered Eddy, and pretend. And so you are engaged?”

“And so I am engaged.”

“Is she nice?”

“Charming.”

“Tall?”

“Immensely tall!” He says it with emphasis, and Rosa—short as a bird—tilts her head.

“Must be gawky, I should think,” Rosa observes quietly, enjoying her sweetmeat.

“I beg your pardon; not at all,” he contradicts, rising. “What is termed a fine woman; a splendid woman.”

“Big nose, no doubt,” says Rosa, tranquilly.

“Not a little one, certainly,” he replies quickly—her own being a little one.

“Long pale nose, with a red knob in the middle. I know the sort of nose,” says Rosa, nodding with the satisfied air of a food-historian identifying a spice by smell alone.

“You don’t know the sort of nose, Rosa,” he returns with some warmth, “because it’s nothing of the kind.”

“Not a pale nose, Eddy?”

“No.” He will not yield.

“A red nose? O! I don’t like red noses. However; to be sure she can always powder it.”

“She would scorn to powder it,” Edwin says, heating.

“Would she? What a stupid thing she must be! Is she stupid in everything?”

“No; in nothing.”

After a pause—in which Rosa’s whimsically wicked face has watched him with the same patient attention she gives the slow dissolving of sugar—she says, “And this most sensible of creatures likes the idea of being carried off to Egypt; does she, Eddy?”

“Yes. She takes a sensible interest in triumphs of engineering skill: especially when they are to change the whole condition of an undeveloped country.”

“Lor!” Rosa laughs, and shrugs her shoulders. “Do you object,” Edwin inquires, with a majestic downward look upon her fairy figure, “do you object, Rosa, to her feeling that interest?”

“Object? my dear Eddy! But really, doesn’t she hate boilers and things?”

“I can answer for her not being so idiotic as to hate Boilers,” he returns with angry emphasis; “though I cannot answer for her views about Things; really not understanding what Things are meant.”

“But don’t she hate Arabs, and Turks, and Fellahs, and people?”

“Certainly not.” Very firmly.

“At least she must hate the Pyramids? Come, Eddy?”

“Why should she be such a little—tall, I mean—goose, as to hate the Pyramids, Rosa?”

“Ah! you should hear Miss Twinkleton,” Rosa says, nodding and savouring her Lumps, “bore about them, and then you wouldn’t ask. Tiresome old burying-grounds! Isises, and Ibises, and Cheopses, and Pharaohses; who cares about them? And then there was Belzoni, or somebody, dragged out by the legs, half-choked with bats and dust. All the girls say: Serve him right, and hope it hurt him, and wish he had been quite choked.”

They wander, side by side but no longer arm-in-arm, about the old Close. Fallen leaves, damp and faintly mould-sweet, lie in drifts; each of them sometimes stops and presses a deeper footprint into the soft decay, as if to make a point the other must feel. Above them, the Cathedral’s great body holds its breath of stone; the town’s usual quiet is broken only by their little frictions and the distant rook-voices writing black sound against the sky.

“Well!” Edwin says at last, after a lengthy silence. “According to custom. We can’t get on, Rosa.”

Rosa tosses her head. She doesn’t want to get on.

“That’s a pretty sentiment, Rosa, considering.”

“Considering what?”

“If I say what, you’ll go wrong again.”

“You’ll go wrong, you mean, Eddy. Don’t be ungenerous.”

“Ungenerous! I like that!”

“Then I don’t like that, and so I tell you plainly,” Rosa pouts.

“Now, Rosa, I put it to you. Who disparaged my profession, my destination—”

“You are not going to be buried in the Pyramids, I hope?” she interrupts, arching her delicate eyebrows. “You never said you were. If you are, why haven’t you mentioned it to me? I can’t find out your plans by instinct.”

“Now, Rosa, you know very well what I mean, my dear.”

“Well then, why did you begin with your detestable red-nosed giantesses? And she would, she would, she would, she would, she WOULD powder it!” Rosa cries in a burst of comical contradictory spleen.

“Somehow or other, I never can come right in these discussions,” Edwin sighs, becoming resigned.

“How is it possible, sir, that you ever can come right when you’re always wrong? And as to Belzoni, I suppose he’s dead;—I’m sure I hope he is—and how can his legs or his chokes concern you?”

“It is nearly time for your return, Rosa. We have not had a very happy walk, have we?”

“A happy walk? A detestably unhappy walk, sir. If I go up-stairs the moment I get in and cry till I can’t take my dancing lesson, you are responsible, mind!”

“Let us be friends, Rosa.”

“Ah!” Rosa cries, shaking her head, and now the tears come real, not theatrical. “I wish we could be friends! It’s because we can’t be friends, that we try one another so. I am a young little thing, Eddy, to have an old heartache; but I really, really have, sometimes. Don’t be angry. I know you have one yourself too often. We should both of us have done better, if What is to be had been left What might have been. I am quite a little serious thing now, and not teasing you. Let each of us forbear, this one time, on our own account, and on the other’s!”

He is disarmed by this sudden glimpse of woman’s nature in the spoilt child—though for an instant disposed to resent it, as if it implied that he is an enforced infliction upon her. He watches her cry and sob, both hands pressing a handkerchief to her eyes; then, as she composes herself and in her young inconstancy begins to laugh at herself for being so moved, he leads her to a seat under the elm-trees close by.

“One clear word of understanding, Pussy dear,” he begins awkwardly. “I am not clever out of my own line—now I come to think of it, I don’t know that I am particularly clever in it—but I want to do right. There is not—there may be—I really don’t see my way to what I want to say, but I must say it before we part—there is not any other young—”

“O no, Eddy! It’s generous of you to ask me; but no, no, no!”

They have drawn very near the Cathedral windows. At that moment the organ and choir sound out sublimely. The music rolls through the air like warm bread scent through a cold house—filling every hollow, making the very stones seem to resonate. It is the town’s purest sound, gathered and released with patient power; and as they sit listening, Edwin Drood’s mind lifts the confidence of last night and sets it beside this great concord, thinking how unlike it is to discordance.

“I fancy I can distinguish Jack’s voice,” he remarks, low, as if not to scratch the sound.

“Take me back at once, please,” Rosa urges, quickly laying her light hand upon his wrist. “They will all be coming out directly; let us get away. O, what a resounding chord! But don’t let us stop to listen to it; let us get away!”

Her hurry is over as soon as they have passed out of the Close. The acoustics change; the Cathedral’s swell falls behind like a tide retreating; the High-street receives them with its narrow hush and its watchful windows. Now they go arm-in-arm, gravely and deliberately enough, along the old way back to the Nuns’ House.

At the gate, the street within sight is empty. Edwin bends down his face to Rosebud’s. She remonstrates, laughing, and is a childish schoolgirl again.

“Eddy, no! I’m too sticky to be kissed. But give me your hand, and I’ll blow a kiss into that.”

He does so. She breathes a light breath into his palm and keeps hold of it, peering into it as if it were a small, private relic.

“Now say, what do you see?”

“See, Rosa?”

“Why, I thought you Egyptian boys could look into a hand and see all sorts of phantoms. Can’t you see a happy Future?”

For certain, neither of them sees a happy Present, as the gate opens and closes, and one goes in, and the other goes away—leaving Cloisterham’s old air to settle again, and its old sounds to resume their rule.

If Cloisterham ever bred a man who could auction off his own reflection and buy it back at a profit, it was Mr. Thomas Sapsea.

He stood for the town’s notion of weight and consequence: broad in the middle, slow in the mind, sonorous in the throat. He “dressed at” the Dean, and was so often mistaken—bowed to, spoken to, half-saluted—for some higher ecclesiastical article, that the mistake had hardened into a private proof of greatness. He had even trained his sale-voice to a mild intonation, as if landed property were to be disposed of with a blessing; and when he finished a public auction, he would spread his hands with a grave, flowing motion—as though the next act must be Confirmation, and the next sound an Amen.

His premises lay in the High Street opposite the Nuns’ House, a building of much the same vintage: old bones irregularly patched, like a household that has survived plagues by gradually admitting air and light. Above Sapsea’s doorway hung a wooden effigy of his father—curled wig, toga, and a little hammer poised—caught in the eternal act of selling. The town admired the chastity of the idea, and the delicacy of the carved little finger, as if that finger had always known precisely where value resided.

Inside, Sapsea sat in his ground-floor room that looked first upon a paved back-yard and then, beyond rails, upon a strip of garden. An early fire burned—a luxury he allowed himself without apology, for it was a cool, raw autumn evening, and the draughts that prowled old Cloisterham stone could chill a man into needless humility. The room smelt of hot coal, dusty paper, and the sweet, raisiny breath of port uncorked and waiting. His portrait watched him; an eight-day clock ticked at him; a weather-glass stood ready to contradict the sky. They were his chosen witnesses: he against mankind, his clock against time, his glass against weather.

On the table by the fire lay a writing-desk and materials, and beside them a tray holding three clean wineglasses. Sapsea glanced at a scrap of manuscript, lifted his chin, and read it in his head with such dignity that only one word escaped him—audible, rounded, and self-satisfied:

“Ethelinda.”

He began to pace with his thumbs in his waistcoat arm-holes, repeating the lines from memory, giving each syllable the weight of a deed of conveyance.

His maid came in.

“Mr. Jasper is come, sir.”

Sapsea, without haste, waved the air as if granting an audience. “Admit him.” He drew two wineglasses from the tray with the air of a man recognizing claims already established by law.

John Jasper entered—Choirmaster and musician, his face composed, his eyes bright with that fixed attention which can look like sympathy until it turns. The warmth of the fire and the red of the port made a small, domestic theatre; outside, the town’s evening sounds pressed faintly at the panes: a cart-rumble dying in the High Street, a distant shout from boys who never learned quiet, and, above all, the Cathedral’s presence—felt even when it held its voice.

Sapsea welcomed him with swelling courtesy.

“Glad to see you, sir. I congratulate myself on having the honour of receiving you here for the first time.”

“You are very good. The honour is mine and the self-congratulation is mine,” Jasper returned smoothly.

“You are pleased to say so, sir. But I do assure you it is a satisfaction to me to receive you in my humble home. And that is what I would not say to everybody.” His tone left hanging, like an unstruck bell, the rest of the meaning: that Jasper might count himself fortunate to be thought satisfiable.

“I have for some time desired to know you, Mr. Sapsea.”

“And I, sir, have long known you by reputation as a man of taste.” Sapsea filled Jasper’s glass, then his own. He raised it as if it were a national banner. “I will give you, sir: When the French come over, may we meet them at Dover!”

It was an old toast, the sort that had survived into the present like a preserved fruit gone leathery with age; but Sapsea drank it as if it had been invented that morning for his lips. Jasper watched him with a slight smile.

“You can scarcely be ignorant, Mr. Sapsea,” Jasper said, letting the firelight play over his face, “that you know the world.”

“Well, sir,” chuckled Sapsea, stretching his legs before the fire, “I think I know something of it; something of it.”

“Your reputation for that knowledge has always interested and surprised me, and made me wish to know you. For Cloisterham is a little place. Cooped up in it myself, I know nothing beyond it, and feel it to be a very little place.”

Sapsea took this as tribute. “If I have not gone to foreign countries, young man—” He checked himself, as if politeness were a rare spice requiring careful measurement. “You will excuse me calling you young man, Mr. Jasper? You are much my junior.”

“By all means.”

“If I have not gone to foreign countries, young man, foreign countries have come to me. They have come to me in the way of business, and I have improved upon my opportunities. Put it that I take an inventory, or make a catalogue. I see a French clock. I never saw him before, in my life, but I instantly lay my finger on him and say ‘Paris!’ I see some cups and saucers of Chinese make, equally strangers to me personally: I put my finger on them, then and there, and I say ‘Pekin, Nankin, and Canton.’ It is the same with Japan, with Egypt, and with bamboo and sandalwood from the East Indies; I put my finger on them all. I have put my finger on the North Pole before now, and said ‘Spear of Esquimaux make, for half a pint of pale sherry!’”

Jasper’s smile widened a fraction, as if the soundscape of the world—wind at sea-edges, market-calls in foreign tongues, the hush of snow—had all been reduced here to labels and prices, and he found the reduction amusing.

“Really? A very remarkable way, Mr. Sapsea, of acquiring a knowledge of men and things.”

“I mention it, sir,” Sapsea returned, brimming with complacency, “because, as I say, it don’t do to boast of what you are; but show how you came to be it, and then you prove it.”

Jasper let the subject fall where it lay, like a heavy key on a table. “Most interesting. We were to speak of the late Mrs. Sapsea.”

“We were, sir.”

Sapsea filled both glasses again and took the decanter back as if securing a treasure. He lifted a folded scrap of paper, the manuscript Jasper had come for, but held it in suspense—like a cook describing a dish at length before permitting the first taste.

“Before I consult your opinion as a man of taste on this little trifle,” he said, holding the paper up to the firelight, “which is but a trifle, and still has required some thought, sir, some little fever of the brow, I ought perhaps to describe the character of the late Mrs. Sapsea, now dead three quarters of a year.”

Jasper, caught mid-yawn behind his wineglass, lowered it quickly and summoned an attentive look—though his watering eyes betrayed the effort.

“Half a dozen years ago, or so,” Sapsea went on, “when I had enlarged my mind up to—I will not say to what it now is, for that might seem to aim at too much—but up to the pitch of wanting another mind to be absorbed in it—I cast my eye about me for a nuptial partner. Because, as I say, it is not good for man to be alone.”

Jasper’s brows lifted slightly, as if storing the phrase for later use in another voice, another setting.

“Miss Brobity at that time kept—I will not call it the rival establishment to the establishment at the Nuns’ House opposite, but I will call it the other parallel establishment down town. The world did have it that she showed a passion for attending my sales, when they took place on half holidays, or in vacation time. The world did put it about, that she admired my style. The world did notice that as time flowed by, my style became traceable in the dictation-exercises of Miss Brobity’s pupils.”

His deep voice rolled like a drum in a small room, pushing aside the softer domestic sounds—the tick of the clock, the fire’s shift and crack, the faint far-off wheel on stone.

“Young man, a whisper even sprang up in obscure malignity, that one ignorant and besotted Churl (a parent) so committed himself as to object to it by name. But I do not believe this. For is it likely that any human creature in his right senses would so lay himself open to be pointed at, by what I call the finger of scorn?”

Jasper shook his head gravely. Not in the least likely.

Sapsea, luxuriating in his own narrative, seemed about to refill Jasper’s glass though it was already full; he did refill his own, which was empty.

“Miss Brobity’s Being, young man, was deeply imbued with homage to Mind. She revered Mind, when launched, or, as I say, precipitated, on an extensive knowledge of the world. When I made my proposal, she did me the honour to be so overshadowed with a species of Awe, as to be able to articulate only the two words, ‘O Thou!’ meaning myself. Her limpid blue eyes were fixed upon me, her semi-transparent hands were clasped together, pallor overspread her aquiline features, and, though encouraged to proceed, she never did proceed a word further. I disposed of the parallel establishment by private contract, and we became as nearly one as could be expected under the circumstances. But she never could, and she never did, find a phrase satisfactory to her perhaps-too-favourable estimate of my intellect. To the very last (feeble action of liver), she addressed me in the same unfinished terms.”

As Sapsea’s voice deepened further, Jasper’s eyes drifted shut, as if the cadence itself were soporific. Then, abruptly, he opened them and answered the deep voice with a deep sound of his own.

“Ah!” he said—almost as if he had checked himself from adding, men!

“I have been since,” Sapsea continued, stretching out before the fire as if warming not only his body but his self-importance, “what you behold me; I have been since a solitary mourner; I have been since, as I say, wasting my evening conversation on the desert air. I will not say that I have reproached myself; but there have been times when I have asked myself the question: What if her husband had been nearer on a level with her? If she had not had to look up quite so high, what might the stimulating action have been upon the liver?”

Jasper’s face assumed a fallen, almost doleful cast. “I suppose it was to be.”

“We can only suppose so, sir,” Sapsea agreed. “As I say, Man proposes, Heaven disposes. It may or may not be putting the same thought in another form; but that is the way I put it.”

Jasper murmured assent, his fingers resting lightly on the warm bowl of his glass, the port’s scent rising—berries and old wood and a faint spirit-bite—mingled with coal smoke.

“And now, Mr. Jasper,” Sapsea resumed, producing the scrap of manuscript as if unveiling a monument in miniature, “Mrs. Sapsea’s monument having had full time to settle and dry, let me take your opinion, as a man of taste, on the inscription I have—(as I before remarked, not without some little fever of the brow)—drawn out for it. Take it in your own hand. The setting out of the lines requires to be followed with the eye, as well as the contents with the mind.”

Jasper took the paper and read it. The letters stood in a kind of carved arrogance even on the page:

ETHELINDA,  
Reverential Wife of  
MR. THOMAS SAPSEA,  
AUCTIONEER, VALUER, ESTATE AGENT, &c.,  
OF THIS CITY.

Whose Knowledge of the World,  
Though somewhat extensive,  
Never brought him acquainted with  
A SPIRIT  
More capable of  
LOOKING UP TO HIM.

STRANGER, PAUSE  
And ask thyself the Question,  
CANST THOU DO LIKEWISE?  
If Not,  
WITH A BLUSH RETIRE.

Sapsea had risen and planted himself with his back to the fire, watching Jasper’s face for admiration as keenly as he watched a bidder’s twitch at an auction. His stance set his face toward the door; and at that moment the maid appeared again.

“Durdles is come, sir!”

Sapsea promptly drew forth and filled the third wineglass, as being now claimed. “Show Durdles in.”

Jasper handed back the paper. “Admirable!”

“You approve, sir?”

“Impossible not to approve. Striking, characteristic, and complete.”

Sapsea inclined his head as if accepting a formal receipt. Then he invited Durdles—entering with a breath of chill stone and damp air clinging to his clothes—to take off the glass of wine, for it would warm him.

Durdles was a stonemason, and seemed made of the very colour of his trade—dusty as a gravestone from head to foot, as if the Cathedral’s powdered centuries had settled upon him and stayed. Everyone in Cloisterham knew him. He was the town’s chartered libertine: praised as a wonderful workman (though he seldom worked), and known without dispute as a wonderful sot. Of the Cathedral crypt he knew more than any living authority—perhaps more than any dead one, too. It was said he had first taken to it as a hiding-place where he might lock out the town boys and sleep away drink, having access through his contracts for rough repairs. However begun, the intimacy endured. In demolition of old wall, buttress, and pavement he had seen strange sights, and recounted them with that half-drunken precision which makes the incredible sound like a thing you could touch.

He often spoke of himself in the third person, either because his identity was always a little fogged, or because Cloisterham’s habit of labeling people had infected his own speech.

“Durdles come upon the old chap,” he would say of some buried magnate, “by striking right into the coffin with his pick. The old chap gave Durdles a look with his open eyes, as much as to say, ‘Is your name Durdles? Why, my man, I’ve been waiting for you a devil of a time!’ And then he turned to powder.”

A two-foot rule lived in his pocket, a mason’s hammer almost always in his hand. He was forever tapping and sounding about the Cathedral, listening for hollow places. Whenever he told Tope, “Here’s another old ’un in here!” Tope dutifully passed the announcement to the Dean as an established discovery, as though stone itself had spoken.

Tonight he wore coarse flannel with horn buttons, a yellow neckerchief with draggle ends, an old hat more russet than black, and boots the colour of quarried grit. He led a hazy gipsy life, carrying his dinner tied in a little bundle and eating it anywhere—often seated on tombstones, as if the dead were simply part of the furniture of his day. That dinner bundle had become one of Cloisterham’s familiar sights; on a few famous occasions it had been taken into custody with him and exhibited before the bench at the Town Hall when he was brought in drunk and incapable. But such occasions were rare, because Durdles was as seldom drunk as sober.

He lived alone in a cramped, unfinished little house—believed to be built, so far, of stones stolen from the city wall. The approach was ankle-deep in chips, like a petrified grove of sculptured tomb fragments—urns, draperies, broken columns—at every stage of becoming. In the yard two journeymen chipped incessantly while two others sawed stone facing one another, dipping in and out of their sentry-box shelters with such mechanical regularity that they might have been figures designed to symbolize Time and Death; their saw’s rasp carried on the air like a harsh, modern interruption to Cloisterham’s older music, as if industry had learned to grind its teeth in public.

Sapsea put the manuscript into Durdles’s hands as if conferring a relic.

Durdles unfeelingly drew out his two-foot rule, and measured the lines as calmly as if they were a paving-stone, dusting them with stone-grit from his fingers.

“This is for the monument, is it, Mr. Sapsea?”

“The Inscription. Yes.”

Sapsea waited for awe. Durdles gave arithmetic.

“It’ll come in to a eighth of a inch,” said Durdles, as though that were the only praise worth having.

He drained the port with the air of a man swallowing warmth rather than wine.

“Your servant, Mr. Jasper. Hope I see you well.”

“How are you, Durdles?” Jasper asked.

“I’ve got a touch of the Tombatism on me, Mr. Jasper, but that I must expect.”

“You mean the Rheumatism,” Sapsea corrected sharply, nettled that his grand composition had met the measuring-stick first.

“No, I don’t. I mean, Mr. Sapsea, the Tombatism. It’s another sort from Rheumatism. Mr. Jasper knows what Durdles means. You get among them Tombs afore it’s well light on a winter morning, and keep on, as the Catechism says, a-walking in the same all the days of your life, and you’ll know what Durdles means.”

“It is a bitter cold place,” Jasper assented, shivering—not with sympathy, but as if the thought of that underworld damp crept into him.

“And if it’s bitter cold for you, up in the chancel, with a lot of live breath smoking out about you,” Durdles returned, “what the bitterness is to Durdles, down in the crypt among the earthy damps there, and the dead breath of the old ’uns—Durdles leaves you to judge.—Is this to be put in hand at once, Mr. Sapsea?”

Sapsea, with an author’s impatience to see himself in print on stone, replied that it could not be out of hand too soon.

“Then you had better let me have the key,” said Durdles.

“Why, man, it is not to be put inside the monument!”

Durdles looked at him with dogged certainty, like a man who knows the habits of stone and the secrets of locks. “Durdles knows where it’s to be put, Mr. Sapsea; no man better. Ask ’ere a man in Cloisterham whether Durdles knows his work.”

Sapsea rose, opened a drawer, and took a key; then unlocked an iron safe let into the wall and drew forth another key—both handled with reverence, as if they were medals of his respectability.

“When Durdles puts a touch or a finish upon his work,” Durdles explained, “no matter where, inside or outside, Durdles likes to look at his work all round, and see that his work is a-doing him credit.”

The key was large. Before accepting it, Durdles slipped his two-foot rule into a side-pocket made for it, opened his flannel coat, and opened the mouth of a vast inner breast-pocket, as deliberately as a man stowing away a tool that might save his life. He took the key and placed it deep within.

“Why, Durdles!” Jasper said, amused, “you are undermined with pockets!”

“And I carries weight in ’em too, Mr. Jasper. Feel those!” He produced two other large keys with a clank of iron. “Hand me Mr. Sapsea’s likewise. Surely this is the heaviest of the three.”

“You’ll find ’em much of a muchness, I expect,” Durdles said. “They all belong to monuments. They all open Durdles’s work. Durdles keeps the keys of his work mostly. Not that they’re much used.”

Jasper took the three keys for a moment and let them weigh his palm. Their cold metal made a blunt music when they touched—an unlovely little chime, like a parody of church bells: not calling anyone to prayer, only to secrecy.

“By the bye,” Jasper said, as if idly, bending over the fire and making the keys click together, “I have been going to ask you, many a day, and have always forgotten. You know they sometimes call you Stony Durdles, don’t you?”

“Cloisterham knows me as Durdles, Mr. Jasper.”

“I am aware of that, of course. But the boys sometimes—”

“O! if you mind them young imps of boys—” Durdles broke in gruffly.

“I don’t mind them any more than you do. But there was a discussion the other day among the Choir, whether Stony stood for Tony.” He clinked one key against another.

“Take care of the wards, Mr. Jasper,” Durdles warned.

“Or whether Stony stood for Stephen.” Jasper changed keys, clinking again.

“You can’t make a pitch pipe of ’em, Mr. Jasper.”
