“Or whether the name comes from your trade. How stands the fact?”

Jasper lifted his head and handed the keys back with a face of open friendliness, as if the question were harmless as a sip of wine. But Durdles, gruff as his own stone, lived in a perpetual uncertainty of dignity and offence; his haze could harden in an instant into suspicion. He pocketed his two keys one by one and buttoned them in. He took up his dinner-bundle from the chair-back where he had hung it. He tied the third key into the bundle as if he were an ostrich and liked to dine upon cold iron. Then he went out without answering at all.

Sapsea, the least able man in Cloisterham to recognize a door slammed in his face, proposed a hit at backgammon. Jasper consented. The dice clicked, the pieces slid with small wooden sounds, and Sapsea’s voice poured on—diffuse, improving, self-regarding—until, later, supper was brought: cold roast beef and salad.

The beef had that firm, satisfied look of yesterday’s joint, cut in thick slices that showed their grain; the fat held a faint sheen in the firelight. The salad—crisp leaves dressed sharp—gave off a clean, green smell as it met vinegar, a brief bright note in the room’s heavier perfumes of coal, old wood, and port. Knife against plate made its homely scrape, and the clock’s ticking kept time like a small, stubborn drummer.

So the evening was beguiled, and the good autumn fire did its steady work. When at last Jasper rose to go, he hinted that he would return for more of Mr. Sapsea’s wisdom on future occasions. Sapsea, satisfied to have his portion admired and his inscription approved, allowed him to escape for the present—carrying away, in his own mind, the instalment he had received, and perhaps other weights besides, that chimed faintly when they touched.

John Jasper, going home through the Close, was pulled up short by a picture that seemed at once too common for wonder and too monstrous for indifference: Stony Durdles, dinner-bundle tucked under his arm as if it were part of his ribcage, leaned his back against the iron railing of the burial-ground; and in the clear moonlight a small ragged boy—sharp as a thorn and as ugly—stood off and flung stones at him as at a chalked target.

Some stones struck—thudding on coat or shoulder with a sound duller than it ought to have been, as if Durdles were half wall himself. Some missed—spatting on paving, ticking off iron, and skittering away into the Close’s hush where every little ricochet spoke too loudly. Durdles seemed equally unmoved by hit or miss. The boy, on the other hand, when he hit, put his mouth to a jagged gap where teeth were wanting and blew a whistle of triumph that split the night like a butcher’s blade on bone; and when he missed, he yelped, “Mulled agin!” and took a more vicious, more careful aim.

Jasper stepped out of shadow into moonlight, his own tread softened by the Close’s ancient stones and the great Cathedral’s patient listening.

“What are you doing to the man?” he demanded.

“Making a cock-shy of him,” returned the boy, with the readiness of a creature that had never been taught to fear words.

“Give me those stones in your hand.”

“Yes, I’ll give ’em you down your throat, if you come a-ketching hold of me,” the boy answered, shaking loose and backing, his boots a ruin of rags and broken leather that slapped the ground. “I’ll smash your eye, if you don’t look out!”

“Baby-Devil that you are,” said Jasper, with disgust more controlled than his anger, “what has the man done to you?”

“He won’t go home.”

“What is that to you?”

“He gives me a ’apenny to pelt him home if I ketches him out too late,” said the boy; and then, half stumbling, half dancing—like some wild refrain that had learned to live in his knees—he chanted:

“Widdy widdy wen!
I—ket—ches—Im—out—ar—ter—ten,
Widdy widdy wy!
Then—E—don’t—go—then—I—shy—
Widdy Widdy Wake-cock warning!”

With a comprehensive sweep on the last word, he let fly again at Durdles. The chant had the air of a customary signal—warning enough to tell Durdles, if he chose, to stand clear or to go.

Jasper beckoned the boy with his head to follow—feeling it useless to seize him or plead with him—and crossed to the iron railing where Durdles, stoned and stony, stood sunk in meditation among the tombs as among familiar tools.

“Do you know this thing—this child?” Jasper asked, at a loss for a word that would put a proper name to such a specimen.

“Deputy,” said Durdles, nodding.

“Is that its—his—name?”

“Deputy,” Durdles assented.

“I’m man-servant up at the Travellers’ Twopenny in Gas Works Garding,” the creature explained, as if that settled every question of birth and breeding. “All us man-servants at Travellers’ Lodgings is named Deputy. When we’re chock full and the Travellers is all a-bed I come out for my ’elth.”

Then he withdrew into the road, took aim, and began again:

“Widdy widdy wen!
I—ket—ches—Im—out—ar—ter—”

“Hold your hand!” cried Jasper. “Don’t throw while I stand so near him, or I’ll kill you. Come, Durdles; let me walk home with you to-night. Shall I carry your bundle?”

“Not on any account,” replied Durdles, adjusting it with jealous possession, as if what lay inside were as personal as breath.

“Durdles was making his reflections here when you come up, sir,” he went on, with the slow gravity of beery suddenness, “surrounded by his works, like a poplar Author.—Your own brother-in-law,” introducing with a motion of his head a sarcophagus within the railing, white and cold in the moonlight. “Mrs. Sapsea,” indicating the monument of that devoted wife. “Late Incumbent,” indicating the reverend gentleman’s broken column. “Departed Assessed Taxes,” indicating a vase and towel, standing on what might represent the cake of soap. “Former pastrycook and Muffin-maker, much respected,” indicating a gravestone. “All safe and sound here, sir, and all Durdles’s work. Of the common folk, that is merely bundled up in turf and brambles, the less said the better. A poor lot, soon forgot.”

The Close held those words strangely. It was a place made for echoes—organ-notes, children’s hymn-singing, the scrape of a verger’s shoe—and now it had to hold, too, the boy’s stone-clicks and wolfish yelps, and Durdles’s blunt verdict on memory. The Cathedral’s shadow lay over them like an old language that few could speak properly now, and fewer still could translate.

“This creature, Deputy, is behind us,” said Jasper, glancing back. “Is he to follow us?”

The relations between Durdles and Deputy were capricious. Durdles turned with the sluggish grandeur of a man who had drunk and worked among stone-dust all his life, and Deputy instantly made a wide circuit into the road and stood on the defensive, his head cocked for danger like a street-dog’s.

“You never cried Widdy Warning before you begun to-night,” said Durdles, as if unexpectedly reminded of an injury—or inventing one on the spot.

“Yer lie, I did,” said Deputy, in his single form of polite contradiction.

“Own brother, sir,” observed Durdles, turning again, and as suddenly forgetting his grievance as he had recalled or imagined it, “own brother to Peter the Wild Boy! But I gave him an object in life.”

“At which he takes aim?” Jasper suggested.

“That’s it, sir,” returned Durdles, satisfied. “At which he takes aim. I took him in hand and gave him an object. What was he before? A destroyer. What work did he do? Nothing but destruction. What did he earn by it? Short terms in Cloisterham jail. Not a person, not a piece of property, not a winder, not a horse, nor a dog, nor a cat, nor a bird, nor a fowl, nor a pig, but what he stoned, for want of an enlightened object. I put that enlightened object before him, and now he can turn his honest halfpenny by the three penn’orth a week.”

“I wonder he has no competitors.”

“He has plenty, Mr. Jasper, but he stones ’em all away. Now, I don’t know what this scheme of mine comes to,” pursued Durdles, with the same sodden consideration, “I don’t know what you may precisely call it. It ain’t a sort of a—scheme of a—National Education?”

“I should say not,” replied Jasper.

“I should say not,” assented Durdles. “Then we won’t try to give it a name.”

Still, behind them, Deputy kept his distance, and with it his noise—picking up stones and flinging them at every wall, post, pillar, and shuttered surface as they went by the deserted way. The quiet of the Close was not empty; it was peopled with small sounds that mattered—the nesting-stir of birds in ivy, the faint hush of wind in old carvings, the far-off tick of a clock—and Deputy’s clatter was like the intrusion of cheap, tinny commerce into a sacred concert, a kind of pollution that could not be unseen once heard.

“Is there anything new down in the crypt, Durdles?” Jasper asked.

“Anything old, I think you mean,” growled Durdles. “It ain’t a spot for novelty.”

“Any new discovery on your part, I meant.”

“There’s a old ’un under the seventh pillar on the left as you go down the broken steps of the little underground chapel as formerly was; I make him out (so fur as I’ve made him out yet) to be one of them old ’uns with a crook. To judge from the size of the passages in the walls, and of the steps and doors, by which they come and went, them crooks must have been a good deal in the way of the old ’uns! Two on ’em meeting promiscuous must have hitched one another by the mitre pretty often, I should say.”

Jasper offered no correction. He watched Durdles—powdered head to foot with mortar, lime, and stone-grit—as if the man’s daily intimacy with hidden masonry and buried names had begun to wear a romantic colour. The dead were Durdles’s pantry and ledger; he knew what lay where, and how close it lay, with a tradesman’s certainty that had nothing poetic in it, yet made poetry possible.

“Yours is a curious existence,” Jasper said.

Durdles replied, gruffly, “Yours is another,” and gave no clue whether he meant it as praise or rebuke.

“Well! inasmuch as my lot is cast in the same old earthy, chilly, never-changing place, yes,” said Jasper. “But there is much more mystery and interest in your connection with the Cathedral than in mine. Indeed, I am beginning to have some idea of asking you to take me on as a sort of student, or free ’prentice, under you, and to let me go about with you sometimes, and see some of these odd nooks in which you pass your days.”

“All right,” returned the Stony One, in general. “Everybody knows where to find Durdles, when he’s wanted.” Which, if not strictly true, came near enough, for Durdles was always in vagabondage somewhere within reach of stone and shadow.

“What I dwell upon most,” Jasper pursued, “is the remarkable accuracy with which you would seem to find out where people are buried.—What is the matter? That bundle is in your way; let me hold it.”

Durdles had stopped and backed a little. Deputy, attentive as a carrion-bird to any sign of action, instantly skirmished into the road. Durdles looked for some ledge or corner to place his bundle on; when Jasper relieved him of it, he said:

“Just you give me my hammer out of that, and I’ll show you.”

Clink, clink. The hammer changed hands, and Durdles’s grip on it had the comfort of habit—the way a cook’s hand finds the weight of a knife without looking, the way an elder finds the staff that makes the ground answer.

“Now, lookee here. You pitch your note, don’t you, Mr. Jasper?”

“Yes.”

“So I sound for mine. I take my hammer, and I tap.” He struck the pavement—tap—tap—tap—and the dull report ran along the stones and died against iron and wall. Deputy widened his range, as if supposing his own head might be called for in the demonstration.

“I tap, tap, tap. Solid! I go on tapping. Solid still! Tap again. Holloa! Hollow! Tap again, persevering. Solid in hollow! Tap, tap, tap, to try it better. Solid in hollow; and inside solid, hollow again! There you are! Old ’un crumbled away in stone coffin, in vault!”

“Astonishing!” said Jasper.

“I have even done this,” Durdles went on, drawing out his two-foot rule. Deputy crept nearer again, his hunger for mischief turning into a hunger for treasure; his imagination already licking its lips at the notion of discovery, accusation, and the “delicious treat” of seeing others pay for it.

“Say that hammer of mine’s a wall—my work. Two; four; and two is six,” measuring on the pavement. “Six foot inside that wall is Mrs. Sapsea.”

“Not really Mrs. Sapsea?”

“Say Mrs. Sapsea. Her wall’s thicker, but say Mrs. Sapsea. Durdles taps, that wall represented by that hammer, and says, after good sounding: ‘Something betwixt us!’ Sure enough, some rubbish has been left in that same six-foot space by Durdles’s men!”

Jasper opined, “Such accuracy is a gift.”

“I wouldn’t have it at a gift,” returned Durdles, ill-pleased. “I worked it out for myself. Durdles comes by his knowledge through grubbing deep for it, and having it up by the roots when it don’t want to come.—Holloa you Deputy!”

“Widdy!” shrilled Deputy, standing off again.

“Catch that ha’penny. And don’t let me see any more of you to-night, after we come to the Travellers’ Twopenny.”

“Warning!” returned Deputy, catching it; and that mystic word, tossed back into the night, seemed his assent to the bargain.

They crossed what had once been the monastery vineyard—ground that had fed men who once spoke their prayers in an older tongue, now paved over with later meanings—and came into the narrow back lane where stood the crazy wooden house of two low stories known as the Travellers’ Twopenny. It was warped and distorted, like the morals of the travellers; a lattice-porch lingered in scant remains above the door, and a rustic fence in scant remains before its stamped-out garden, because the travellers were so tenderly attached to the premises—or so fond of having a roadside fire by day—that nothing could persuade or threaten them into departure without their violently possessing themselves of some wooden forget-me-not and bearing it off.

The place attempted the semblance of an inn with fragments of conventional red curtaining in the windows. Those rags were made muddily transparent in the night by feeble lights of rush or cotton dip burning dully in the close air within, giving out that stale, greasy smell of cheap flame and damp boards—an odour that speaks of bellies never properly warmed, of broth stretched thin, of bread that has forgotten wheat. Over the door an inscribed paper lantern addressed them with the house’s purport, swaying a little, whispering paper against wire in the faintest draft like a tired insect.

As Durdles and Jasper drew near, half-a-dozen other hideous small boys—twopenny lodgers, followers, hangers-on, who could say—started into the moonlight as if Deputy’s presence had sent out a carrion-scent. Instantly they fell to stoning him and one another, the lane alive with yells and the hard bright chatter of pebbles.

“Stop, you young brutes!” cried Jasper angrily. “Let us go by!”

The remonstrance was received with yells and flying stones, in the comfortable custom of late years whereby Christians are stoned on all sides as if Saint Stephen’s days were revived. Durdles, with some point, remarked, “They haven’t got an object,” and led the way down the lane.

At the corner, Jasper, hotly enraged, checked his companion and looked back. For a moment all was silent. Then a stone came rattling at his hat; and far off a yell of “Wake-Cock! Warning!” rose, followed by a crow as from some infernally-hatched Chanticleer, proclaiming under whose victorious fire he stood. Jasper turned the corner into safety and took Durdles home, Durdles stumbling among the litter of his stony yard as if he were about to turn head-foremost into one of the unfinished tombs.

Jasper returned by another way to his gatehouse. Letting himself in softly with his key, he found his fire still burning—a small domestic red eye, watching. He went to a locked press and took from it a peculiar-looking pipe, which he filled—but not with tobacco—and with a little instrument adjusted the contents of the bowl very carefully, as if measuring and seasoning something that must be exact to the grain.

Then he ascended an inner staircase of only a few steps, leading to two rooms. There was a light in each. In one, his nephew lay asleep, calm and untroubled, as if the world’s noises could not reach him. Jasper stood looking down upon him for some time, the unlighted pipe in his hand, his attention fixed and deep, listening—perhaps—to the boy’s even breathing, perhaps to the Close’s distant hush, perhaps to something else that had no name in any living dialect.

At last, hushing his footsteps, he passed to his own room, lit his pipe, and delivered himself to the Spectres it invoked at midnight.

The Reverend Septimus Crisparkle—Septimus because six earlier little brothers, lighted like rushlights, had gone out one by one—had already made his morning covenant with Cloisterham: not in a study, but at the weir, where the day’s first skin of ice took a crisp crack under his cheerful plunge. The river answered him in its own tongue: a quick sucking hush round his shoulders, reeds whispering at the edges, and the far Cathedral bell laying a slow, solemn line over the water as if sound itself were a kind of blessing that could be heard and felt.

Back in Minor Canon Corner, with his hair still holding the scent of cold stones and running water, he stood before the pier-glass and boxed at his own reflection with the calm exactness of an artist. His gloves—great, padded, ridiculous-looking things—might have belonged to a prizefighter; his face belonged to a child who had never been able to keep a hard thought against anybody for long. He feinted, he slipped, he struck straight from the shoulder; the leather thudded softly, and each controlled exhalation was a little gust that made the room’s quiet audible: the tick of the clock, the faint creak of oaken beam, a rook’s croak outside as if even the birds kept their voices low out of respect for the Cathedral’s shadow.

Mrs. Crisparkle—his mother, not his wife—came in at that very moment, neat as a china shepherdess, bright-eyed, trim and compact, her ribbons as carefully chosen as a cook chooses herbs. Septimus stopped as if obeying a bell, took her entering face between the boxing-gloves, and kissed it.

“I say, every morning of my life, that you’ll do it at last, Sept,” she observed, watching with affectionate alarm.

“Do what, Ma dear?”

“Break the pier-glass, or burst a blood-vessel.”

“Neither, please God, Ma dear. Here’s wind, Ma. Look at this!”

He gave a final round that was severe in form but merry in spirit, and ended by getting the old lady’s cap into Chancery—so the professors of the Noble Art called that light entanglement—without stirring the smallest lavender or cherry riband. Then, with the quick humility of a man who likes to seem caught doing nothing remarkable, he slipped his gloves into a drawer and assumed a contemplative gaze out of window just as a servant entered.

Breakfast in Minor Canon Corner had its own ritual sounds and smells: the urn beginning to sing, a lid’s soft click, china whispering against cloth, a warm breath of tea rising with the faintly hay-like fragrance of Souchong, and beneath it the comfortable, persuasive odours of ham and toast and eggs. The Cathedral did not fall silent for domesticity; rather, domesticity and Cathedral made a duet—bells, rooks, rare footsteps echoing down the old stones, and now and then an organ-roll like distant weather. Cloisterham’s noises were not the clattering assault of a modern street, but an acoustic ecology that soothed by being measured: each sound knew its place, and kept it.

When the two were alone again, she stood to say the Lord’s Prayer aloud. Septimus stood with bent head to hear it, though he was within five years of forty—much as he had stood to hear the same words from the same lips when he was within five months of four. It was one of those circular moments that make a life feel stitched, not torn: the same cadence, the same voice, the same small room holding the same act as if time were a patient housekeeper.

They sat down opposite each other. On the table lay a letter she had just read and set upon the breakfast-cloth as if it were a dish not yet served.

“And what, Ma dear,” asked the Minor Canon, doing honest work upon his breakfast, “does the letter say?”

She passed it over.

Mrs. Crisparkle’s pride was that her bright eyes could read writing without spectacles; her son’s pride was to make her pride bloom. He had therefore invented the fiction that he could not read manuscript unless he put on a pair of grave and prodigious spectacles, which pinched his nose, clouded his breakfast, and made his eyes water—eyes which, unassisted, were as sharp as a microscope married to a telescope.

“It’s from Mr. Honeythunder, of course,” she said, folding her arms with a little preparatory stiffness, as one might brace for a strong draught.

“Of course,” returned Septimus, and began, stumbling in a way that belonged not to letters but to lenses. “‘Haven of Philanthropy, Chief Offices, London, Wednesday. Dear Madam. I write in the—;’ In the what’s this? What does he write in?”

“In the chair,” said the old lady, quick as a knife.

Septimus lifted off the spectacles to see her face, half protesting, half appealing. “Why, what should he write in?”

“Bless me, bless me, Sept, you don’t see the context! Give it back to me, my dear.”

Glad to be rid of the watery torture, he returned it, murmuring dutifully that his sight for reading got worse and worse daily.

She read, very perspicuously: “‘I write from the chair, to which I shall probably be confined for some hours.’”

Septimus glanced at the row of chairs against the wall with a look that suggested chairs could be prisons if one insisted upon it.

“‘We have,’” she continued, with a touch more emphasis, “‘a meeting of our Convened Chief Composite Committee of Central and District Philanthropists, at our Head Haven as above; and it is their unanimous pleasure that I take the chair.’”

Septimus breathed more freely. “O! if he comes to that, let him,” he muttered.

“‘Not to lose a day’s post, I take the opportunity of a long report being read, denouncing a public miscreant—’”

“It is a most extraordinary thing,” broke in the gentle Minor Canon, laying down knife and fork to rub his ear in vexation, “that these philanthropists are always denouncing somebody. And it is another most extraordinary thing that they are always so violently flush of miscreants!”

“‘Denouncing a public miscreant,’” his mother resumed, “‘to get our little affair of business off my mind. I have spoken with my two wards, Neville and Helena Landless, on the subject of their defective education, and they give in to the plan proposed; as I should have taken good care they did, whether they liked it or not.’”

“And it is another most extraordinary thing,” Septimus added in the same tone, “that these philanthropists are so given to seizing their fellow-creatures by the scruff of the neck, and—one may say—bumping them into the paths of peace. I beg your pardon, Ma dear, for interrupting.”

“‘Therefore, dear Madam, you will please prepare your son, the Rev. Mr. Septimus, to expect Neville as an inmate to be read with, on Monday next. On the same day Helena will accompany him to Cloisterham, to take up her quarters at the Nuns’ House, the establishment recommended by yourself and son jointly. Please likewise to prepare for her reception and tuition there. The terms in both cases are understood to be exactly as stated to me in writing by yourself, when I opened a correspondence with you on this subject, after the honour of being introduced to you at your sister’s house in town here. With compliments to the Rev. Mr. Septimus, I am, Dear Madam, Your affectionate brother (In Philanthropy), Luke Honeythunder.’”

Septimus rubbed his ear again, as if the very name made a noise in it. “Well, Ma, we must try it. There can be no doubt we have room for an inmate, and that I have time to bestow upon him, and inclination too. I must confess to feeling rather glad he is not Mr. Honeythunder himself. Though that seems wretchedly prejudiced—does it not?—for I never saw him. Is he a large man, Ma?”

“I should call him a large man, my dear,” she replied after hesitation, “but that his voice is so much larger.”

“Than himself?”

“Than anybody.”

“Hah!” said Septimus; and finished his breakfast as if the flavour of the Superior Family Souchong, and also of the ham and toast and eggs, had taken a little fright and withdrawn.

The slender history of how Mr. Honeythunder had come to press his wards upon Cloisterham was soon told in Minor Canon Corner. Mrs. Crisparkle’s sister—another Dresden piece, so neatly matched that the two ought never to have been parted—was the childless wife of a City clergyman. During the last annual rematching of those ornaments (during Mrs. Crisparkle’s last visit), Mr. Honeythunder, in his public capacity of Professor of Philanthropy, had made their acquaintance after a charitable occasion where devoted orphans of tender years had been glutted with plum buns and plump bumptiousness. From that sugared, noisy scene had come this quiet domestic consequence.

Septimus thought the matter over as the morning went on, the house breathing its old calm—ivy against brick, lattices keeping out glare, panelled rooms smelling faintly of beeswax and seasoned oak, and stone-walled gardens where annual fruit still ripened on monkish trees as if the trees remembered a language their keepers had forgotten.

“I am sure you will agree with me, Ma,” he said at last, “that the first thing to be done is to put these young people as much at their ease as possible. There is nothing disinterested in the notion, because we cannot be at our ease with them unless they are at their ease with us. Now, Jasper’s nephew is down here at present; and like takes to like, and youth takes to youth. He is a cordial young fellow, and we will have him to meet the brother and sister at dinner. That’s three. We can’t think of asking him, without asking Jasper. That’s four. Add Miss Twinkleton and the fairy bride that is to be, and that’s six. Add our two selves, and that’s eight. Would eight at a friendly dinner at all put you out, Ma?”

“Nine would, Sept,” returned the old lady, visibly nervous.

“My dear Ma, I particularise eight.”

“The exact size of the table and the room, my dear,” she said, as if numbers were not arithmetic but architecture.

So it was settled. When Septimus called with his mother on Miss Twinkleton to arrange for Miss Helena Landless’s reception at the Nuns’ House, the two other invitations having reference to that establishment were proffered and accepted. Miss Twinkleton did indeed cast a glance at her globes—as if regretting they were not formed to be taken out into society—but reconciled herself to leaving them behind. Messages were dispatched to the philanthropist, instructions given for the departure and arrival in good time for dinner; and, in Minor Canon Corner, stock for soup began to make the very air fragrant, as if the house itself were practicing hospitality in advance.

In those days there was no railway to Cloisterham, and Mr. Sapsea said there never would be. Mr. Sapsea said more: he said there never should be. (And yet, marvellous to consider, later times would bring express trains that did not think Cloisterham worth stopping at, but yelled and whirled through it on larger errands—leaving only dust, and a kind of insult, as testimony against its insignificance.) For now, Cloisterham’s traffic, already unsettled by some remote fragment of a Main Line to somewhere else, came sneaking in by a back stable-way for many years labelled at the corner, Beware of the Dog.

To that ignominious avenue Septimus repaired, awaiting the daily omnibus between Cloisterham and external mankind: a short, squat vehicle with a disproportionate heap of luggage on the roof, like a little Elephant with infinitely too much Castle.

As it lumbered up, he could hardly see anything else of it for a large outside passenger on the box-seat, elbows squared, hands on knees, compressing the driver into a most uncomfortably small compass. The man glowered about him with a strongly-marked face, and his very presence seemed to raise the volume of the street, as if he carried his own thunder and compelled the air to be louder.

“Is this Cloisterham?” demanded the passenger, in a tremendous voice.

“It is,” replied the driver, rubbing himself as if he ached, after throwing the reins to the ostler. “And I never was so glad to see it.”

“Tell your master to make his box-seat wider, then,” returned the passenger. “Your master is morally bound—and ought to be legally, under ruinous penalties—to provide for the comfort of his fellow-man.”

The driver began a superficial perquisition into his own skeleton with both palms, which did not improve his temper.

“Have I sat upon you?” asked the passenger.

“You have,” said the driver, as if he did not like it at all.

“Take that card, my friend.”

“I think I won’t deprive you on it,” returned the driver, casting his eyes over the card without taking it. “What’s the good of it to me?”

“Be a Member of that Society,” said the passenger.

“What shall I get by it?” asked the driver.

“Brotherhood,” returned the passenger, in the same ferocious key.

“Thankee,” said the driver, very deliberately, climbing down. “My mother was contented with myself, and so am I. I don’t want no brothers.”

“But you must have them,” replied the passenger, descending too. “Whether you like it or not. I am your brother.”

“I say!” expostulated the driver, growing chafed. “Not too fur! The worm will, when—”

Here Septimus interposed, aside, in a friendly voice that was itself a kind of soothing sound in the little street. “Joe, Joe, Joe! Don’t forget yourself, Joe, my good fellow!” And then, when Joe peaceably touched his hat, Septimus addressed the passenger with: “Mr. Honeythunder?”

“That is my name, sir.”

“My name is Crisparkle.”

“Reverend Mr. Septimus? Glad to see you, sir. Neville and Helena are inside. Having a little succumbed of late, under the pressure of my public labours, I thought I would take a mouthful of fresh air, and come down with them, and return at night. So you are the Reverend Mr. Septimus, are you?”

He surveyed Septimus with disappointment, and twisted a double eye-glass by its ribbon as if he were roasting it, but not otherwise using it.

“Hah! I expected to see you older, sir.”

“I hope you will,” replied Septimus, good-humouredly.

“Eh?” demanded Mr. Honeythunder.

“Only a poor little joke. Not worth repeating.”

“Joke? Ay; I never see a joke,” Mr. Honeythunder retorted, frowning. “A joke is wasted upon me, sir. Where are they? Helena and Neville, come here! Mr. Crisparkle has come down to meet you.”

They appeared: an unusually handsome, lithe young fellow, and an unusually handsome, lithe girl—much alike; both very dark and very rich in colour. She had almost the gipsy type; there was something untamed about them both, a hunter-and-huntress air, yet also the air of being the objects of the chase rather than the followers. Slender, supple, quick of eye and limb; half shy, half defiant; fierce of look; and on each, face and form alike, an indefinable pause that came and went—like the pause before a crouch or a bound.

Septimus invited Mr. Honeythunder to dinner with a troubled mind, for he could already feel, as one feels a draught before a door opens, the discomfiture it would bring upon his dear old china shepherdess at home. He gave his arm to Helena Landless. As they walked through the ancient streets, brother and sister took great delight in what he pointed out—Cathedral, monastery ruin, old stones speaking in their worn dialect of chisels and prayers—and they wondered much, as if they had been beautiful barbaric captives brought from some wild tropical dominion where the birds cried in another key and the herbs had other names.

Mr. Honeythunder walked in the middle of the road, shouldering the natives out of his way, and loudly developing a scheme for making a raid on all the unemployed persons in the United Kingdom, laying them every one by the heels in jail, and forcing them, on pain of prompt extermination, to become philanthropists. His voice rolled through Cloisterham’s mild soundscape like a cartload of iron bars; it bruised the quiet, and seemed to make even the rooks listen with offence.

Mrs. Crisparkle had need of her own share of philanthropy when she beheld this very large and very loud excrescence on the little party. Always something in the nature of a boil upon the face of society, Mr. Honeythunder expanded into an inflammatory wen in Minor Canon Corner.

Though it was not literally true—as public unbelievers facetiously charged—that he called aloud to his fellow-creatures, “Curse your souls and bodies, come here and be blessed!” still his philanthropy was of that gunpowderous sort whose difference from animosity was hard to determine. You were to abolish military force, but first bring all commanding officers who had done their duty to trial by court-martial for that offence, and shoot them. You were to abolish war, but make converts by making war upon them and charging them with loving war as the apple of their eye. You were to have no capital punishment, but first sweep off the face of the earth all legislators, jurists, and judges who differed. You were to have universal concord, and get it by eliminating all who wouldn’t—or conscientiously couldn’t—be concordant. You were to love your brother as yourself, but only after an indefinite interval of maligning him (very much as if you hated him) and calling him all manner of names.

Above all things, you were to do nothing in private, or on your own account. You were to go to the offices of the Haven of Philanthropy and put your name down as a Member and a Professing Philanthropist; then pay your subscription, receive your card, your riband and medal, and evermore live upon a platform—evermore say what Mr. Honeythunder said, what the Treasurer said, what the sub-Treasurer said, what the Committee said, what the sub-Committee said, what the Secretary said, what the Vice-Secretary said—until your own voice became merely the echo of a dominant lexicon. In that lexicon, everybody outside the Haven was defined by resolutions unanimously carried, under hand and seal, to the effect: “That this assembled Body of Professing Philanthropists views, with indignant scorn and contempt, not unmixed with utter detestation and loathing abhorrence—” in short, the baseness of all those who did not belong to it; and pledged itself to make as many obnoxious statements as possible about them, without being at all particular as to facts. It was language as cudgel, language as badge; and one could hear in it the slow erosion of whatever small, local, home-spoken tenderness people might otherwise have used—those unrecorded dialect-words that fit a hearth the way a well-worn spoon fits a hand.

Dinner, meant to be a gentle net cast to catch two shy young creatures and draw them into ease, became a most doleful breakdown. The soup, for all its honest fragrance—long-simmered bones giving up their comfort, onions melting to sweetness, herbs releasing their hidden green—could not repair the table’s ruined harmony. Mr. Honeythunder deranged the symmetry at once: he planted himself where waiting was impeded, blocked the thoroughfare, and drove Mr. Tope (assisting the parlour-maid) to the verge of distraction by passing plates and dishes on over his own head as if service were a moral obstacle course.

Nobody could talk to anybody, because Mr. Honeythunder held forth to everybody at once, as if the company had no individual existence but were a Meeting. He impounded Septimus as an official personage to be addressed—a human peg on which to hang his oratorical hat—and fell into the exasperating habit, common among such speakers, of impersonating him as a wicked and weak opponent. He would cry, “And will you, sir, now stultify yourself by telling me—” when the innocent Minor Canon had not opened his lips, nor meant to. Or he would thunder:

“Now see, sir, to what a position you are reduced. I will leave you no escape. After exhausting all the resources of fraud and falsehood, during years upon years; after exhibiting a combination of dastardly meanness with ensanguined daring, such as the world has not often witnessed; you have now the hypocrisy to bend the knee before the most degraded of mankind, and to sue and whine and howl for mercy!”

At which, Septimus would look partly indignant, partly perplexed—like a man suddenly accused of crimes in a language he had never learned—while his worthy mother sat bridling, with tears in her bright eyes. The remainder of the party lapsed into a sort of gelatinous state, in which there was no flavour or solidity and very little resistance. Even the room’s kind sounds—the clink of cutlery, the pour of wine—seemed to shrink, as if the house itself were trying to keep small under the booming oppression.

Yet when at last Mr. Honeythunder’s departure began to impend, the gush of philanthropy that burst forth must have been highly gratifying to his feelings. His coffee was produced by the special activity of Mr. Tope a full hour before he wanted it. Septimus sat with his watch in his hand for about the same period, lest the man should overstay his time. The four young people were unanimous in believing the Cathedral clock struck three-quarters when it struck but one. Miss Twinkleton estimated the distance to the omnibus at five-and-twenty minutes’ walk when it was really five. The affectionate kindness of the whole circle hustled Mr. Honeythunder into his greatcoat and shoved him out into the moonlight as if he were a fugitive traitor with whom they sympathised and a troop of horse were at the back door. Septimus and his new charge, escorting him to the omnibus, were so fervent in their apprehensions of his catching cold that they shut him up in it instantly and left him there with still half-an-hour to spare—Cloisterham’s quiet closing round the vehicle again the moment his tremendous voice was corked inside, like a kitchen settling back into its own proper murmurs after a pot has boiled over.

“I know very little of that gentleman, sir,” Neville Landless said, as he and the Minor Canon turned back from the riverward path and took the quieter way under the moon.

“You know very little of your guardian?” Mr. Crisparkle repeated, pausing as if the words themselves had clinked oddly, like a spoon against a cracked cup.

“Almost nothing.”

“How came he—”

“To be my guardian?” Neville’s voice tightened, then steadied, as though he had set his jaw on the admission. “I’ll tell you, sir. I suppose you know that we come—my sister and I—from Ceylon?”

“Indeed, no.”

Neville gave a short, surprised breath. “I wonder at that. We lived with a stepfather there. Our mother died there, when we were little children. We have had a wretched existence. She made him our guardian, and he was a miserly wretch who grudged us food to eat, and clothes to wear.” The words came out with the bluntness of a boy remembering hunger: not the grand hunger of romance, but the small domestic one—thin rice, watery curries served as if charity, the smell of cooking always promising more than it ever yielded. “At his death, he passed us over to this man; for no better reason that I know of, than his being a friend or connexion of his, whose name was always in print and catching his attention.”

“That was lately, I suppose?”

“Quite lately, sir.” Neville’s pace quickened for a few steps, his shadow sliding long and sharp across the pale stones. “This stepfather of ours was a cruel brute as well as a grinding one. It is well he died when he did, or I might have killed him.”

Mr. Crisparkle stopped short in the moonlight and looked at his hopeful pupil as if a sudden gun had gone off in the close air.

“I surprise you, sir?” Neville asked, and the fierceness drained into something instantly submissive, like a flame checked by a lid. “You shock me; unspeakably shock me,” Mr. Crisparkle returned.

The young man’s head fell. The town lay hushed around them—old walls holding in the night, and the Cathedral’s great bulk seeming to drink up sound. Even so, Cloisterham had its noises: distant water in the weir, a footfall far down a lane, the soft scrape of their shoes; and Mr. Crisparkle, who loved order in a life as much as in a choir, felt how easily a harsh word could bruise such quiet.

After a little while Neville said, low: “You never saw him beat your sister. I have seen him beat mine, more than once or twice, and I never forgot it.”

“Nothing,” Mr. Crisparkle answered—his voice firm, yet his indignation warming him in spite of himself—“not even a beloved and beautiful sister’s tears under dastardly ill-usage, could justify those horrible expressions that you used.”

“I am sorry I used them, and especially to you, sir. I beg to recall them.” Then, as if he could not bear that even pity should misconceive her: “But permit me to set you right on one point. You spoke of my sister’s tears. My sister would have let him tear her to pieces, before she would have let him believe that he could make her shed a tear.”

Mr. Crisparkle, turning over the notes he had already made of Helena Landless’s face and bearing, was neither surprised nor inclined to dispute it.

“Perhaps you will think it strange, sir,” Neville went on, hesitating as if he had been taught to fear the sound of his own candour, “that I should so soon ask you to allow me to confide in you, and to have the kindness to hear a word or two from me in my defence?”

“Defence?” Mr. Crisparkle repeated. “You are not on your defence, Mr. Neville.”

“I think I am, sir. At least I know I should be, if you were better acquainted with my character.”

“Well, Mr. Neville,” the Minor Canon replied, “what if you leave me to find it out?”

“Since it is your pleasure, sir,” Neville answered, and a sullen disappointment came up swiftly, darkening the deference, “since it is your pleasure to check me in my impulse, I must submit.”

There was something in the tone that pricked the conscience of the man who heard it—something that hinted how a trust, once turned aside, might never return, and how easily a mis-shapen mind, offered its first honest morsel of confession, might snatch it back and go hungry again.

They were within sight of the lights in Mr. Crisparkle’s windows. Laughter and voices, homely and cheerful, floated out into the cool night like warmth from an open oven. The Minor Canon stopped.

“Let us turn back and take a turn or two up and down, Mr. Neville, or you may not have time to finish what you wish to say to me. You are hasty in thinking that I mean to check you. Quite the contrary. I invite your confidence.”

“You have invited it, sir, without knowing it, ever since I came here. I say ‘ever since,’ as if I had been here a week.” Neville gave a quick, uncomfortable smile. “The truth is, we came here—my sister and I—to quarrel with you, and affront you, and break away again.”

“Really?” said Mr. Crisparkle, at a loss for any other word that would not judge too quickly.

“You see, we could not know what you were beforehand, sir; could we?”

“Clearly not.”

“And having liked no one else with whom we have ever been brought into contact, we had made up our minds not to like you.”

“Really?” Mr. Crisparkle said again, more softly, as if to keep the confession from echoing too loud in the night.

“But we do like you, sir; and we see an unmistakable difference between your house and your reception of us, and anything else we have ever known.” Neville’s gaze travelled the still street, the old stones, the high dark line of the Cathedral tower cutting the sky. “This—and my happening to be alone with you—and everything around us seeming so quiet and peaceful after Mr. Honeythunder’s departure—and Cloisterham being so old and grave and beautiful, with the moon shining on it—these things inclined me to open my heart.”

“I quite understand, Mr. Neville. And it is salutary to listen to such influences.”

“In describing my own imperfections, sir, I must ask you not to suppose that I am describing my sister’s. She has come out of the disadvantages of our miserable life as much better than I am, as that Cathedral tower is higher than those chimneys.”

Mr. Crisparkle, for his own part, was not so sure; he had seen in Helena a force that might scorch as readily as it could warm.

“I have had, sir, from my earliest remembrance, to suppress a deadly and bitter hatred. This has made me secret and revengeful. I have been always tyrannically held down by the strong hand. This has driven me, in my weakness, to the resource of being false and mean. I have been stinted of education, liberty, money, dress, the very necessaries of life, the commonest pleasures of childhood, the commonest possessions of youth. This has caused me to be utterly wanting in—I don’t know what emotions, or remembrances, or good instincts—I have not even a name for the thing, you see!—that you have had to work upon in other young men to whom you have been accustomed.”

This is evidently true, Mr. Crisparkle thought; and this is not encouraging.

“And to finish with, sir: I have been brought up among abject and servile dependents, of an inferior race, and I may easily have contracted some affinity with them. Sometimes, I don’t know but that it may be a drop of what is tigerish in their blood.”

As in the case of that remark just now, Mr. Crisparkle thought.

“In a last word of reference to my sister, sir—we are twin children—you ought to know, to her honour, that nothing in our misery ever subdued her, though it often cowed me. When we ran away from it”—his voice faltered, as if he tasted again the grit of dust on the road and the fear of capture like a bitter spice—“we ran away four times in six years, to be soon brought back and cruelly punished), the flight was always of her planning and leading. Each time she dressed as a boy, and showed the daring of a man. I take it we were seven years old when we first decamped; but I remember, when I lost the pocket-knife with which she was to have cut her hair short, how desperately she tried to tear it out, or bite it off. I have nothing further to say, sir, except that I hope you will bear with me and make allowance for me.”

“Of that, Mr. Neville, you may be sure,” returned the Minor Canon. “I don’t preach more than I can help, and I will not repay your confidence with a sermon. But I entreat you to bear in mind, very seriously and steadily, that if I am to do you any good, it can only be with your own assistance; and that you can only render that, efficiently, by seeking aid from Heaven.”

“I will try to do my part, sir.”

“And, Mr. Neville, I will try to do mine. Here is my hand on it. May God bless our endeavours!”

They clasped hands, and the act had the quietness of a contract made without witnesses, under a moon that had looked down upon a hundred such private vows in Cloisterham, and forgotten none.

They stood at the door now, the cheerful sound of voices and laughter within. Mr. Crisparkle, however, held back.

“We will take one more turn before going in,” he said, “for I want to ask you a question. When you said you were in a changed mind concerning me, you spoke not only for yourself, but for your sister too?”

“Undoubtedly I did, sir.”

“Excuse me, Mr. Neville, but I think you have had no opportunity of communicating with your sister, since I met you. Mr. Honeythunder was very eloquent; but perhaps I may venture to say, without ill-nature, that he rather monopolised the occasion. May you not have answered for your sister without sufficient warrant?”

Neville shook his head, and a proud smile touched his mouth. “You don’t know, sir, yet, what a complete understanding can exist between my sister and me, though no spoken word—perhaps hardly as much as a look—may have passed between us. She not only feels as I have described, but she very well knows that I am taking this opportunity of speaking to you, both for her and for myself.”

Mr. Crisparkle looked in his face with some incredulity; yet there was such absolute conviction there that the Minor Canon found his eyes dropping to the pavement, and he mused until they came to the door again, as if the stones held an older language than speech—one the twins had learned early, in silence.

“I will ask for one more turn, sir, this time,” Neville said, colour rising, and something sharper stirring under the civility. “But for Mr. Honeythunder’s—I think you called it eloquence, sir?” There was a faint slyness in it.

“I—yes, I called it eloquence,” said Mr. Crisparkle.

“But for Mr. Honeythunder’s eloquence, I might have had no need to ask you what I am going to ask you. This Mr. Edwin Drood, sir: I think that’s the name?”

“Quite correct,” said Mr. Crisparkle. “D-r-double o-d.”

“Does he—or did he—read with you, sir?”

“Never, Mr. Neville. He comes here visiting his relation, Mr. Jasper.”

“Is Miss Bud his relation too, sir?” Neville asked, and there was a sudden superciliousness in the question that made Mr. Crisparkle’s attention leap, as if a twig had cracked in a hedgerow.

He explained, aloud, what he knew of their little story: that Edwin and Rosa were betrothed.

“O! that’s it, is it?” Neville said. “I understand his air of proprietorship now!”

He spoke it so much to himself—so much as if he were tasting an unpleasant word and could not help letting it out—that Mr. Crisparkle felt it would be like reading over a man’s shoulder to take notice.

A moment after, they re-entered the house.

Mr. Jasper was at the piano as they came into the drawing-room, accompanying Miss Rosebud while she sang. He played without notes, and because she was a heedless little creature, apt to wander off the key as a child wanders from a path to pick flowers, he followed her lips most attentively, with his eyes as well as his hands, and from time to time gave her the key-note—softly, carefully—as if dropping a crumb for her to find her way back.

Standing with an arm drawn round Rosa, but with a face far more intent on Jasper than on the singing, stood Helena. Between her and her brother, as Neville entered, an instantaneous recognition passed—too quick to be called a sign, and yet so bright in its brevity that Mr. Crisparkle saw, or thought he saw, the understanding Neville had spoken of flash out and vanish.

Neville took his admiring station, leaning against the piano opposite the singer. Mr. Crisparkle sat down by the china shepherdess. Edwin Drood, in his easy gallantry, furled and unfurled Miss Twinkleton’s fan; and that lady passively claimed that exhibitor’s proprietorship in the accomplishment on view which Mr. Tope the Verger daily claimed in the Cathedral service, as if art were a thing to be shown, ticketed, and approved.

The song went on—a sorrowful strain of parting. Rosa’s fresh young voice was very plaintive and tender, like milk just beginning to steam before it boils over. Yet beneath it lay the room’s other music: the pressed hush of listeners, the faint stir of fabric, the breathing held and released, and, through it all, Jasper’s guidance, which seemed less accompaniment than possession.

As Jasper watched the pretty lips and again and again hinted the one note—as though it were a low whisper from himself—the voice grew less steady, until all at once the singer broke into a burst of tears, and shrieked out, with her hands over her eyes:

“I can’t bear this! I am frightened! Take me away!”

With one swift turn of her lithe figure, Helena laid the little beauty on a sofa as if she had never caught her up. Then, on one knee beside her, and with one hand upon Rosa’s rosy mouth while with the other she appealed to all the rest, Helena said:

“It’s nothing; it’s all over; don’t speak to her for one minute, and she is well!”

Jasper’s hands had, in the same instant, lifted themselves from the keys, and were poised above them as though he waited to resume. In that attitude he sat quite quiet, not even looking round when all the rest had changed their places and were reassuring one another. It was as if he listened not to their voices but to the room’s altered silence, and found it to his taste.

“Pussy’s not used to an audience; that’s the fact,” Edwin Drood said, with the careless tone of one who treats terror like a small domestic accident—spilled tea, a scorched crust. “She got nervous, and couldn’t hold out. Besides, Jack, you are such a conscientious master, and require so much, that I believe you make her afraid of you. No wonder.”

“No wonder,” repeated Helena.

“There, Jack, you hear!” Edwin went on, laughing. “You would be afraid of him, under similar circumstances, wouldn’t you, Miss Landless?”

“Not under any circumstances,” Helena returned, and if there was fear in her nature it did not live in her voice.

Jasper brought down his hands, looked over his shoulder, and begged to thank Miss Landless for her vindication of his character. Then he fell to dumbly playing, without striking the notes—moving as if sound were beneath him, and only the shape of it mattered—while his little pupil was taken to an open window for air, and otherwise petted and restored.

When Rosa was brought back, his place was empty.

“Jack’s gone, Pussy,” Edwin told her. “I am more than half afraid he didn’t like to be charged with being the Monster who had frightened you.”

But she answered never a word, and shivered, as if the night air had been laid on her too long and too cold.

Miss Twinkleton now opined that indeed these were late hours, Mrs. Crisparkle, for finding ourselves outside the walls of the Nuns’ House; and that we who undertook the formation of the future wives and mothers of England—here dropping her voice, as if the phrase were a preserve to be whispered over—were really bound—voice rising again—to set a better example than one of rakish habits. Wrappers were put in requisition, and the two young cavaliers volunteered to see the ladies home.

It was soon done. The gate of the Nuns’ House closed upon them with a sound that seemed to seal away the last laughter, leaving only the thin night-noises of the town—distant steps, a faint latch, the far-off murmur of water—like a careful kitchen grown quiet after supper.

The boarders had retired. Only Mrs. Tisher, in solitary vigil, awaited the new pupil. Helena’s bedroom being within Rosa’s, very little introduction or explanation was necessary before Helena was placed in charge of her new friend and left for the night.

“This is a blessed relief, my dear,” Helena said, when the door had shut and the house seemed to breathe again in its own stillness. “I have been dreading all day that I should be brought to bay at this time.”

“There are not many of us,” Rosa returned, “and we are good-natured girls; at least the others are; I can answer for them.”

“I can answer for you,” Helena laughed, searching the lovely little face with her dark, fiery eyes, and tenderly caressing the small figure—as if she would reassure herself by touch that this bright, delicate thing was real and within reach. “You will be a friend to me, won’t you?”

“I hope so. But the idea of my being a friend to you seems too absurd, though.”

“Why?”

“O, I am such a mite of a thing, and you are so womanly and handsome. You seem to have resolution and power enough to crush me. I shrink into nothing by the side of your presence even.”

“I am a neglected creature, my dear,” Helena said, and now there was no laughing in it, “unacquainted with all accomplishments, sensitively conscious that I have everything to learn, and deeply ashamed to own my ignorance.”

“And yet you acknowledge everything to me!” Rosa said.

“My pretty one, can I help it? There is a fascination in you.”

“O! is there though?” Rosa pouted, half jest and half earnest, and the pettishness had in it a child’s hunger to be prized. “What a pity Master Eddy doesn’t feel it more!”

Of course her relations with that young gentleman had already been imparted in Minor Canon Corner, as ordinary information is passed with tea and bread-and-butter, and yet can carry the weight of a fate.

“Why, surely he must love you with all his heart!” Helena cried, with an earnestness that threatened to blaze into ferocity if he did not.

“Eh? O, well, I suppose he does,” Rosa said, pouting again. “I am sure I have no right to say he doesn’t. Perhaps it’s my fault. Perhaps I am not as nice to him as I ought to be. I don’t think I am. But it is so ridiculous!”

Helena’s eyes demanded what was.

“We are,” Rosa answered, as if she had spoken aloud already. “We are such a ridiculous couple. And we are always quarrelling.”

“Why?”

“Because we both know we are ridiculous, my dear!” Rosa gave that answer as if it shut the matter up like a box with a clasp.

Helena looked at her for some moments with a masterful intentness, and then, as if she could not keep her need in check, impulsively put out both her hands.

“You will be my friend and help me?”

“Indeed, my dear, I will,” Rosa replied, with affectionate childishness that went straight to Helena’s heart. “I will be as good a friend as such a mite of a thing can be to such a noble creature as you. And be a friend to me, please; I don’t understand myself: and I want a friend who can understand me, very much indeed.”

Helena kissed her, and retaining both her hands said, very quietly:

“Who is Mr. Jasper?”

Rosa turned her head aside in answering, as if the name had a sound that bruised her ear. “Eddy’s uncle, and my music-master.”

“You do not love him?”

“Ugh!” Rosa put her hands up to her face and shook with fear or horror, as if she had tasted something rancid and could not get it from her mouth.

“You know that he loves you?”

“O, don’t, don’t, don’t!” Rosa cried, dropping on her knees and clinging to her new resource. “Don’t tell me of it! He terrifies me. He haunts my thoughts like a dreadful ghost. I feel that I am never safe from him. I feel as if he could pass in through the wall when he is spoken of.”

She actually looked round, as if expecting the shadow behind her to thicken into his shape.

“Try to tell me more about it, darling,” Helena said; and the strength in her voice was like a steady hand upon a shaking cup, preventing the spill.

“Yes, I will, I will. Because you are so strong. But hold me the while, and stay with me afterwards.”

“My child! You speak as if he had threatened you in some dark way.”

“He has never spoken to me about—that. Never.”

“What has he done?”

“He has made a slave of me with his looks. He has forced me to understand him, without his saying a word; and he has forced me to keep silence, without his uttering a threat.” Rosa’s words came in little rushes, breathy and broken, as if she were being pursued. “When I play, he never moves his eyes from my hands. When I sing, he never moves his eyes from my lips. When he corrects me, and strikes a note, or a chord, or plays a passage, he himself is in the sounds, whispering that he pursues me as a lover, and commanding me to keep his secret.”

The very idea made the room feel louder in its quiet: the imagined piano-note, the remembered pressure of his presence, the way a single sound can take over a mind until even silence seems to throb.

“I avoid his eyes, but he forces me to see them without looking at them. Even when a glaze comes over them—which is sometimes the case—and he seems to wander away into a frightful sort of dream in which he threatens most, he obliges me to know it, and to know that he is sitting close at my side, more terrible to me than ever.”

“What is this imagined threatening, pretty one? What is threatened?”

“I don’t know. I have never even dared to think or wonder what it is.”

“And was this all, to-night?”

“This was all; except that to-night when he watched my lips so closely as I was singing, besides feeling terrified I felt ashamed and passionately hurt. It was as if he kissed me, and I couldn’t bear it, but cried out.” Rosa clung harder, as if she would hide inside Helena’s strength. “You must never breathe this to any one. Eddy is devoted to him. But you said to-night that you would not be afraid of him, under any circumstances, and that gives me—who am so much afraid of him—courage to tell only you. Hold me! Stay with me! I am too frightened to be left by myself.”

The lustrous gipsy-face drooped over the clinging arms and bosom; the wild black hair fell down protectingly over the childish form. In Helena’s intense dark eyes there slept a gleam of fire, though they were softened now with compassion and admiration, and with something else—older than the present hour, and as resolute as an oath made under the moon.

Let whomsoever it most concerned look well to it.

They waited until the two young ladies had passed the Nuns’ House gate and were swallowed by its enclosed quiet, leaving behind them the cold stare of the polished door-plate, bright as an unblinking eye. The street lay long and pale under moonlight, and in that light the stones seemed to keep their own counsel. Somewhere beyond, Cloisterham’s night-sounds were at work: the measured drip in a gutter, the far drag of a cart-wheel dying away, the Cathedral’s vast hush holding all the smaller noises as if in a hand.

Edwin Drood and Neville Landless looked after the vanished figures, looked at each other, and then, without agreement spoken, fell into step and walked slowly away.

“Do you stay here long, Mr. Drood?” Neville asked.

“Not this time.” Edwin’s answer had the offhand air of a man shrugging on a coat. “I go up to London again to-morrow. But I shall be here, off and on, until next Midsummer. Then I take my leave of Cloisterham—and England too—for many a long day, I expect.”

“Are you going abroad?”

“Going to wake up Egypt a little,” Edwin said, with the easy superiority of someone who has already seen his name written neatly into the future.

“Are you reading?” Neville asked, as if that word—reading—were a rung that might help him climb.

“Reading?” Edwin repeated, and the sound carried contempt, like a spoon striking an empty plate. “No. Doing. Working. Engineering. My small patrimony was left—part of it—as capital in the Firm I’m with, by my father, a former partner. I’m a charge upon the Firm till I come of age, and then I step into my modest share. Jack—you met him at dinner—is, until then, my guardian and trustee.”

“I heard from Mr. Crisparkle of your other good fortune.”

Edwin stopped as if a pebble had been kicked against his toe on purpose. “What do you mean by my other good fortune?”

Neville had spoken in that odd manner of his—advancing, yet wary; like a man who expects both to strike and to be struck. Edwin’s sudden edge was not polite, and the air between them tightened.

“I hope,” Neville said, watching Edwin’s face, “there is no offence, Mr. Drood, in my innocently referring to your betrothal?”

“By George!” Edwin broke out, moving on again, quicker now, as if pace could scrape off irritation. “Everybody in this chattering old Cloisterham refers to it. I wonder no public-house has been set up with my portrait for the sign of The Betrothed’s Head. Or Pussy’s portrait. One or the other.”

“I am not accountable for Mr. Crisparkle’s mentioning the matter to me, quite openly,” Neville began.

“No; that’s true; you are not,” Edwin returned. “But—”

“But I am accountable for mentioning it to you,” Neville said, and there was a flush of earnestness in him, like heat rising too fast from a pan. “And I did so, on the supposition that you could not fail to be highly proud of it.”

In that moment two secret irritations—each fed by a different face—met and took fire. Neville, already stirred by Rose Maylie’s quiet sweetness, could not bear to hear her treated as if she were a settled portion in a ledger. Edwin, already struck by Helena Landless’s presence, could not bear her brother’s tone, as if Edwin were easily appraised and easily dismissed.

Edwin answered at last, with a mock-urbanity that stung all the more for its tune. “I don’t know, Mr. Neville”—he adopted the address he had heard from Mr. Crisparkle—“that what people are proudest of they usually talk most about; I don’t know either that what they are proudest of they most like other people to talk about. But I live a busy life, and I speak under correction by you readers, who ought to know everything, and I daresay do.”

The night received those words and held them. They walked under a strip of moon, then through shadow; and Edwin, under the transparent cover of a half-hummed popular air, would stop now and again to pretend admiration for some “picturesque effect,” as if scenery were a dish set before him to be tasted and praised, while Neville grew hotter at every leisurely pause.

“It does not seem to me very civil in you,” Neville said at length, “to reflect upon a stranger who comes here not having had your advantages, to try to make up for lost time. But, to be sure, I was not brought up in ‘busy life,’ and my ideas of civility were formed among Heathens.”

“Perhaps the best civility,” Edwin retorted, “whatever kind of people we’re brought up among, is to mind our own business. If you will set me that example, I promise to follow it.”

“Do you know that you take a great deal too much upon yourself?” Neville flung back, “and that in the part of the world I come from you would be called to account for it?”

“By whom, for instance?” Edwin demanded, halting and surveying him with disdain.

A hand came down on Edwin’s shoulder—startling, steady—and before either young man could gather himself, John Jasper stepped between them. He had approached from behind, keeping to the shadowy side of the road as noiselessly as a practised listener. Cloisterham’s night had its own acoustic rules: a footfall on stone could be thunder or nothing, depending on how you placed it, and Jasper placed his as if he meant never to be heard.

“Ned, Ned, Ned!” Jasper said. His voice was low, but it cut cleanly through the tension, the way a knife parts twine. “We must have no more of this. I don’t like this. I have overheard high words between you two. Remember, my dear boy, you are almost in the position of host to-night. You belong, as it were, to the place, and in a manner represent it towards a stranger. Mr. Neville is a stranger, and you should respect the obligations of hospitality.”

Then, laying his left hand on Neville’s inner shoulder, and keeping his right on Edwin’s, he walked on between them, holding them as if he held two restive dogs by the collar.

“And, Mr. Neville,” he went on, “you will pardon me; but I appeal to you to govern your temper too. Now, what is amiss? But why ask! Let there be nothing amiss, and the question is superfluous. We are all three on a good understanding, are we not?”

There was a small silent wrestle between the two young men as to who should speak last. Edwin won it.

“So far as I am concerned, Jack, there is no anger in me.”

“Nor in me,” Neville said, though his words came less freely, as if he had to force them past something sharp in his throat. “But if Mr. Drood knew all that lies behind me, far away from here, he might know better how it is that sharp-edged words have sharp edges to wound me.”

“Perhaps,” Jasper soothed, “we had better not qualify our good understanding. We had better not say anything having the appearance of a remonstrance or condition; it might not seem generous. Frankly and freely, you see there is no anger in Ned. Frankly and freely, there is no anger in you, Mr. Neville?”

“None at all, Mr. Jasper.”

“All over then!” Jasper said, brightening his tone as if drawing a curtain on the scene. “Now, my bachelor gatehouse is a few yards from here, and the heater is on the fire, and the wine and glasses are on the table, and it is not a stone’s throw from Minor Canon Corner. Ned, you are up and away to-morrow. We will carry Mr. Neville in with us, to take a stirrup-cup.”

“With all my heart, Jack,” Edwin answered.

“And with all mine, Mr. Jasper,” Neville said, because he could not say less; though something in him resisted going, the way the body resists a room where it knows a quarrel will warm itself back to life.

Jasper, still between them, kept his hands upon their shoulders and, with a musician’s ease, turned the refrain of a drinking song, letting its familiar cadence smooth the air. Cloisterham’s night, which had been a tense, listening thing, seemed for an instant to accept the tune as part of domestic order: fire, glass, a late cup, and men pretending good humour.

They went up to Jasper’s rooms.

When Jasper added the light of a lamp to the fire’s glow, the first object to assert itself was the portrait above the chimneypiece. It stood there like a witness—Rose Maylie’s face, caught and fixed. It was not the sort of witness that improves an understanding already strained. Both young men glanced at it, and both said nothing.

Jasper, who seemed to have only an imperfect clue to the cause of their earlier heat, called attention to it at once, shading the lamp to throw a stronger light upon the painted features.

“You recognise that picture, Mr. Neville?”

“I recognise it,” Neville returned, “but it is far from flattering the original.”

“O, you are hard upon it!” Jasper said. “It was done by Ned, who made me a present of it.”

“I am sorry for that, Mr. Drood.” Neville’s apology was real. “If I had known I was in the artist’s presence—”

“O, a joke, sir, a mere joke,” Edwin cut in, and yawned, provokingly at ease. “A little humouring of Pussy’s points! I’m going to paint her gravely one of these days, if she’s good.”

He spoke with leisurely patronage and indifference, and threw himself back in his chair, clasping his hands behind his head to make a rest for it, as if he sat before an empty hearth in a club and not in a room where every word was food for offence. To Neville—whose feelings ran on quick, sharp heat—this ease was exasperating. Jasper’s eyes moved observantly from one to the other; a slight smile touched his mouth, and he turned his back to the two young men to mix a jug of mulled wine by the fire.

The warm scent began to build: wine meeting heat; spice bruised and waking; something sweet—perhaps sugar—melting down into the dark. Jasper compounded it as if he were balancing a recipe that must come out right: stirring, pausing, tasting the air rather than the liquid. The fire gave a soft, living crackle, and the kettle—or heater—made its small domestic sighs. Against that soundscape, the clink of glass and the scrape of chair-legs were sudden, bright intrusions, like metal on stone.

“I suppose, Mr. Neville,” Edwin said, quick to resent what he saw plainly in Neville’s face, “I suppose that if you painted the picture of your lady love—”

“I can’t paint,” Neville interrupted, too fast.

“That’s your misfortune, and not your fault. You would if you could. But if you could, I suppose you would make her—no matter what she was in reality—Juno, Minerva, Diana, and Venus, all in one. Eh?”

“I have no lady love, and I can’t say.”

“If I were to try my hand,” Edwin went on, the boyish boast getting up in him like froth, “on a portrait of Miss Landless—in earnest, mind you; in earnest—you should see what I could do!”

“My sister’s consent to sit for it being first got, I suppose?” Neville answered, stiff with anger held back by manners. “As it never will be got, I am afraid I shall never see what you can do. I must bear the loss.”

Jasper turned from the fire, filled a large goblet for Neville, filled a large goblet for Edwin, and handed each his own; then filled for himself.

“Come, Mr. Neville,” Jasper said, “we are to drink to my nephew, Ned. As it is his foot that is in the stirrup—metaphorically—our stirrup-cup is to be devoted to him. Ned, my dearest fellow, my love!”

He set the example by nearly emptying his glass. Neville followed. Edwin, with, “Thank you both very much,” followed their example.

The mulled wine went down warm and spiced, its heat spreading quickly, too quickly; the head took it as a sudden blaze takes dry kindling. Edwin’s cheeks grew notably flushed; so did Neville’s. The room felt closer, the fire louder, the night outside farther away—its clean silence shut out by the thickened air and the drink’s swift encouragement.

“Look at him,” Jasper cried, stretching out a hand admiringly, tenderly, rallyingly. “See where he lounges so easily, Mr. Neville! The world is all before him where to choose. A life of stirring work and interest, a life of change and excitement, a life of domestic ease and love! Look at him!”

Edwin still sat thrown back, hands clasped behind his head.

“See how little he heeds it all!” Jasper bantered on. “It is hardly worth his while to pluck the golden fruit that hangs ripe on the tree for him. And yet consider the contrast, Mr. Neville. You and I have no prospect of stirring work and interest, or of change and excitement, or of domestic ease and love. You and I have no prospect—unless you are more fortunate than I am, which may easily be—but the tedious unchanging round of this dull place.”

“Upon my soul, Jack,” Edwin said, complacently, “I feel quite apologetic for having my way smoothed as you describe. But you know what I know, Jack, and it may not be so very easy as it seems, after all. May it, Pussy?”

He snapped his thumb and finger toward the portrait, as if calling a dog to heel.

“We have got to hit it off yet; haven’t we, Pussy? You know what I mean, Jack.”

His words had thickened, as though his tongue were coated. Jasper, quiet and self-possessed, looked to Neville as if expecting his comment. When Neville spoke, his own speech was thick too, blurred at the edges.

“It might have been better for Mr. Drood to have known some hardships,” he said defiantly.

“Pray,” Edwin retorted, turning only his eyes, “pray why might it have been better for Mr. Drood to have known some hardships?”

“Ay,” Jasper assented, interested; “let us know why?”

“Because they might have made him more sensible,” Neville said, “of good fortune that is not by any means necessarily the result of his own merits.”

Jasper’s eyes flicked to Edwin, waiting for the blow to be returned.

“Have you known hardships, may I ask?” Edwin said, sitting upright at last.

Jasper’s eyes turned to Neville, waiting again.

“I have.”

“And what have they made you sensible of?”

“I have told you once before to-night.”

“You have done nothing of the sort.”

“I tell you I have. That you take a great deal too much upon yourself.”

“You added something else to that, if I remember?”

“Yes, I did say something else.”

“Say it again.”

“I said that in the part of the world I come from, you would be called to account for it.”

“Only there?” Edwin cried, with a contemptuous laugh. “A long way off, I believe? Yes; I see! That part of the world is at a safe distance.”

“Say here, then,” Neville rejoined, rising in a fury that shoved his chair back with a harsh scrape. “Say anywhere! Your vanity is intolerable, your conceit is beyond endurance; you talk as if you were some rare and precious prize, instead of a common boaster. You are a common fellow, and a common boaster.”

“Pooh, pooh,” Edwin said, equally furious but more collected. “How should you know? You may know a black common fellow, or a black common boaster, when you see him—and no doubt you have a large acquaintance that way—but you are no judge of white men.”

The insult, flung as carelessly as a slop thrown from a doorstep, struck Neville like boiling liquid. His dark face, already heated by wine and rage, turned a deeper, dangerous red. The room’s sounds sharpened: the fire’s crack, the glass’s faint ring in Jasper’s hand, the heavy beat of blood in the ears as if the house itself had acquired a hammering heart.

Neville flung the dregs of his wine in Edwin’s face, and snatched up the goblet to hurl after it. Jasper caught his arm in the nick of time.

“Ned, my dear fellow!” Jasper cried aloud. “I entreat you, I command you, to be still!”

There was a rush of all three, a clatter of glass, a violent overturning of chairs.

“Mr. Neville, for shame! Give this glass to me. Open your hand, sir. I will have it!”

Neville threw him off and stood for an instant with the goblet still uplifted, trembling in murderous passion. Then he dashed it down under the grate with such force that broken splinters sprang out again in a shower, glittering and flying like brief bright insects. He left the house.

When he first emerged into the night air, nothing around him was still or steady. The street reeled; the moon seemed to whirl; the Cathedral tower leaned and righted itself like a great ship. He only knew that he stood bareheaded in the midst of a blood-red turning, waiting to be struggled with and to struggle to the death.

But nothing happened. The moon looked down upon him with that impartial brightness that makes rage feel foolish, as if he were already dead after his fit. He held his head, where his heart seemed to beat up into his skull like a steam-hammer, and staggered away.

Half-conscious, he heard himself bolted and barred out behind him, as if he were a dangerous animal. The sound of metal sliding home—small, domestic, final—had in it a kind of cruelty: not loud, not theatrical, but decisive. For a few moments he did not know what to do. Wild thoughts of the river rose and sank; the river’s dark hush offered itself like an end to noise. But the moonlight lay on the Cathedral and the graves, washing them into a cold, pale calm; and remembrance of his sister came over him, and the thought of what he owed to the good man who that very day had won his confidence and given him his pledge.

He went to Minor Canon Corner and knocked softly.

It was Mr. Crisparkle’s custom to sit up last of his early household, very softly touching his piano and practising his favourite parts in concerted vocal music. The very air there seemed trained to gentleness; the night wind that slipped along the Corner on a still night was not more subdued than the Minor Canon’s careful touch, out of regard for sleepers, and for the china shepherdess who presided over the domestic peace. Against such quiet, Neville’s knock was a harsh little thing, yet he made it as light as shame would allow.

It was answered immediately by Mr. Crisparkle himself. He opened the door with a candle in his hand. His cheerful face fell into disappointed amazement.

“Mr. Neville! In this disorder! Where have you been?”

“I have been to Mr. Jasper’s, sir. With his nephew.”

“Come in.”

The Minor Canon took him by the elbow with a strong hand, in a strictly scientific manner worthy of his morning trainings, and guided him into his little book-room, shutting the door upon the rest of the house’s sleep.

“I have begun ill, sir,” Neville said. “I have begun dreadfully ill.”

“Too true. You are not sober, Mr. Neville.”

“I am afraid I am not, sir—though I can satisfy you at another time that I have had very little indeed to drink, and that it overcame me in the strangest and most sudden manner.”

“Mr. Neville, Mr. Neville,” the Minor Canon said, shaking his head with a sorrowful smile, “I have heard that said before.”

“I think—my mind is much confused, but I think—it is equally true of Mr. Jasper’s nephew, sir.”

“Very likely,” was the dry rejoinder.

“We quarrelled, sir. He insulted me most grossly. He had heated that tigerish blood I told you of to-day, before then.”
