“Mr. Neville,” Mr. Crisparkle returned, mildly but firmly, “I request you not to speak to me with that clenched right hand. Unclench it, if you please.”

“He goaded me, sir,” Neville said, instantly obeying, “beyond my power of endurance. I cannot say whether or no he meant it at first, but he did it. He certainly meant it at last. In short, sir”—and now the confession burst from him like a sob turned to speech—“in the passion into which he lashed me, I would have cut him down if I could, and I tried to do it.”

“You have clenched that hand again,” Mr. Crisparkle quietly observed.

“I beg your pardon, sir.”

“You know your room, for I showed it you before dinner; but I will accompany you to it once more. Your arm, if you please. Softly, for the house is all a-bed.”

He scooped his hand into the same trained elbow-rest as before, backing it with the inert strength of his arm, as skilfully as a Police Expert and with a repose that steadied the unsteady. Thus he conducted Neville to the pleasant, orderly old room prepared for him.

There Neville threw himself into a chair and, flinging his arms upon the reading-table, laid his head on them with wretched self-reproach. The Minor Canon had it in mind to leave without a word. But he looked back, saw the dejected figure, and returned to the door; touched it with a mild hand and said simply, “Good night!”

A sob was Neville’s only answer.

Down-stairs, another soft knock came to the outer door. Mr. Crisparkle opened it to John Jasper, who stood there holding Neville’s hat.

“We have had an awful scene with him,” Jasper said in a low voice.

“Has it been so bad as that?”

“Murderous!”

Mr. Crisparkle remonstrated at once. “No, no, no. Do not use such strong words.”

“He might have laid my dear boy dead at my feet. It is no fault of his that he did not. But that I was—through the mercy of God—swift and strong with him, he would have cut him down on my hearth.”

The phrase smote home; it had Neville’s own words in it, turned back as evidence. Mr. Crisparkle felt the blow inwardly, though his face stayed composed.

“Seeing what I have seen to-night, and hearing what I have heard,” Jasper added with great earnestness, “I shall never know peace of mind when there is danger of those two coming together, with no one else to interfere. It was horrible. There is something of the tiger in his dark blood.”

Again—what Neville had said; again, repeated with a different emphasis, made to sound like a verdict. Mr. Crisparkle heard, beneath Jasper’s solicitude, the power of words to brand; how certain phrases, once adopted by a dominant tongue and repeated with confidence, can steal a man’s whole history and reduce it to a convenient creature.

“You, my dear sir,” Jasper pursued, taking his hand, “even you, have accepted a dangerous charge.”

“You need have no fear for me, Jasper,” Mr. Crisparkle returned with a quiet smile. “I have none for myself.”

“I have none for myself,” Jasper answered, with an emphasis on the last pronoun, “because I am not, nor am I in the way of being, the object of his hostility. But you may be, and my dear boy has been. Good night!”

Mr. Crisparkle went in with the hat—so easily, so almost imperceptibly acquiring the right to be hung up in his hall—hung it on its place, and went thoughtfully to bed, while the house settled again into its careful hush: piano silenced, candle out, only the small domestic creaks and the far, steady presence of the Cathedral’s night pressing on, as if listening to remember what words had been spoken and what words had been used to name a man.

Rosa had learned early to taste absence the way other children tasted jam—sweet at first, because it was served with pity, and then bitter in the mouth because it never ended.

She could not remember any home before the Nuns’ House with the steadiness with which she remembered a single room, a single bed, and a single dress darkened by water. Her mother—so young in Rosa’s mind that she seemed hardly older than Rosa herself—had been carried in from a pleasure-party drowned, laid out in a thin summer gown whose every fold and faded colour Rosa could still count as if with her fingertips. The wet hair had streamed and clung; and, caught in it, crushed petals—little shreds of flowers that had gone to ruin against that quiet head. The picture had fixed itself like a smell that will not leave a garment: river-cold, bruised green, and the faint, sick sweetness of something once festive.

Her father’s grief followed as surely as the tide. There had been a year of wildness, then a year of being bowed down, and then—on the first anniversary—he died as if the calendar itself had turned a key in him.

It was in that same year, when kindness went looking for something to do with its hands, that old friends—Drood and the young widower who was Rosa’s father—had fastened a plan together: the children should be pledged. The plan outlived the men who made it. Drood, too, went silently where all pilgrimages end; and so Edwin and Rosa came to stand on the path laid for them by dead feet.

From the time Rosa had been brought to Cloisterham, there had always been a tender atmosphere around her, like gauze hung between her and the world. It changed colour as she changed—pale with her childhood, then brightening as she grew prettier and livelier—but it never lifted. The desire to console her made people treat her as younger than she was, and then, when she was grown, made them still pet her as if she were not. There were soft rivalries at the Nuns’ House about who should please her most: who should tuck some little present into her hand, who should do a trifling service, who should take her away for holidays, who should write the most letters. The rivalry was all smiles and ribbons on the surface, but it could pinch a little underneath; and if such gentler competitions could sour, who knew what harsher contests the good Nuns might once have hidden beneath veil and rosary?

Thus Rosa grew into a small, winning creature—amiable, giddy, wilful—spoilt, yes, in that she counted on kindness as on daylight, but not spoilt in any cold way; for she repaid affection with affection. There was a deep well in her, always sparkling at the top and never yet truly stirred. What would happen when it was—what strong draught might be drawn up from its depth—remained to be seen.

This morning, before Miss Twinkleton had descended to breakfast, the Nuns’ House was already humming with news, as if every shutter had been opened and gossip had flown in on the air.

How it came, nobody could tell. Perhaps it rode on the sparrows’ scuffle in the courtyard; perhaps it was folded into the baker’s loaves like a dark currant, or poured into the milk along with whatever else the milkman poured. Perhaps the housemaids, beating their mats at the gateposts, beat out more than dust and beat in the town’s whisper. However it happened, the story had found every gable and passage, and climbed the stairs to Miss Twinkleton’s sacred room itself, arriving by way of Mrs. Tisher while Miss Twinkleton was dressing—sacrificing to the Graces, as she would have phrased it to any mythological parent.

At first, the tale was one shape, then another, as rumours do when they are cooked too fast and too often.

Miss Landless’s brother had thrown a bottle at Mr. Edwin Drood.

No—he had thrown a knife.

A knife suggested a fork; therefore he had thrown a fork.

By the time the kitchen had seasoned it to its liking, he had thrown bottle, knife, fork, and decanter—every hard, shining thing that could fly from a table—and, with no more introduction than a clatter, had sent them all whistling at Mr. Edwin Drood’s head.

The cook, who dealt in causes as she dealt in gravies (thickening everything), explained why. Miss Landless’s brother had said he admired Miss Bud. Mr. Edwin Drood had replied that he had no business to admire Miss Bud. And then—up came the bottle, knife, fork, and decanter. The cook’s mind could not see a quarrel without imagining crockery in motion.

Poor little Rosa, at the first burst of it, put a forefinger into each ear and slipped into a corner as if into a cupboard, beseeching—no, commanding—never to be told another word. But Helena Landless, pale and resolved, asked leave of Miss Twinkleton to go to her brother; and since she plainly looked like one who would go without leave if leave were refused, she took the more measured course of going first to Mr. Crisparkle for the truth of it.

When she returned, she was closeted with Miss Twinkleton—as if the head of the house were a fine sieve through which the news must pass before any girl might swallow it. What Helena told Rosa alone was as exact as she could make it: there had been words; there had been provocation. She spoke with a flush on her cheek, dwelling on the affront her brother had endured, touching lightly—because she would not wound Rosa—on the ease with which Edwin could begin such things by taking the world so lightly. She brought, too, a petition from Neville Landless: he begged Rosa to forgive him. Helena delivered it with sisterly force, and then would have the subject ended, as if it were a dish set down too hot and too dangerous to be handled further.

It fell to Miss Twinkleton to cool the establishment, to take the boiling pot and set it back on the hob.

So she entered, stately, into what plebeians called the school-room but what she—by patrician twist of language—named “the apartment allotted to study.” She said, with a forensic air that made even a whisper feel like contempt of court:

“Ladies!”

All rose. Mrs. Tisher grouped herself behind her chief as if forming, by posture alone, a second Miss Twinkleton of lesser historical importance.

Miss Twinkleton spoke of Rumour—painted full of tongues by the Bard of Avon—needless to mention the immortal Shakespeare, also called the Swan of his native river, with an allusion to the superstition of that bird singing sweetly on the approach of death (for which, she conceded, there was no ornithological authority). Rumour in Cloisterham—Miss Ferdinand would honour her with attention—was no exception. A slight fracas between two young gentlemen, occurring last night within a hundred miles of these peaceful walls, had been grossly exaggerated. In the first alarm arising from sympathy with “a sweet young friend” not wholly dissociable from one of the gladiators in this bloodless arena, certain maiden elevations had descended into unfit discussion. Responsible inquiry had shown it to be one of those airy nothings pointed at by the Poet—Miss Giggles would supply his name and date of birth within half an hour—and now the subject would be discarded in favour of the grateful labours of the day.

The subject, however, lived on like a smell of scorched sugar that will cling to a clean kitchen. It survived into dinner, where Miss Ferdinand, in defiance of both discipline and decency, contrived a paper moustache, clapped it on, and performed the pantomime of aiming a water-bottle at Miss Giggles, who, in spirited defence, drew a table-spoon like a sword. The clink of it, ridiculous as it was, made Rosa’s stomach feel hollow.

For Rosa could not keep her mind from the quarrel. It came back again and again, like a refrain in a song you do not like but cannot forget. She felt—uncomfortably, guiltily—that she was involved in it: cause, consequence, pretext, prize. It tangled with that other discomfort which never quite left her when she was with Edwin: the sense that she stood in a false position altogether as to her engagement. If she could not feel easy beside him, she was not likely to feel easy away from him. And today she was thrown upon herself, deprived of her new friend’s free companionship, because the quarrel belonged to Helena’s brother and Helena—honest in everything—avoided the subject as both delicate and painful.

At this very time, when Rosa most wanted an ally and least wanted an examiner, her guardian was announced.

Mr. Grewgious had been chosen for her trust because he was incorruptible; for any other surface suitability, one would have searched him as vainly as for a rose in a dry ditch. He was an arid, sandy man who looked as if, put into a mill, he would grind at once into snuff. His hair was a scant, flat crop—colour and consistency like mangy yellow fur. It looked too unlike hair to be real; yet the notion that anyone would wear such a wig was so stupendous that it must have been his own. His face offered little play; the few curves it had were cut so deep they seemed more like hard work than expression. There were notches in his forehead as if Nature had meant to chisel him into sensibility, had grown impatient, and had thrown down the tool: Let him go unfinished.

Too much throat at the top, too much heel and ankle at the bottom; near-sighted, shambling, awkward, and hesitating; with black suit and a betraying flash of white cotton stocking—he should have been merely absurd. Yet, for all that, there was in him some odd power of making an agreeable impression, as some very plain broth can be made sustaining by honesty of ingredients.

Rosa found him, on being admitted, plainly discomfited by the grandeur of being in Miss Twinkleton’s company in Miss Twinkleton’s own sacred room. He looked like a man with a dim foreboding of being examined in something he had never been taught.

“My dear, how do you do? I am glad to see you. My dear, how much improved you are. Permit me to hand you a chair, my dear.”

Miss Twinkleton rose at her writing-table, the very picture of sweet consideration addressed to the polite Universe.

“Will you permit me to retire?”

“By no means, madam, on my account. I beg that you will not move.”

“I must entreat permission to move,” returned Miss Twinkleton, giving the word a little curving grace. “But I will not withdraw, since you are so obliging. If I wheel my desk to this corner window, shall I be in the way?”

“Madam! In the way!”

“You are very kind. Rosa, my dear, you will be under no restraint, I am sure.”

So Miss Twinkleton wheeled her desk to the corner window, where she could both write and listen without seeming to listen; and Rosa and Mr. Grewgious remained by the fire.

“My dear, how do you do? I am glad to see you, my dear,” Mr. Grewgious said again, as if the phrase were a key he had to turn twice before the lock would yield. When Rosa sat, he sat too.

“My visits,” he began, “are like those of the angels—not that I compare myself to an angel.”

“No, sir,” said Rosa, with involuntary sincerity.

“Not by any means,” he agreed, quite unoffended. “I merely refer to my visits, which are few and far between. The angels are, we know very well, up-stairs.”

Miss Twinkleton looked round with a stiff stare that made her pen pause.

“I refer, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious, laying his hand on Rosa’s as if in terror of calling Miss Twinkleton “my dear” by accident, “to the other young ladies.”

Miss Twinkleton, satisfied, resumed her writing.

Mr. Grewgious, as if conscious he had not launched his opening neatly, smoothed his head from back to front in his habitual manner—as if he had dived and were pressing water out—and drew a pocket-book from his coat and a stump of black-lead pencil from his waistcoat.

“I made,” he said, turning leaves, “a guiding memorandum or so—as I usually do, for I have no conversational powers whatever—to which I will, with your permission, my dear, refer. ‘Well and happy.’ Truly. You are well and happy, my dear? You look so.”

“Yes indeed, sir,” Rosa answered.

“For which,” he said, bending his head slightly toward the corner window, “our warmest acknowledgments are due, and I am sure are rendered, to the maternal kindness and constant care and consideration of the lady whom I have now the honour to see before me.”

The compliment fell with a thud, like a dumpling that refuses to rise. Miss Twinkleton, feeling that courtesy required her to be beyond the conversation by now, bit the end of her pen and looked upward, as if awaiting inspiration from some obliging Muse.

Mr. Grewgious smoothed his head again, consulted his notes, and drew a line through “well and happy.”

“‘Pounds, shillings, and pence,’ is my next note. A dry subject for a young lady, but an important subject too. Life is pounds, shillings, and pence. Death is—”

He stopped abruptly, as if the word had opened the drawer where Rosa’s mother lay drowned and her father lay broken; and then, in a softer tone, with the negative seeming to come as an afterthought:

“Death is not pounds, shillings, and pence.”

His voice was as hard and dry as himself—one could imagine it ground into snuff—yet, through those scant means, some kindness made itself felt, like warmth through a thick glove.

“‘Pounds, shillings, and pence.’ You find your allowance always sufficient for your wants, my dear?”

Rosa, wanting for nothing, said it was ample.

“And you are not in debt?”

Rosa laughed. Debt, to her, was a kind of bogey for older people, like gout. Mr. Grewgious stretched his near sight to be sure her laughter meant what he thought it meant, and then said, with a furtive glance toward Miss Twinkleton as he lined out the item:

“Ah! I spoke of having got among the angels! So I did!”

Rosa felt, long before he found the next memorandum, what it would be. She began to blush and, with one embarrassed hand, folded and refolded a crease in her dress.

“‘Marriage.’ Hem!” Mr. Grewgious drew his smoothing hand down over eyes and nose and even chin, then shifted his chair a little nearer and lowered his voice. “I now touch, my dear, upon the point that is the direct cause of my troubling you with the present visit. Otherwise, being a particularly Angular man, I should not have intruded here. I am the last man to intrude into a sphere for which I am so entirely unfitted. I feel, on these premises, as if I was a bear—with the cramp—in a youthful Cotillon.”

His ungainliness gave his simile such likeness that Rosa broke into hearty laughter.

“It strikes you in the same light,” said Mr. Grewgious, perfectly calm. “Just so. To return to my memorandum. Mr. Edwin has been to and fro here, as was arranged. You have mentioned that, in your quarterly letters to me. And you like him, and he likes you.”

“I like him very much, sir,” Rosa said, with a shy emphasis on the verb that meant more than the words.

“So I said, my dear,” returned Mr. Grewgious, whose ear could not catch so fine a distinction. “Good. And you correspond.”

“We write to one another,” Rosa pouted, remembering how their letters sometimes jarred—how her words and his words never quite tasted the same on the tongue.

“Such is the meaning that I attach to the word ‘correspond’ in this application, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious. “Good. All goes well, time works on, and at this next Christmas-time it will become necessary, as a matter of form, to give the exemplary lady in the corner window—business notice of your departure in the ensuing half-year. Your relations with her are far more than business relations, no doubt; but a residue of business remains in them, and business is business ever. I am a particularly Angular man,” he proceeded, as if it had suddenly occurred to him, “and I am not used to give anything away. If, for these two reasons, some competent Proxy would give you away, I should take it very kindly.”

Rosa, eyes on the ground, intimated that a substitute might surely be found if required.

“Surely, surely,” said Mr. Grewgious. “For instance, the gentleman who teaches Dancing here—he would know how to do it with graceful propriety. He would advance and retire in a manner satisfactory to the feelings of the officiating clergyman, and of yourself, and the bridegroom, and all parties concerned. I am—I am a particularly Angular man,” he repeated, as if forcing the confession out at last, “and should only blunder.”

Rosa sat still. The ceremony he spoke of—advancing, retiring, being “given away”—felt to her less like a day on the calendar than like a noise heard from far off, a bell not yet within earshot but already feared.

“Memorandum, ‘Will.’ Now, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious, consulting his notes, drawing a line through “Marriage,” and taking a paper from his pocket, “although I have before possessed you with the contents of your father’s will, I think it right at this time to leave a certified copy of it in your hands. And although Mr. Edwin is also aware of its contents, I think it right at this time likewise to place a certified copy of it in Mr. Jasper’s hand—”

“Not in his own!” Rosa broke in, looking up quickly. “Cannot the copy go to Eddy himself?”

“Why yes, my dear, if you particularly wish it; but I spoke of Mr. Jasper as being his trustee.”

“I do particularly wish it, if you please,” said Rosa, hurried and earnest. “I don’t like Mr. Jasper to come between us, in any way.”

“It is natural, I suppose,” said Mr. Grewgious, “that your young husband should be all in all. Yes. You observe that I say, I suppose. The fact is, I am a particularly Unnatural man, and I don’t know from my own knowledge.”

Rosa stared at him a little, unsure whether he mocked her or himself.

“I mean,” he explained, without any change of tone, “that young ways were never my ways. I was the only offspring of parents far advanced in life, and I half believe I was born advanced in life myself. No personality is intended towards the name you will so soon change, when I remark that while the general growth of people seem to have come into existence, buds, I seem to have come into existence a chip. I was a chip—and a very dry one—when I first became aware of myself. Respecting the other certified copy, your wish shall be complied with.

“Respecting your inheritance, I think you know all. It is an annuity of two hundred and fifty pounds. The savings upon that annuity, and some other items to your credit, all duly carried to account, with vouchers, will place you in possession of a lump sum of money rather exceeding seventeen hundred pounds. I am empowered to advance the cost of your preparations for your marriage out of that fund. All is told.”

Rosa took the paper with a prettily knitted brow, but did not open it. The language of law lay on pages like food gone cold: nourishing, perhaps, but hard to swallow. She held it, instead, as if the paper itself could answer plain questions.

“Will you please tell me,” she said, “whether I am right in what I am going to say? I can understand what you tell me so very much better than what I read in law-writings. My poor papa and Eddy’s father made their agreement together, as very dear and firm and fast friends, in order that we, too, might be very dear and firm and fast friends after them?”

“Just so.”

“For the lasting good of both of us, and the lasting happiness of both of us?”

“Just so.”

“That we might be to one another even much more than they had been to one another?”

“Just so.”

“It was not bound upon Eddy, and it was not bound upon me, by any forfeit, in case—”

“Don’t be agitated, my dear.” His dry voice softened, not in sound but in intention. “In the case that it brings tears into your affectionate eyes even to picture to yourself—in the case of your not marrying one another—no, no forfeiture on either side. You would then have been my ward until you were of age. No worse would have befallen you. Bad enough perhaps!”

“And Eddy?”

“He would have come into his partnership derived from his father, and into its arrears to his credit (if any), on attaining his majority, just as now.”

Rosa, perplexed, bit the corner of her attested copy without knowing she did it. Her head tilted as if she listened to something under the floorboards. She looked down and, with her foot, smoothed the carpet as she smoothed her thoughts—back and forth, back and forth, as if to press a wrinkle out of her own mind.

“In short,” said Mr. Grewgious, “this betrothal is a wish, a sentiment, a friendly project, tenderly expressed on both sides. That it was strongly felt, and that there was a lively hope that it would prosper, there can be no doubt. When you were both children, you began to be accustomed to it, and it has prospered. But circumstances alter cases; and I made this visit to-day, partly, indeed principally, to discharge myself of the duty of telling you, my dear, that two young people can only be betrothed in marriage—except as a matter of convenience, and therefore mockery and misery—of their own free will, their own attachment, and their own assurance (it may or it may not prove a mistaken one, but we must take our chance of that) that they are suited to each other, and will make each other happy.

“Is it to be supposed, for example, that if either of your fathers were living now, and had any mistrust on that subject, his mind would not be changed by the change of circumstances involved in the change of your years? Untenable, unreasonable, inconclusive, and preposterous!”

He delivered even this—meant for tenderness and freedom—as if reciting a lesson, face and manner offering no help beyond the words themselves. Yet the words, once spoken, hung in the room like a window unlatched.

“I have now, my dear,” he added, drawing his pencil line through “Will,” “discharged myself of what is doubtless a formal duty in this case, but still a duty in such a case. Memorandum, ‘Wishes.’ My dear, is there any wish of yours that I can further?”

Rosa shook her head, but with a slight, almost pleading hesitation, as if there were indeed a wish and she could not find the language for it.

“Is there any instruction that I can take from you with reference to your affairs?”

“I—I should like to settle them with Eddy first, if you please,” she said, plaiting and unplaiting the crease in her dress.

“Surely, surely,” returned Mr. Grewgious. “You two should be of one mind in all things. Is the young gentleman expected shortly?”

“He has gone away only this morning. He will be back at Christmas.”

“Nothing could happen better. You will, on his return at Christmas, arrange all matters of detail with him; you will then communicate with me; and I will discharge myself—as a mere business acquaintance—of my business responsibilities towards the accomplished lady in the corner window. They will accrue at that season.” He blurred his pencil over the page again. “Memorandum, ‘Leave.’ Yes. I will now, my dear, take my leave.”

He jerked himself out of his chair in his awkward way, as if his joints did not entirely trust one another; and Rosa rose too, suddenly conscious of how near Christmas sounded when it was spoken aloud.

“Could I,” she asked—her voice small but urgent—“could I ask you, most kindly, to come to me at Christmas, if I had anything particular to say to you?”

People will tell you, with a certainty that sounds like bell-metal, that women divine men’s characters by instinct. They do; and in Cloisterham, where everything—mortar, moss, and manners—seems to have stood long enough to take a permanent set, that instinct often hardens into a judgment which no after-proof can soften. When once delivered, it is not revised; it is defended. Contradiction does not correct it; contradiction irritates it. And the more a woman has put herself—her pride, her care, her household peace—into that judgment, the more it resembles testimony from a witness who cannot help being interested in the verdict.

“Now, don’t you think, Ma dear,” said the Minor Canon to his mother, one day, as she sat knitting in his little book-room, “that you are rather hard on Mr. Neville?”

“No, I do _not_, Sept,” returned the old lady, with the calm emphasis of a person who has never been wrong in her life without immediately forgetting the circumstance.

“Let us discuss it, Ma.”

“I have no objection to discuss it, Sept. I trust, my dear, I am always open to discussion.” Her cap gave a faint tremor—as if the threads of it had remembered a hundred discussions and the unalterable endings of them.

“Very good, Ma. There is nothing like being open to discussion.”

“I hope not, my dear,” said the old lady; and somehow, though her words invited debate, her knitting needles clicked like shutters closing.

“Well. Mr. Neville, on that unfortunate occasion, commits himself under provocation.”

“And under mulled wine,” added the old lady, without looking up.

“I must admit the wine. Though I believe the two young men were much alike in that regard.”

“I don’t,” said the old lady.

“Why not, Ma?”

“Because I _don’t_,” returned she, knitting steadily. “Still, I am quite open to discussion.”

“But, my dear Ma, I cannot see how we are to discuss, if you take that line.”

“Blame Mr. Neville for it, Sept, and not me,” said Mrs. Crisparkle, with a stately severity that made even the arm-chair appear to sit straighter.

“My dear Ma! why Mr. Neville?”

“Because,” said she, retreating upon the fortress of first principles, “he came home intoxicated, and did great discredit to this house, and showed great disrespect to this family.”

“That is not to be denied, Ma. He was then, and he is now, very sorry for it.”

“But for Mr. Jasper’s well-bred consideration in coming up to me, next day, after service, in the Nave itself, with his gown still on, and expressing his hope that I had not been greatly alarmed or had my rest violently broken, I believe I might never have heard of that disgraceful transaction,” said the old lady, as though the recollection were kept, like lavender, between the leaves of a Bible.

“To be candid, Ma, I think I should have kept it from you if I could: though I had not decidedly made up my mind. I was following Jasper out, to confer with him on the subject, and to consider the expediency of his and my jointly hushing the thing up on all accounts, when I found him speaking to you. Then it was too late.”

“Too late, indeed, Sept. He was still as pale as gentlemanly ashes at what had taken place in his rooms overnight.”

“If I _had_ kept it from you, Ma, you may be sure it would have been for your peace and quiet, and for the good of the young men, and in my best discharge of my duty according to my lights.”

The old lady put down her knitting, crossed the room, and kissed him—an instant softening that never extended as far as her opinions.

“Of course, my dear Sept, I am sure of that.”

“However,” said Mr. Crisparkle, rubbing his ear with the air of a man who rubs away an awkward fact and finds it still there, “it became the town-talk, and passed out of my power.”

“And I said then, Sept,” returned the old lady, taking up her needles again as if resuming a legal document, “that I thought ill of Mr. Neville. And I say now, that I think ill of Mr. Neville. And I said then, and I say now, that I hope Mr. Neville may come to good, but I don’t believe he will.” The cap vibrated in decisive accompaniment.

“I am sorry to hear you say so, Ma—”

“I am sorry to say so, my dear,” she interposed, “but I can’t help it.”

“—For it is undeniable that Mr. Neville is exceedingly industrious and attentive, and that he improves apace, and that he has—I hope I may say—an attachment to me.”

“There is no merit in the last article, my dear,” said the old lady, quickly. “And if he says there is, I think the worse of him for the boast.”

“But, my dear Ma, he never said there was.”

“Perhaps not,” returned she; “still, I don’t see that it greatly signifies.”

There was no impatience in the Minor Canon’s pleasant look at his mother—pretty old piece of china that she was, bright-glazed, finely painted, and not a thing to argue with too closely for fear of a crack—yet there was a quiet humor in his sense of her unyielding nature.

“Besides, Sept,” she pursued, “ask yourself what he would be without his sister. You know what an influence she has over him; you know what a capacity she has; you know that whatever he reads with you, he reads with her. Give her her fair share of your praise, and how much do you leave for him?”

At that, Mr. Crisparkle fell into one of his mild reveries; and as he did, the house seemed to settle itself around him in its accustomed music—the soft scratch of needles, the far-off Cathedral note that was never wholly absent, the domestic hush between the great peals: an acoustic order as regular as prayer, and far kinder to the mind than the modern world’s grinding clatter would ever be. He saw again, as clearly as if the pages were before him, the brother and sister bending together over one of his old college books; and he heard—so to speak—their silence, that attentive silence in which another mind is present though no word passes. He saw them, too, in the rimy mornings when he made those sharpening pilgrimages to Cloisterham Weir; and in the sombre evenings when he faced the wind at sunset on his favourite beetling fragment of monastery ruin, and watched them move below along the margin of the river, the town fires already lighting and blearing the water, so that the landscape looked bleaker for its own reflections. He remembered how, by degrees, he had found himself teaching two, adapting an explanation not only to the mind he met daily, but to the mind he approached through it. He recalled the Nuns’ House gossip: that Helena—whom he had first mistrusted as proud and fierce—submitted herself, strangely enough, to the fairy-bride (as he called the slight young lady who had become her friend), and learned from her what she knew. He thought of that picturesque alliance between two so unlike in look and manner. And he marvelled—perhaps most of all—how few weeks these things had been, and yet how they had already threaded themselves into the routine of his life as surely as the Cathedral hours.

Whenever the Reverend Septimus fell a-musing, his good mother took it as a sure sign that he “wanted support.” Support, in her philosophy, did not come in the form of argument; it came in a glass. She rose and went, with the importance of a priestess entering a shrine, to the dining-room closet.

It was a closet worthy of Cloisterham and Minor Canon Corner: not a blunt cupboard that flung all its secrets open at once, but a contrivance of gradual revelations, as ceremonious as a service. Above it, Handel—flowing wig, knowing eyes—looked down as if he had composed, in his head, a fugue out of all the sweet and sharp and cordial notes within. The lock sat in mid-air where two perpendicular slides met; one fell down, one pushed up, and the mysteries advanced by degrees, like verses.

Down came the upper slide: shelves deep as small caverns showed pickle-jars, jam-pots, tin canisters, spice-boxes, and agreeably outlandish blue-and-white vessels where preserved tamarinds and ginger lay in their syrup as if embalmed against forgetfulness. Every benevolent inmate had his name painted on his stomach. The pickles, in rich brown double-breasted coats with yellow or drab continuations, announced themselves in printed capitals as WALNUT, GHERKIN, ONION, CABBAGE, CAULIFLOWER, MIXED—portly, military fellows, sharp to the tongue and good for the spirits. The jams—less masculine, and as if wearing curlpapers—declared in a soft feminine hand that they were Raspberry, Gooseberry, Apricot, Plum, Damson, Apple, Peach: the very syllables seeming to carry the scent of summer hedges into the winter room.

Then the lower slide ascended and disclosed oranges, attended by a mighty japanned sugar-box—ready to correct any acerbity, as Cloisterham corrected strong natures with its own slow sweetness. Home-made biscuits waited at court; a goodly fragment of plum-cake sat in state; and slender ladies’ fingers lay ready to be dipped into sweet wine and kissed. Lowest of all, in a compact leaden vault, the Constantia and cordials were stored—Seville Orange, Lemon, Almond, Caraway-seed—each bottle holding, like a remembered phrase in a dying dialect, something that could not be translated into mere “sweet” or “strong” but must be tasted to be known. The whole closet seemed to have been hummed through for ages by Cathedral bell and organ, until those venerable bees had made sublimated honey of everything it contained. And it was always observed that whoever dipped head, shoulders, and elbows among its deep shelves came forth mellow-faced, as if saccharine transfigured.

Mr. Crisparkle took his glass of Constantia with an excellent grace—warm, golden, faintly scented with sun and old grapes—and ate a biscuit that crumbled with the honest dryness of home baking, and was softened on the tongue by the wine. In his mother’s system, that was support.

So supported, he went about the remaining duties of the day. The orderly hours brought round Vesper Service and twilight. The Cathedral, cold as stone remembers cold, sent him out after service into a brisk trot, his breath smoking in the dusk. The trot ended in a charge at his favourite fragment of ruin, to be carried by storm without a pause for breath; and he carried it in masterly fashion, climbing as if the old stones had been built for his foot.

Not even winded then, he stood looking down upon the river. Cloisterham lay near enough to the sea for the tide to throw up seaweed often; an unusual quantity had come in with the last tide, tangled and dark on the banks. The water was confused; gulls dipped and flapped noisily, their cries sharp as knives in the air; beyond the brown-sailed barges that were turning black, an angry light out seaward foreshadowed a stormy night. He listened to that unresting outer sound—the sea’s hoarse breath coming up the river—and contrasted it, as he had been doing all day, with the quiet harbour of Minor Canon Corner, where even the Cathedral’s great voice was softened by distance into something that steadied rather than shook the mind. How much of a man’s temper, he thought, was made—or marred—by the sounds he lived under! And how little the busy modern world understood the cruelty of perpetual clang and din, calling it progress and selling it as pleasure.

As he stood so, Helena and Neville Landless passed below him along the river-walk. Having had them in his thoughts all day, he climbed down at once to speak to them together. The footing was rough in the uncertain light for any tread save that of a good climber; but the Minor Canon was a good climber as most men are not, and he stood beside them before many would have been half-way.

“A wild evening, Miss Landless! Do you not find your usual walk with your brother too exposed and cold for the time of year? Or at all events, when the sun is down and the weather is driving in from the sea?”

Helena thought not. It was their favourite walk. It was very retired.

“It is very retired,” assented Mr. Crisparkle, taking hold of his opportunity at once as they walked on together. “It is a place of all others where one can speak without interruption, as I wish to do. Mr. Neville, I believe you tell your sister everything that passes between us?”

“Everything, sir.”

“Consequently,” said Mr. Crisparkle, looking rather to Helena than to Neville, “your sister is aware that I have repeatedly urged you to make some kind of apology for that unfortunate occurrence which befell on the night of your arrival here.”

“Yes,” Helena answered, and the word carried her brother’s loyalty in it.

“I call it unfortunate, Miss Helena,” resumed Mr. Crisparkle, “forasmuch as it certainly has engendered a prejudice against Neville. There is a notion about that he is a dangerously passionate fellow, of an uncontrollable and furious temper: he is really avoided as such.”

“I have no doubt he is, poor fellow,” said Helena, with proud compassion for her brother, as though she would have taken the prejudice into her own breast and borne it for him. “I should be quite sure of it, from your saying so; but what you tell me is confirmed by suppressed hints and references that I meet with every day.”

“Now,” Mr. Crisparkle resumed, mild but firm, “is not this to be regretted, and ought it not to be amended? These are early days of Neville’s in Cloisterham, and I have no fear of his outliving such a prejudice and proving himself to have been misunderstood. But how much wiser to take action at once than to trust to uncertain time! Besides, apart from its being politic, it is right. For there can be no question that Neville was wrong.”

“He was provoked,” Helena submitted, a little quickening in her voice.

“He was the assailant,” Mr. Crisparkle submitted, quietly.

They walked on in silence; and the soundscape of the place seemed to argue too—the river’s slap, the gulls’ crying, the far organ-note of the town’s life, all mingling like a reminder that tempers, once loosed, spread and echo. At length Helena lifted her eyes to his face and said, almost reproachfully:

“O Mr. Crisparkle, would you have Neville throw himself at young Drood’s feet, or at Mr. Jasper’s, who maligns him every day? In your heart you cannot mean it. From your heart you could not do it, if his case were yours.”

“I have represented to Mr. Crisparkle, Helena,” said Neville, glancing with deference towards his tutor, “that if I could do it from my heart, I would. But I cannot, and I revolt from the pretence. You forget, however, that to put the case to Mr. Crisparkle as his own, is to suppose to have done what I did.”

“I ask his pardon,” said Helena at once.

“You see,” remarked Mr. Crisparkle, again laying hold of his opportunity, delicately, “you both instinctively acknowledge that Neville did wrong. Then why stop short, and not otherwise acknowledge it?”

“Is there no difference,” asked Helena, faltering a little, “between submission to a generous spirit, and submission to a base or trivial one?”

Before the Minor Canon could quite shape his answer to that nice distinction, Neville struck in, his voice roughened by the very restraint he tried to keep.

“Help me to clear myself with Mr. Crisparkle, Helena. Help me to convince him that I cannot be the first to make concessions without mockery and falsehood. My nature must be changed before I can do so, and it is not changed. I am sensible of inexpressible affront, and deliberate aggravation of inexpressible affront, and I am angry. The plain truth is, I am still as angry when I recall that night as I was that night.”

“Neville,” hinted the Minor Canon, steady-faced, “you have repeated that former action of your hands, which I so much dislike.”

“I am sorry for it, sir, but it was involuntary. I confessed that I was still as angry.”

“And I confess,” said Mr. Crisparkle, “that I hoped for better things.”

“I am sorry to disappoint you, sir, but it would be far worse to deceive you, and I should deceive you grossly if I pretended that you had softened me in this respect. The time may come when your powerful influence will do even that with the difficult pupil whose antecedents you know; but it has not come yet. Is this so, and in spite of my struggles against myself, Helena?”

Helena’s dark eyes were watching what every word did to Mr. Crisparkle’s face; and she answered him—not her brother—quietly: “It is so.” Then, after a brief pause, she met the slightest inquiry in Neville’s eyes with the slightest affirmative bend of her head, and Neville went on, as if that small assent gave him courage.

“I have never yet had the courage to say to you, sir, what in full openness I ought to have said when you first talked with me on this subject. It is not easy to say, and I have been withheld by a fear of its seeming ridiculous, which is very strong upon me down to this last moment, and might, but for my sister, prevent my being quite open with you even now.—I admire Miss Bud, sir, so very much, that I cannot bear her being treated with conceit or indifference; and even if I did not feel that I had an injury against young Drood on my own account, I should feel that I had an injury against him on hers.”

Mr. Crisparkle, utterly amazed, looked to Helena for corroboration. He found it in her expressive face—full corroboration, and a plea besides, as if she begged him to take hold of the matter with both hands before it ran away into disaster.

“The young lady of whom you speak is, as you know, Mr. Neville, shortly to be married,” said Mr. Crisparkle, gravely. “Therefore your admiration, if it be of that special nature which you seem to indicate, is outrageously misplaced. Moreover, it is monstrous that you should take upon yourself to be the young lady’s champion against her chosen husband. Besides, you have seen them only once. The young lady has become your sister’s friend; and I wonder that your sister, even on her behalf, has not checked you in this irrational and culpable fancy.”

“She has tried, sir, but uselessly. Husband or no husband, that fellow is incapable of the feeling with which I am inspired towards the beautiful young creature whom he treats like a doll. I say he is as incapable of it, as he is unworthy of her. I say she is sacrificed in being bestowed upon him. I say that I love her, and despise and hate him!”

He spoke with such a flushed face and such a violent gesture that Helena crossed at once to his side, caught his arm, and remonstrated, “Neville, Neville!” as if she would physically hold back the very word.

Thus recalled to himself, he became sensible in a moment that he had lost the guard he had set upon his passionate tendency. He covered his face with his hand, repentant and wretched, as if ashamed to have let his heart show its rawness in the air.

Mr. Crisparkle watched him attentively, and at the same time thought how to proceed. They walked on some paces in silence; the wind worried the reeds; the gulls quarrelled overhead; and the rising storm seemed to lend Neville’s confession a dreadful fitness—as though the outer world were always too ready to translate a man’s inner tumult into weather.

Then Mr. Crisparkle spoke.

“Mr. Neville, Mr. Neville, I am sorely grieved to see in you more traces of a character as sullen, angry, and wild, as the night now closing in. They are of too serious an aspect to leave me the resource of treating the infatuation you have disclosed as undeserving serious consideration. I give it very serious consideration, and I speak to you accordingly. This feud between you and young Drood must not go on. I cannot permit it to go on any longer, knowing what I now know from you, and you living under my roof. Whatever prejudiced and unauthorised constructions your blind and envious wrath may put upon his character, it is a frank, good-natured character. I know I can trust to it for that.

“Now, pray observe what I am about to say. On reflection, and on your sister’s representation, I am willing to admit that, in making peace with young Drood, you have a right to be met half-way. I will engage that you shall be, and even that young Drood shall make the first advance. This condition fulfilled, you will pledge me the honour of a Christian gentleman that the quarrel is for ever at an end on your side. What may be in your heart when you give him your hand can only be known to the Searcher of all hearts; but it will never go well with you if there be any treachery there.

“So far, as to that. Next, as to what I must again speak of as your infatuation. I understand it to have been confided to me, and to be known to no other person save your sister and yourself. Do I understand aright?”

Helena answered in a low voice: “It is only known to us three who are here together.”

“It is not at all known to the young lady, your friend?”

“On my soul, no!” said Neville, with a quick misery, as if the notion scorched him.

“I require you, then, to give me your similar and solemn pledge, Mr. Neville, that it shall remain the secret it is, and that you will take no other action whatsoever upon it than endeavouring—most earnestly—to erase it from your mind. I will not tell you that it will soon pass; I will not tell you that it is the fancy of the moment; I will not tell you that such caprices have their rise and fall among the young and ardent every hour. I will leave you undisturbed in the belief that it has few parallels or none, that it will abide with you a long time, and that it will be very difficult to conquer. So much the more weight shall I attach to the pledge I require from you, when it is unreservedly given.”

The young man tried twice or thrice to speak, and could not. The words, like a dish too hot, would not be taken into the mouth.

“Let me leave you with your sister, whom it is time you took home,” said Mr. Crisparkle. “You will find me alone in my room by-and-by.”

“Pray do not leave us yet,” Helena implored him. “Another minute.”

“I should not,” said Neville, his hand still on his face, “have needed so much as another minute, if you had been less patient with me, Mr. Crisparkle, less considerate of me, and less unpretendingly good and true. O, if in my childhood I had known such a guide!”

“Follow your guide now, Neville,” murmured Helena, “and follow him to Heaven!”

There was something in her tone—so fervent, so grateful, so ready to sanctify the human help she had found—that broke the Minor Canon’s voice, or it would have gently put her right. As it was, he only laid a finger on his lips and looked towards her brother.

“To say that I give both pledges, Mr. Crisparkle, out of my innermost heart, and to say that there is no treachery in it, is to say nothing!” returned Neville, greatly moved. “I beg your forgiveness for my miserable lapse into a burst of passion.”

“Not mine, Neville, not mine. You know with whom forgiveness lies, as the highest attribute conceivable. Miss Helena, you and your brother are twin children. You came into this world with the same dispositions, and you passed your younger days together surrounded by the same adverse circumstances. What you have overcome in yourself, can you not overcome in him? You see the rock that lies in his course. Who but you can keep him clear of it?”

“Who but you, sir?” replied Helena. “What is my influence, or my weak wisdom, compared with yours!”

“You have the wisdom of Love,” returned the Minor Canon, “and it was the highest wisdom ever known upon this earth, remember. As to mine—but the less said of that commonplace commodity the better. Good night!”

She took the hand he offered her and gratefully, almost reverently, raised it to her lips.

“Tut!” said the Minor Canon softly, as if embarrassed by any ceremony that made a commodity of goodness. “I am much overpaid!” And he turned away.

Retracing his steps towards the Cathedral Close, he tried, as he went along in the dark, to think out the best means of bringing to pass what he had promised and what must somehow be done. The wind was rising; the sea’s voice was louder up the river; and through it all the Cathedral’s presence held—its great mass, its measured sounds—like a reminder that there were older harmonies than anger. “I shall probably be asked to marry them,” he reflected, “and I would they were married and gone! But this presses first.”

He debated chiefly whether he should write to young Drood or whether he should speak to Jasper. The consciousness of being popular with the whole Cathedral establishment inclined him to the latter course; and the well-timed sight of the lighted gatehouse decided him.

“I will strike while the iron is hot,” he said, and turned to go in and see Jasper now.

Behind the oldest stretch of Holborn, where a few gabled houses still leaned forward like tired witnesses—still peering, as if for the vanished Bourne whose name they kept after its water had gone—there opened, by a narrow turning, a pocket of quiet called Staple Inn. You stepped into it as into a pantry off a roaring kitchen: the street’s iron clatter dulled at once, as if someone had laid soft cloth over your ears. Even the air changed its temper. A few smoky sparrows, lodged in a few smoky trees, chirped with a thin, stubborn cheerfulness that pretended at hedgerows and stubble-fields; and the little squares of mould and gravel beneath them were their whole countryside, scratched and prized with a courage that made the small place feel almost rural.

Yet it was a legal nook too, with its little Hall and its little lantern in the roof—one of those architectural trifles that look like they were built to delay business, and paid for by people who did not suspect they were paying.

In years when provincial England could take mortal offence at the mere rumour of a railway line in the distance, no tall modern structure had yet risen to tower over Staple Inn. The westering sun could once have reached it cleanly; the south-west wind could once have wandered in without being sifted through brick and smoke. But on a December afternoon, towards six o’clock, neither sun nor wind did it any service. Fog had found its way into every angle of the place and sat there, thick and taciturn; candles in the occupied windows shone as if behind greasy paper, their light blurred to a jaundiced smear.

One set of chambers, in a corner house of the inner quadrangle, showed above its ill-favoured doorway an inscription, black on white, with the air of a cipher:

P J T 1747

In that room sat Mr. Grewgious, writing by the fire, and never troubling himself much about what the letters meant—except on odd occasions, when he glanced up and let his mind toy with “Perhaps John Thomas,” or “Perhaps Joe Tyler,” as a man idly tastes a word he does not mean to swallow.

What had Mr. Grewgious been in youth—ambitious or thwarted? His face would not tell. He had been bred to the Bar and aimed at chamber work: deeds, settlements, conveyancing—the dry craft of making property change hands without seeming to move. But Conveyancing and he had never properly met; it was as though he had courted a lady who would not even come to the door. They had separated by tacit consent—if separation can be said where there was no union. Then Arbitration, blown to him on some lucky wind, found in him an obstinate passion for right; after that a Receivership—fatter, and traceable to more ordinary sources—settled into his pocket. Thus by chance he found his groove, and sank into it with relief. He became Receiver and Agent to two rich estates, passed their law-business downstairs to solicitors worth paying, and put away whatever spark might once have been called ambition. If he had ever struck a light, he had long since snuffed it.

His room was a neat anatomy of trust: account-books, files of correspondence, strong boxes—each in its place with the severe tenderness of a man who could not bear that, after his death, a single figure should limp or a single fact look doubtful. The very thought of dying suddenly and leaving a matter half-verified would have turned him rigid on the spot. Fidelity was his circulating blood. It did not run quick or warm; but it ran clean.

There was no luxury. Comfort, yes—dry walls, a good fire, and a snug hearth, though the furniture was faded as an old coat. The room’s private life gathered itself about the fire: an easy-chair in the corner, and a small old-fashioned round table which, after business hours, was drawn out upon the rug from a corner where it stood turned up on its edge like a mahogany shield. Behind that defensive posture was a closet that usually held something good to drink. Beyond was the clerk’s room; Mr. Grewgious slept across the common stair; and he had cellarage below that stair which was not empty. Most days in the year he crossed to a hotel in Furnival’s Inn for dinner, and returned, to make the best of these austerities until morning brought business back again, and P. J. T., date seventeen-forty-seven, resumed its quiet rule.

As Mr. Grewgious wrote, his clerk wrote too, at a fire of his own in the outer room. This clerk was named Bazzard: a pale, puffy-faced, dark-haired man of thirty, with large dark eyes that seemed to have forgotten how to shine, and a dissatisfied, doughy complexion that suggested it wanted sending to the baker’s. He was mysterious; and, more mysterious still, he had some strange hold upon Mr. Grewgious. Like a Familiar called up by spell and never dismissed, he clung to his master’s stool when any ordinary comfort would have kicked him away. His tangled locks and his general air belonged to some gloomy shade where lies ripen luxuriantly; and yet Mr. Grewgious treated him with an unaccountable consideration.

Toward the end of the afternoon, as Mr. Grewgious arranged his papers for the night, Bazzard entered.

“Now, Bazzard,” said Mr. Grewgious, looking up across the twin flames of his office candles, “what is in the wind besides fog?”

“Mr. Drood,” said Bazzard.

“What of him?”

“Has called,” said Bazzard.

“You might have shown him in.”

“I am doing it,” returned Bazzard, and withdrew with a gloom that was almost resentful of obedience.

The visitor came in at once.

“Dear me!” said Mr. Grewgious, peering round the candles. “I thought you had called and merely left your name and gone. How do you do, Mr. Edwin? Dear me, you’re choking!”

“It’s this fog,” Edwin Drood answered, rubbing at his eyes. “It makes them smart like Cayenne pepper.”

“Is it really so bad as that? Pray undo your wrappers. It’s fortunate I have so good a fire; but Mr. Bazzard has taken care of me.”

From the doorway came Bazzard’s voice, sour as cold tea. “No I haven’t.”

“Ah! then it follows,” said Mr. Grewgious, “that I must have taken care of myself without observing it. Pray be seated in my chair. No. I beg! Coming out of such an atmosphere, in my chair.”

Edwin, smiling, yielded and sank into the easy-chair in the corner. The fog he carried in—clinging to coat and neck-shawl like damp flour to a cook’s sleeve—began at once to be licked away by the fire, which took it with a greedy hiss.

“I look,” Edwin said, “as if I had come to stop.”

“By the bye,” cried Mr. Grewgious, as if a thought had leapt out of the coals, “excuse my interrupting you; do stop. The fog may clear in an hour or two. We can have dinner in from just across Holborn. You had better take your Cayenne pepper here than outside; pray stop and dine.”

“You are very kind,” Edwin said, glancing around the bare, orderly room with the curious relish a young man has for an unaccustomed domestic arrangement—like being invited to a strange, makeshift feast.

“Not at all,” returned Mr. Grewgious. “You are very kind to join issue with a bachelor in chambers, and take pot-luck. And I’ll ask”—he dropped his voice, and his eye twinkled with a bright, secret prudence—“I’ll ask Bazzard. He mightn’t like it else.—Bazzard!”

Bazzard reappeared, as if summoned from behind a curtain of discontent.

“Dine presently with Mr. Drood and me.”

“If I am ordered to dine, of course I will, sir,” said Bazzard, with the air of a man required to eat punishment.

“Save the man!” cried Mr. Grewgious. “You’re not ordered; you’re invited.”

“Thank you, sir,” said Bazzard. “In that case I don’t care if I do.”

“That’s arranged. And perhaps you wouldn’t mind stepping over to the hotel in Furnival’s, and asking them to send in materials for laying the cloth. For dinner we’ll have a tureen of the hottest and strongest soup available, and we’ll have the best made-dish that can be recommended, and we’ll have a joint (such as a haunch of mutton), and we’ll have a goose, or a turkey, or any little stuffed thing of that sort that may happen to be in the bill of fare—in short, we’ll have whatever there is on hand.”

He issued these instructions with the same uninflected manner as if he were reading an inventory, not ordering food. Yet the list itself conjured the warmth of a kitchen against a hostile night: soup that would bite back at the fog; a made-dish—some careful composition of minced meat, gravy, herbs, and perhaps a sharp edge of wine; a solid joint with fat that would melt glossy under the knife; and a stuffed bird, its belly packed with bread-crumbs, sage, onion, and pepper, steaming like a domestic hearth made edible.

Bazzard drew out the round table and withdrew to execute the foraging.

“I was a little delicate, you see,” said Mr. Grewgious, lowering his voice when they were alone again, “about employing him in the commissariat department. Because he mightn’t like it.”

“He seems to have his own way, sir,” Edwin observed.

“His own way?” Mr. Grewgious repeated, in genuine surprise. “O dear no! Poor fellow, you quite mistake him. If he had his own way, he wouldn’t be here.”

Edwin wondered—where then would he be? But he kept the thought to himself, for Mr. Grewgious had moved to the hearth, set his shoulder-blades against the chimneypiece, and gathered his skirts as if he were bracing himself to talk at ease.

“I take it,” said Mr. Grewgious, “without having the gift of prophecy, that you have done me the favour of looking in to mention that you are going down yonder—where I can tell you, you are expected—and to offer to execute any little commission from me to my charming ward, and perhaps to sharpen me up a bit in any proceedings? Eh, Mr. Edwin?”

“I called, sir, before going down, as an act of attention.”

“Of attention!” repeated Mr. Grewgious. “Ah! of course, not of impatience?”

“Impatience, sir?”

Mr. Grewgious had meant to be arch. The intention, however, did not survive long in his face. It collapsed before Edwin’s composed manner, leaving nothing but the heat of the fire; and he shifted, rubbing himself as if he had stood too close and scorched the joke.

“I have lately been down yonder,” said Mr. Grewgious, rearranging his skirts. “And that was what I referred to when I said I could tell you you are expected.”

“Indeed, sir! Yes; I knew that Pussy was looking out for me.”

“Do you keep a cat down there?” asked Mr. Grewgious.

Edwin coloured. “I call Rosa Pussy.”

“O, really,” said Mr. Grewgious, smoothing down his head, as if he were ironing a crease out of himself. “That’s very affable.”

Edwin glanced at him, uncertain whether the word was rebuke or neutrality. It might as well have been a glance at a clock-face: the hands told the time, and nothing else.

“A pet name, sir,” Edwin explained, as if defending a seasoning that another palate did not understand.

“Umps,” said Mr. Grewgious, nodding—but with such a compromise between assent and dissent that Edwin felt oddly checked.

“Did R—” Edwin began, trying to recover his footing.

“PRosa?” Mr. Grewgious repeated.

“I was going to say Pussy, and changed my mind,” Edwin admitted. “Did she tell you anything about the Landlesses?”

“No,” said Mr. Grewgious. “What is the Landlesses? An estate? A villa? A farm?”

“A brother and sister. The sister is at the Nuns’ House, and has become a great friend of P—”

“PRosa’s,” struck in Mr. Grewgious, with his fixed face.

“She is a strikingly handsome girl, sir,” Edwin went on, “and I thought she might have been described to you, or presented to you perhaps?”

“Neither,” said Mr. Grewgious. “But here is Bazzard.”

Bazzard returned, ushering in two waiters and a fresh gust of fog that made the fire roar anew. One waiter was immovable: he brought nothing but authority and complaint. The other was flying: he carried everything on his shoulders, quick as a kitchen-boy with too many plates and not enough breath. The flying waiter whisked the cloth over the table and set it true with swift, practised hands; the immovable waiter found fault with the straightness. The flying waiter then polished the glasses to a bright transparency; the immovable waiter held them up and looked through them, as if searching for defects in the world.

Then began the flights across Holborn: out for soup, back with a tureen that breathed a peppery, meaty heat into the room; out again for the made-dish; back with a covered entrée sending up a rich scent of browned crust and gravy; out again for the joint and the poultry; back with weight and steam and the promise of fat rendered to sweetness. And between those errands, more frantic dashes for forgotten requisites—salt, mustard, bread, perhaps a relish—because each time it was “discovered” that the immovable waiter had forgotten everything at once. No matter how the flying waiter cleft the foggy air, he was reproached on every return for bringing fog with him and being out of breath, as if he ought to have left his lungs somewhere on the threshold.

When the meal was done and the flying waiter stood blown and damp at the edges like pastry that has met too much steam, the immovable waiter gathered the tablecloth under his arm with a grand air, watched the other set the clean glasses about, and then directed towards Mr. Grewgious a final look which said, as plainly as a stamped seal: Understand that the reward is mine, and Nil the claim of this slave. So, pushing the flying waiter before him, he marched out.

It was a miniature portrait—highly finished—of offices where the man who does nothing claims all credit and the man who does all is blamed for the weather. If it had been painted on ivory, it might have hung, edifyingly, in a national gallery of administrative vices.

If fog had been the cause of the feast, it served also as its sauce. Outside, the clerks in the Inn-yard could be heard sneezing and wheezing, stamping their feet on gravel in an effort to keep themselves alive; the sound sharpened the relish of warmth within. To shiver and bid the unfortunate flying waiter shut the door before he had properly opened it gave a spice beyond any bottled condiment. Even the rhythm of the door—tray first, leg after, the leg lingering like the last reluctant note in a phrase—became part of the evening’s texture: a domestic theatre of thresholds, in which cold and noise were the villains.

Mr. Grewgious went below to the cellar and brought up bottles whose colours alone seemed like remembered seasons: ruby, straw, and gold. They were wines ripened long ago in lands where air does not coagulate into fog, and then left slumbering in the dark. Once roused, they tingled as if impatient with their own age, pushing against their corks to help the corkscrew, and when freed they sprang out gaily, like prisoners turning riot into holiday. If P. J. T. in seventeen-forty-seven drank such wine—or any year of his period—then P. J. T. must indeed have been Pretty Jolly Too.

Yet the wines, though they warmed the tongue and carried their bright, sharp fruit through the mouth, made no visible change in Mr. Grewgious. They might have been poured over him and run off to waste; his face kept its high-dried, snuff-coloured composure. But he watched Edwin in his wooden, observant way; and when the dinner ended, and he motioned Edwin back into the easy-chair by the fire, his eyes followed him between the smoothing fingers with which he continually adjusted his own head and face, as if polishing himself into readiness.

“Bazzard!” said Mr. Grewgious, turning suddenly.

“I follow you, sir,” returned Bazzard, who had eaten and drunk with workmanlike gravity, mostly in silence.

“I drink to you, Bazzard; Mr. Edwin, success to Mr. Bazzard!”

“Success to Mr. Bazzard!” echoed Edwin, with an enthusiasm he did not feel, adding silently: Success in what, I wonder?

“And May!” pursued Mr. Grewgious, hesitating in a way that suggested he was trying to open a stiff lock with the wrong key. “I am not at liberty to be definite—May!—my conversational powers are so very limited that I know I shall not come well out of this—May!—it ought to be put imaginatively, but I have no imagination—May!—the thorn of anxiety is as nearly the mark as I am likely to get—May it come out at last!”

Bazzard frowned a smile at the fire and put a hand into his tangled hair, as if feeling for the thorn there; then into his waistcoat; then into his pockets. Edwin watched each motion, half expecting some literal thorn to appear and prick, but nothing emerged. Bazzard only said, “I follow you, sir, and I thank you.”

“I am going,” said Mr. Grewgious, jingling his glass softly on the table, and bending aside under cover of his other hand to whisper to Edwin, “to drink to my ward. But I put Bazzard first. He mightn’t like it else.”

He attempted a wink—so slow that it barely completed itself. Edwin, out of politeness and confusion, winked back without the faintest notion what he meant.

“And now,” said Mr. Grewgious, “I devote a bumper to the fair and fascinating Miss Rosa. Bazzard, the fair and fascinating Miss Rosa!”

“I follow you, sir,” said Bazzard. “And I pledge you!”

“And so do I!” cried Edwin.

Blank silence followed, as if some small ceremony had to cool before speech could be ventured again. Why such pauses fall upon people after they have done a simple social act—done it without any sorrowful intention—is one of those mysteries of company that no law-book explains. The only steady sound was the fire, and beyond it, muffled by fog and distance, the softened world: a hush that made every clink of glass seem louder, and every breath a little more private.

“Lord bless me,” said Mr. Grewgious at last, as though he had been compelled to speak by the weight of his own observation. “I am a particularly Angular man, and yet I fancy—if I may use the word, not having a morsel of fancy—that I could draw a picture of a true lover’s state of mind, to-night.”

“Let us follow you, sir,” said Bazzard, “and have the picture.”

“Mr. Edwin will correct it where it’s wrong,” resumed Mr. Grewgious, “and will throw in a few touches from the life. I dare say it is wrong in many particulars, and wants many touches from the life, for I was born a Chip, and have neither soft sympathies nor soft experiences. Well! I hazard the guess that the true lover’s mind is completely permeated by the beloved object of his affections. I hazard the guess that her dear name is precious to him, cannot be heard or repeated without emotion, and is preserved sacred. If he has any distinguishing appellation of fondness for her, it is reserved for her, and is not for common ears. A name that it would be a privilege to call her by, being alone with her own bright self, it would be a liberty, a coldness, an insensibility, almost a breach of good faith, to flaunt elsewhere.”

He sat bolt upright, hands on knees, chopping the sentences out as if repeating a lesson learnt by heart. He showed no corresponding feeling, unless in a slight tingling at the tip of his nose. Yet the meaning, spoken in that dry tone, fell with a sharper edge—like salt in plain broth.

Edwin, as certain points found him, turned red and then white. He stared into the fire and bit his lip.

“My picture,” Mr. Grewgious went on, “goes on to represent—under correction from you, Mr. Edwin—the true lover as ever impatient to be in the presence or vicinity of the beloved object of his affections; as caring very little for his case in any other society; and as constantly seeking that. If I was to say seeking that, as a bird seeks its nest, I should make an ass of myself, because that would trench upon what I understand to be poetry; and I am so far from trenching upon poetry at any time, that I never, to my knowledge, got within ten thousand miles of it. And I am besides totally unacquainted with the habits of birds, except the birds of Staple Inn, who seek their nests on ledges, and in gutter-pipes and chimneypots, not constructed for them by the beneficent hand of Nature. I beg, therefore, to be understood as foregoing the bird’s-nest. But my picture does represent the true lover as having no existence separable from that of the beloved object of his affections, and as living at once a doubled life and a halved life. And if I do not clearly express what I mean by that, it is either for the reason that having no conversational powers, I cannot express what I mean, or that having no meaning, I do not mean what I fail to express. Which, to the best of my belief, is not the case.”

Abrupt as a snapped string, he concluded and jerked his question at Edwin.

“I figure to myself,” he said, “that there can be no coolness, no lassitude, no doubt, no indifference, no half fire and half smoke state of mind, in a real lover. Pray am I at all near the mark in my picture?”

“I should say, sir,” Edwin stammered, “as you refer the question to me—”

“Yes,” said Mr. Grewgious. “I refer it to you, as an authority.”

“I should say, then, sir,” Edwin went on, visibly hampered, “that the picture you have drawn is generally correct; but I submit that perhaps you may be rather hard upon the unlucky lover.”

“Likely so,” returned Mr. Grewgious. “Likely so. I am a hard man in the grain.”

“He may not show,” Edwin said, “all he feels; or he may not—”

He stopped, hunting for words. And at once Mr. Grewgious struck in, in a way that made the gap yawn wider.

“No to be sure; he may not!”

After that they all sat silent again. Bazzard’s silence, however, had slipped into sleep; his head inclined, his gloom at rest at last.

“His responsibility is very great, though,” said Mr. Grewgious, after a time, his eyes fixed on the coals as if reading there.

Edwin nodded, eyes fixed likewise, as if the fire held the only language he could trust.

“And let him be sure that he trifles with no one,” pursued Mr. Grewgious; “neither with himself, nor with any other.”

Edwin bit his lip again.

“He must not make a plaything of a treasure. Woe betide him if he does! Let him take that well to heart,” said Mr. Grewgious.

He spoke in short sentences, like a charity boy repeating Proverbs; yet there was something almost dreamy—strange in so literal a man—in the way he shook his right forefinger at the live coals, as if warning the fire itself, and then sank into quiet.

Not for long. He rapped his knees, as if a carved idol had come out of reverie, and said briskly: “We must finish this bottle, Mr. Edwin. Let me help you. I’ll help Bazzard too, though he is asleep. He mightn’t like it else.”

He filled their glasses, filled his own, and drained his with decision. Then he set it bottom upward on the table as if he had trapped a bluebottle.

“And now, Mr. Edwin,” he said, wiping mouth and hands with his handkerchief, “to a little piece of business. You received from me, the other day, a certified copy of Miss Rosa’s father’s will. You knew its contents before, but you received it from me as a matter of business. I should have sent it to Mr. Jasper, but for Miss Rosa’s wishing it to come straight to you, in preference. You received it?”

“Quite safely, sir.”

“You should have acknowledged its receipt,” said Mr. Grewgious. “Business being business all the world over. However, you did not.”

“I meant to have acknowledged it when I first came in this evening, sir.”

When Mr. Sapsea had exhausted, for the day, the pleasure of admiring Mr. Sapsea, he took to the Close as to a parlour that belonged to him by right of self-importance. The air was damp enough to lay a cold hand on the railings of the churchyard; the leaves along the path held that wet, herbal smell that rises when earth has been turned and then remembered by rain. He went swelling past the graves with the proprietary gait of a landlord inspecting tenancies, pausing—if chance offered—to imagine some stranger reading his inscription and retreating, appropriately abashed, in obedience to the stone.

His consequence had lately been spiced and puffed up by office: he was Mayor now. Cloisterham, he considered, was a kind of great household that could not possibly keep its accounts, its dignity, its very joints and bones, without a Mayor to bind it together like good gravy. A Mayor might even “go up” with an address; and if English Grammar suffered a few pellets of shot and shell in the discharge, why, Grammar must bear what it could for the commonweal. Rise, Sir Thomas Sapsea—so ran his private, nutritious fancy.

He had also improved his acquaintance with Mr. Jasper, the Cathedral’s Choir-Master. Mr. Sapsea enjoyed the gatehouse for its solid comfort—port, beef, salad, backgammon, and an epitaph or two like pepper thrown over all—and he enjoyed Jasper’s deference more. Jasper would sit down at the piano after dinner and sing until the very air seemed stroked and persuaded; not with the modern, foreign frippery that Mr. Sapsea suspected of treason, but with stout old songs that marched in time like drilled men, and made the room ring as if it had been built expressly to echo national virtue. Jasper’s voice, in those moments, was a kind of domestic hearth: it warmed; it soothed; it made Mr. Sapsea feel that Providence had taken pains with English throats.

This moist evening, as the lamplighter began to seed the Close with small, steady flames—climbing and dropping his little ladder at a practised hurry, that ladder which had been part of the town’s childhood and would have been defended like a family relic—Mr. Sapsea rounded a corner by the churchyard expecting perhaps his usual phantom stranger. Instead he encountered the Dean, talking with the Verger, Mr. Tope, and with Jasper.

Mr. Sapsea bowed at once into a pitch of ecclesiastical humility that would have done credit to an Archbishop, and the Dean—whose humour was as old as the stones but not as heavy—was saying to Jasper:

“You are evidently going to write a book about us, Mr. Jasper; to write a book about us. We are very ancient, and we ought to make a good book. We are not so richly endowed in possessions as in age; but perhaps you will put that in your book, among other things, and call attention to our wrongs.”

Mr. Tope, dutifully entertained, showed his teeth in the proper reverent measure.

“I really have no intention at all, sir,” Jasper answered, “of turning author or archaeologist. It is but a whim of mine. And even for my whim, Mr. Sapsea here is more accountable than I am.”

“How so, Mr. Mayor?” asked the Dean, with a nod that acknowledged Cloisterham’s Fetch. “How is that, Mr. Mayor?”

“I am not aware,” Mr. Sapsea began, looking about him as if the answer might be hanging somewhere on the weather, “to what the Very Reverend the Dean does me the honour of referring.” And then, as if to recover himself by studying authority, he fell to examining the Dean’s person with minute attention.

“Durdles,” hinted Mr. Tope.

“Ay! Durdles,” echoed the Dean, pleased to be reminded.

“The truth is, sir,” Jasper explained, “that my curiosity in the man was first really stimulated by Mr. Sapsea. Mr. Sapsea’s knowledge of mankind and power of drawing out whatever is recluse or odd around him, first led to my bestowing a second thought upon the man—though of course I had met him constantly about. You would not be surprised by this, Mr. Dean, if you had seen Mr. Sapsea deal with him in his own parlour, as I did.”

Mr. Sapsea accepted the compliment as a man accepts a dish he thinks has been prepared in his honour: slowly, with relish, and with a look around to see who notices his appetite.

“O! yes, yes. The Very Reverend the Dean refers to that? Yes. I happened to bring Durdles and Mr. Jasper together. I regard Durdles as a Character.”

“A character,” said Jasper, “that with a few skilful touches you turn inside out.”

“Nay, not quite that,” returned Mr. Sapsea, lumbering into modesty as into a too-tight coat. “I may have a little influence over him, perhaps; and a little insight into his character, perhaps. The Very Reverend the Dean will please to bear in mind that I have seen the world.” He edged a little behind the Dean, as if to inspect the Dean’s coat-buttons and so, by proxy, inspect history itself.

“Well!” said the Dean, glancing round for his verger as though he had mislaid him in his own joke, “I hope, Mr. Mayor, you will use your study and knowledge of Durdles to the good purpose of exhorting him not to break our worthy and respected Choir-Master’s neck; we cannot afford it; his head and voice are much too valuable to us.”
