Mr. Tope, in respectful convulsion, murmured that surely any gentleman would deem it a pleasure and an honour to have his neck broken in return for such a compliment from such a source.

“I will take it upon myself, sir,” Mr. Sapsea announced, “to answer for Mr. Jasper’s neck. I will tell Durdles to be careful of it. He will mind what I say. How is it at present endangered?” He cast the question abroad with a magnificent patronage, as if necks in general were an item he supervised.

“Only by my making a moonlight expedition with Durdles among the tombs, vaults, towers, and ruins,” Jasper returned. “You remember suggesting, when you brought us together, that, as a lover of the picturesque, it might be worth my while?”

“I remember!” said Mr. Sapsea. And, in that moment, he truly believed he did.

“Profiting by your hint,” Jasper pursued, “I have had some day-rambles with the extraordinary old fellow, and we are to make a moonlight hole-and-corner exploration to-night.”

“And here he is,” said the Dean.

Durdles came slouching up with his dinner-bundle in hand, as if he carried not food but some private lump of the day. The smell of him was of grit and lime and old stone that has been cut and wet and left to dry again; the sort of smell that fills the throat in a mason’s shed and makes you aware of your teeth. Seeing the Dean, he took off his hat and was slouching away with it under his arm when Mr. Sapsea stopped him.

“Mind you take care of my friend,” Mr. Sapsea commanded.

“What friend o’ yourn is dead?” Durdles asked. “No orders has come in for any friend o’ yourn.”

“I mean my live friend there.”

“O! him?” Durdles said. “He can take care of himself, can Mister Jarsper.”

“But do you take care of him too,” said Sapsea.

Durdles, feeling the command in the tone rather than any logic in the words, gave the Mayor a sour, deliberate survey from head to foot.

“With submission to his Reverence the Dean, if you’ll mind what concerns you, Mr. Sapsea, Durdles he’ll mind what concerns him.”

“You’re out of temper,” said Mr. Sapsea, winking to the company to observe how suavely he would manage him. “My friend concerns me, and Mr. Jasper is my friend. And you are my friend.”

“Don’t you get into a bad habit of boasting,” Durdles retorted, with a grave nod as if delivering a mason’s measurement. “It’ll grow upon you.”

“You are out of temper,” Mr. Sapsea repeated, reddening, yet still winking as though the redness were part of his method.

“I own to it,” Durdles returned. “I don’t like liberties.”

Mr. Sapsea winked a third time, as who should say, I think you will agree I have settled his business, and stalked out of the controversy.

Durdles gave the Dean good evening and added, setting on his hat again, “You’ll find me at home, Mister Jarsper, as agreed, when you want me; I’m a-going home to clean myself,” and slouched away.

This “cleaning himself” was one of his bargains with propriety. He never appeared cleaner—his hat, his boots, his coat always wearing the same perpetual crust of dust, as if Cloisterham’s stones had claimed him for kin—but the phrase served him like a ritual phrase serves a churchman: it marked the hour and excused the act.

The Dean withdrew to dinner; Mr. Tope to tea; Jasper to his piano. There, with no light but the fire’s, he sat chanting low choir-music, voice flowing like dark wine from a careful bottle, for two or three hours; until the town had grown still, the last doors had muttered on their hinges, and the moon was about to rise.

Then he closed the piano softly. He changed his coat for a pea-jacket with a substantial wicker-cased bottle in its largest pocket, set on a low-crowned, flap-brimmed hat, and went out as quietly as if sound itself were a witness he did not wish to summon. No outward reason required such stealth; yet he moved as though some inward thing crouched and listened for his own footfall.

At Durdles’s unfinished house—more a hole in the city wall than a dwelling—he saw light. He picked his way among gravestones and broken stone-lumber in the yard, where moonlight already touched edges sidewise, making names look newly cut and newly accusatory. Two great saws had been left sticking in their blocks; in the shadow of the sentry-boxes the sawyers’ tools might have been the grin and teeth of two skeleton journeymen from the Dance of Death, ready to set to work on the next pair destined for Cloisterham’s ledger. Likely enough, the men who owned the saws were at supper and thought no more of mortality than of tomorrow’s wage.

“Ho! Durdles!”

The light moved; Durdles appeared at the door with it. His notion of “cleaning” had plainly involved bottle, jug, and tumbler rather than brush or water; no other instruments were visible in the bare brick room with rafters overhead and no plastered ceiling. A sour spirit-smell—spirits and stale smoke and the faint bitter tang of lime dust—lay in the place like a second atmosphere.

“Are you ready?”

“I am ready, Mister Jarsper. Let the old ’uns come out if they dare, when we go among their tombs. My spirit is ready for ’em.”

“Do you mean animal spirits, or ardent?”

“The one’s the t’other,” Durdles answered. “And I mean ’em both.”

He took down a lantern from a hook, pocketed a match or two in case he should need to light it, and went out with Jasper, dinner-bundle and all.

It was an unaccountable expedition. Durdles prowled among graves and ruins as naturally as other men walked to market; the stones were his livelihood and his habit, and the dead were, to him, a set of tenants whose leases he understood. But that Jasper—cathedral musician, domestic guest, man of cultivated ear—should choose to pace the same places for “moonlight effects” in such company, and at such an hour, was another matter. The town’s usual nocturnal soundscape—the measured far-off murmur of the High Street, a cart-wheel complaining, a late voice, a dog’s halfhearted bark—thinned here into hush. Even the lamplighter’s ladder had ceased its clink. The Close had the acoustic quality of old cloth laid over furniture: it muffled.

“’Ware that there mound by the yard-gate, Mister Jarsper,” said Durdles.

“I see it. What is it?”

“Lime.”

Mr. Jasper stopped and waited for him to come up; Durdles lagged, as if the very word had slowed him.

“What you call quick-lime?”

“Ay!” Durdles said. “Quick enough to eat your boots. With a little handy stirring, quick enough to eat your bones.”

They went on, passing the red windows of the Travellers’ Twopenny, where some late drinkers’ laughter leaked out thinly and then was shut back in by glass. Emerging into clearer moonlight, they crossed the Monks’ Vineyard and came to Minor Canon Corner, much of which lay in shadow until the moon should climb higher.

A closing house-door struck their ears: that clean, domestic clap that sounds, in quiet precincts, like a verdict. Two men came out—Mr. Crisparkle and Neville. Jasper’s face, in the dimness, took on a strange, sudden smile. He laid the palm of his hand upon Durdles’s breast and stopped him where he stood.

At that end of the Corner the shadow was profound; and there was a low dwarf wall—breast-high—the last boundary of a garden that had been, and was now a thoroughfare. Jasper and Durdles would have turned it in another moment; but, halted so abruptly, stood behind it.

“Those two are only sauntering,” Jasper whispered. “They will go out into the moonlight soon. Let us keep quiet here, or they will detain us, or want to join us, or what not.”

Durdles nodded and fell to munching fragments from his bundle. There was something pathetic in the homeliness of it: meat and bread and perhaps a bit of cheese carried into the precinct of the dead, as if all hours must be seasoned by eating. In that crunching, one heard life insist on itself. Jasper folded his arms upon the top of the wall and rested his chin on them. He did not watch the Minor Canon at all. He watched Neville—watched him as though his eye lay on the trigger of a loaded rifle and he had him covered and was waiting for the wind. Something in Jasper’s face expressed destructive power so plainly that Durdles paused with an unmunched piece lodged in his cheek, staring.

Meanwhile Mr. Crisparkle and Neville walked to and fro, quietly talking. Their words did not carry in full, only broken by echoes and swallowed by the Corner’s hush, but Jasper caught his own name more than once, like a note struck and repeated.

“This is the first day of the week,” Mr. Crisparkle could be distinctly heard to observe as they turned back; “and the last day of the week is Christmas Eve.”

“You may be certain of me, sir,” Neville replied.

The echoes favoured those phrases and then betrayed them. Jasper caught “confidence,” shattered and pieced together by the stone. As they drew nearer, a fragment came clear: “Not deserved yet, but shall be, sir.” When they turned away again, Jasper heard his own name joined with Mr. Crisparkle’s words: “Remember that I said I answered for you confidently.” The rest became confused; Neville made some earnest action; and then Mr. Crisparkle looked up at the sky and pointed before him. They passed out into the moonlight at the far end and slowly disappeared.

Jasper did not move until they were gone. Then, abruptly, he turned to Durdles and burst into laughter—laughter that did not belong to the Corner’s silence and yet seemed bred by it. Durdles, with that suspended morsel still in his cheek, saw nothing to laugh at and stared. Jasper laid his face down on his arms to have the laugh out. Durdles, as if resigning himself to indigestion and to mysteries beyond masonry, bolted the morsel.

After dark there was almost no stir in these nooks. There was little enough even at noon; but at night the Close seemed to hold its breath. The High Street ran near, and its traffic was the town’s natural channel; yet between it and these precincts the Cathedral rose like a barrier that stopped not only feet but sound. Ask a hundred Cloisterham citizens at midday whether they believed in ghosts, and ninety-nine would say no; ask them at night whether they would take this shorter route or the longer round by shop-lamps and familiar voices, and ninety-nine would choose the longer. It was not local superstition, though a mysterious lady with a child in her arms and a rope at her neck had been seen flitting by witnesses as intangible as herself. It was something more bodily than belief: dust with breath in it shrinking from dust where breath had passed away; and, too, that half-admitted thought that if the dead ever did show themselves, these would be the likeliest surroundings. Even the ear seemed to share the caution. In the Close, every footstep sounded too loud, as if it might wake something.

When Jasper and Durdles paused before a small side door by which the latter had a key, and glanced round, the moonlit expanse was utterly deserted. It was as if the tide of life were stemmed by Jasper’s own gatehouse. One could hear the murmur of it beyond, but no wave passed the archway; Jasper’s lamp burned red behind its curtain like a lighthouse warning ships off.

They entered, locked themselves in, went down rugged steps, and were in the Crypt. The lantern was not needed: moonlight struck through groined windows bare of glass; the broken frames cast patterned bars on the ground like pale latticework. Heavy pillars supported the roof and bred black masses of shade, but between them ran lanes of light. Up and down these lanes they walked, Durdles discoursing of “the old ’uns” he yet counted on disinterring, slapping a wall in which he believed “a whole family on ’em” were stoned and earthed up, just as if he were on familiar visiting terms with the household. His usual taciturnity had been loosened by Jasper’s wicker bottle, which circulated freely—meaning its contents entered freely into Durdles’s circulation. Jasper himself only rinsed his mouth once and cast forth the rinsing, as if he would taste without swallowing, taking the scent and promise rather than the warmth.

They were to ascend the great Tower. On the steps that rose towards the Cathedral level, Durdles paused to gather breath. The stairs were very dark, but out of the darkness they could see those lanes of moonlight below, like strips of pale cloth laid in order. Durdles sat on a step. Jasper sat on another. The smell of spirit soon thickened; the bottle—somehow now in Durdles’s keeping—had plainly been opened. They could not see each other; yet in speaking they turned their faces as though eyes and mouths might meet in the dark.

“This is good stuff, Mister Jarsper!”

“It is very good stuff, I hope.—I bought it on purpose.”

“They don’t show, you see, the old ’uns don’t, Mister Jarsper!”

“It would be a more confused world than it is, if they could.”

“Well, it would lead towards a mixing of things,” Durdles conceded, as if the chief objection to spectres was that they muddled housekeeping. He pondered, then asked with a mason’s stubborn curiosity, “But do you think there may be Ghosts of other things, though not of men and women?”

“What things? Flower-beds and watering-pots? Horses and harness?”

“No. Sounds.”

“What sounds?”

“Cries.”

“What cries do you mean? Chairs to mend?”

“No. I mean screeches. Now I’ll tell you, Mr. Jarsper. Wait a bit till I put the bottle right.” The cork was evidently fussed with—out and in again. “There! Now it’s right! This time last year, only a few days later, I happened to have been doing what was correct by the season, in the way of giving it the welcome it had a right to expect, when them town-boys set on me at their worst. At length I gave ’em the slip, and turned in here. And here I fell asleep. And what woke me? The ghost of a cry. The ghost of one terrific shriek, which shriek was followed by the ghost of the howl of a dog: a long, dismal, woeful howl, such as a dog gives when a person’s dead. That was my last Christmas Eve.”

“What do you mean?” Jasper retorted, abrupt—almost fierce, as if a wrong note had been struck in his hearing.

“I mean that I made inquiries everywhere about, and that no living ears but mine heard either that cry or that howl. So I say they was both ghosts; though why they came to me, I’ve never made out.”

“I thought you were another kind of man,” Jasper said, with scorn that seemed aimed not only at Durdles but at the idea that sound might outlast sense.

“So I thought myself,” Durdles returned, composed. “And yet I was picked out for it.”

Jasper had risen suddenly when he demanded what Durdles meant; now he said, “Come; we shall freeze here; lead the way.”

Durdles got up, not very steadily, used his key again, and they emerged on the Cathedral level in a passage beside the chancel. Here the moonlight was so bright that the nearest stained-glass window threw its colours upon their faces—purple and yellow splashes like bruises and jaundice. Durdles, holding the door open for Jasper to follow as if he were letting him out of a grave, looked ghastly with the colour laid across him; yet he bore Jasper’s close scrutiny without consciousness. Jasper’s hands were busy at his pockets, seeking a key confided to him that would open an iron gate and admit them to the staircase of the great tower.

“That and the bottle are enough for you to carry,” Jasper said, passing the bottle to Durdles. “Hand your bundle to me; I am younger and longer-winded than you.”

Durdles hesitated between bundle and bottle as between duty and delight; but the bottle was by far the better company, and he kept it, consigning the dry weight to Jasper.

Then they went up the winding staircase of the great tower—turning and turning, shoulders and heads lowered to avoid the stair above or the rough stone pivot around which they twisted. Durdles lit the lantern by striking from the cold hard wall a spark of that hidden fire that lives in all things; and by that small star they clambered through cobwebs and dust. They passed through strange places: low-arched galleries where they could look down into the moonlit nave, and where, when Durdles waved his lantern, the dim carved angels’ heads on the corbels seemed to move and watch. Then into narrower, steeper staircases; the night-air began to blow upon them; a startled jackdaw or frightened rook chirped in the confined space, wings beat heavily against stone, and dust and straw rained on their heads as the bird forced past. At last, leaving their light behind a stair because the wind blew fresh up here, they looked down on Cloisterham spread fair in the moonlight: ruins and sanctuaries of the dead at the tower’s base; moss-softened red-tiled roofs and red-brick houses of the living beyond; and the river winding down from the mist on the horizon as though it rose from that whiteness, already heaving with a restless knowledge that its course ran seaward.

Again, what an unaccountable expedition. Jasper—still moving softly, as if even stone could hear—contemplated the scene, especially that stillest part of it which the Cathedral overshadowed. But he contemplated Durdles quite as curiously. Durdles, by times, felt the watchful eyes and shifted, uneasy; only by times, because he was growing drowsy.

As balloon-men lighten their load when they wish to rise, so Durdles had lightened the wicker bottle in coming up. Sleep caught him in snatches, surprising him on his legs and stopping his speech in the middle like a tool dropped. A mild calenture took him: he imagined the ground far below level with the tower, and would as willingly have walked out into air as along the stone. In such a state they began to come down. And as balloon-men make themselves heavier when they wish to descend, so Durdles charged himself with more liquid from the bottle to come down the better.

They reached the iron gate and locked it—not before Durdles had tumbled twice and once cut an eyebrow open—and descended into the crypt again, meaning to issue by the way they had entered. But returning among the lanes of moonlight between the pillars, Durdles grew so uncertain in foot and speech that he half dropped, half threw himself down by one of the heavy columns—scarcely less heavy than himself—and, in a thick, indistinct appeal, begged his companion for forty winks of a second each.

“If you will have it so, or must have it so,” Jasper replied, “I’ll not leave you here. Take them, while I walk to and fro.”

A stillness was settling over Miss Twinkleton’s establishment, the sort of hush that comes not from obedience but from the long inhalation before a door is opened and a household breathes out into the wider world. Christmas recess was at hand. What had been “the half” within living memory—Miss Twinkleton herself once saying it with a classical air, as if the phrase had been minted at Athens—had, by degrees of fashion and the tyranny of labels, become “the term,” for the sound of it was more collegiate, and the sound, in Cloisterham, mattered nearly as much as the sense.

For a few days past, discipline had loosened like a ribbon untied and hurriedly tucked away. There had been secret club-suppers in bedrooms; contraband sustenance smuggled up the stairs under the very noses of Propriety’s handmaids. A dressed tongue—pink, yielding, and scandalously savoury—had been carved not with a knife but with scissors, and passed round in slices pinched between curling-tongs, the metal warmed from hair and candle-flame and leaving faint metallic kisses on the meat. Marmalade had been portioned out, not upon china but upon plates ingeniously constructed of curlpaper—sticky, citrus-bright, and perfumed, so that the whole room smelt for an hour afterwards of orange peel and hair powder. Cowslip wine, pale as diluted sunlight and faintly grassy in its sweetness, had been quaffed from the squat little measuring-glass in which Rickitts—weakly, much dosed, and always spoken of in a whisper of pity—took her steel drops of a morning. The very acoustics of the house altered: stifled giggles behind doors, the hush of feet on the landings, the quick, conspiratorial clink of the measuring-glass set down upon a washstand, and then, somewhere below, the heavy regularity of the town’s traffic beyond the walls, hoarse as if the High Street never learned refinement.

The housemaids had been bribed with odd fragments of ribbon and with shoes more or less down at heel, to know nothing of crumbs in beds. Airiest costumes appeared on these festive occasions, as if girls could dress themselves into invisibility; and the daring Miss Ferdinand had even performed a sprightly solo on the comb-and-curlpaper—an impromptu orchestra of teeth and tissue—until two flowing-haired executioners, laughing breathlessly, smothered her in her own pillow. Modern life had not yet brought machinery into those corridors, but Cloisterham had its own kind of noise pollution: the constant public clatter outside, the gossip that rang like a bell, and the way a word—once let loose—could vibrate through a town for years. The girls, in their tiny rebellion, made a private soundscape, soft and human, as if instinct told them the soul required gentler noises to thrive.

Nor were these the only signals of dispersal. Boxes appeared in bedrooms—excellent pieces of luggage at other times—and a surprising quantity of packing took place, far outweighing the quantity packed. Largess was distributed among attendants: odds and ends of cold cream and pomatum, hairpins, little remnants of scent that carried, in their very grease and sweetness, the domestic memory of another girl’s dressing-table. Under vows of secrecy, confidences were exchanged about “golden youth of England” expected to call at home at the earliest chance. Miss Giggles, deficient in sentiment and proud of it, declared she would meet such homage by making faces; but she was outvoted by an immense majority whose hopes, like their secret suppers, were best enjoyed in company.

On the last night before recess, it was always a point of honour that nobody should go to sleep, and that ghosts should be encouraged by every possible means. The compact invariably broke down: the girls fell asleep very soon and woke very early, because the body will not be bullied for long, no matter what myths the heart tries to conjure. Still, in those early hours, when the house quieted and the Cathedral’s shadow lay long and cold, it was easy to imagine old voices lodged in the stone, speaking a dialect no longer understood—language dying not in one dramatic moment, but in the daily replacement of a word by a smoother one, a phrase by a more “elegant” phrase, until the past could no longer tell its story in its own tongue.

The concluding ceremony occurred at noon on the day of departure. Miss Twinkleton, supported by Mrs. Tisher, held a drawing-room in her own apartment—the globes already wrapped in brown holland, as if knowledge itself were being put away for the holiday—where glasses of white wine and plates of cut pound-cake sat ready upon the table. The cake was dense, buttery, and crumbling at the edge where it had been sliced; it smelled of sugar warmed and cooled again, a scent that clings to napery and fingers. Miss Twinkleton, in her measured voice, addressed them.

“Ladies, another revolving year has brought us round to that festive period at which the first feelings of our nature bounded in our—” She would have said “bosoms,” as she always nearly did, and as she always checked herself. “Hearts. Hem! Again a revolving year, ladies, has brought us to a pause in our studies—let us hope our greatly advanced studies—and, like the mariner in his bark, the warrior in his tent, the captive in his dungeon, and the traveller in his various conveyances, we yearn for home. Did we say, on such an occasion, in the opening words of Mr. Addison’s impressive tragedy: ‘The dawn is overcast, the morning lowers…’? Not so. From horizon to zenith all is couleur de rose, for all is redolent of our relations and friends. Might we find them prospering as we expect; might they find us prospering as they expect! Ladies, we would now, with our love to one another, wish one another good-bye and happiness, until we meet again. And when the time should come for our resumption of those pursuits which—” here the general depression came like a collective sigh “—pursuits which, pursuits which; then let us ever remember what was said by the Spartan General, in words too trite for repetition, at the battle it were superfluous to specify.”

The handmaidens, in their best caps, brought the trays. The young ladies sipped and crumbled. White wine, sharp and faintly appley, cut through the sweet fat of the cake; the small sound of glasses touching teeth and the soft fall of crumbs made a domestic music, while outside the bespoken coaches began to choke the street with their iron rims and impatient horses. Leave-taking followed quickly. Miss Twinkleton kissed each cheek and, with a practised air of delicate surprise, slipped into each hand a neat letter addressed to the next friend at law, “with Miss Twinkleton’s best compliments” in the corner—as if the missive were an unexpected pleasure entirely unconnected with the bill.

So many times had Rosa seen such dispersals, and so little did she know of any other Home, that she was content to remain where she was; and more content than ever now, because her latest friend remained with her. Yet even this friendship had a blank space in it that Rosa could not help but touch, as the tongue touches a missing tooth. Helena Landless—having been party to her brother’s revelation about Rosa, and having entered into a compact of silence with Mr. Crisparkle—shrank from any mention of Edwin Drood. Why she avoided his name was mysterious; but Rosa heard the avoidance as plainly as if it were sounded aloud. If Helena had not flinched from it, Rosa might have unburdened her perplexed little heart; as it was, she could only turn her doubts over and over, and wonder why this omission should continue now that Helena had said there was to be a good understanding re-established between the two young men when Edwin came down.

It would have made a pretty picture: so many pretty girls kissing Rosa in the cold porch of the Nuns’ House, and that sunny little creature peeping out—unconscious of sly faces carved upon spout and gable peeping at her—and waving farewells as if she were the spirit of rosy youth left behind to keep the deserted place warm. The hoarse High Street became musical with silvery voices calling, “Good-bye, Rosebud darling!” Even the effigy of Mr. Sapsea’s father over the opposite doorway seemed to invite the world to admire the last bright lot left behind. Then the sparkle ran dry, and Cloisterham became itself again—staid, stone-heavy, and loud in its habitual way, as if it could not help grinding its own human sounds into something coarser.

If Rosebud, in her bower, now waited Edwin Drood’s coming with uneasiness, Edwin was uneasy too. With far less force of purpose in him than in the childish beauty crowned fairy queen by Miss Twinkleton’s household, he had a conscience, and Mr. Grewgious had pricked it. That gentleman’s steady convictions of right and wrong in such a case could not be frowned aside or laughed aside. But for the dinner in Staple Inn, and but for the ring he carried in his breast-pocket, Edwin would have drifted into his wedding-day without stopping to think—trusting, loosely, that all would go well. Yet that solemn putting him on his truth to the living and the dead had checked him like a hand on the chest. He must either give the ring to Rosa, or take it back.

Once narrowed into so plain a choice, it was curious how he began to consider Rosa’s claims more unselfishly than ever before, and to be less sure of himself than he had been in all his easy-going days.

“I will be guided by what she says, and by how we get on,” he decided as he walked from the gatehouse to the Nuns’ House. “Whatever comes of it, I will bear his words in mind, and try to be true to the living and the dead.”

Rosa was dressed for walking, and she expected him. The day was bright and frosty; the air had that clean, sharp smell that pricks the nostrils and makes the mouth taste of cold iron. Miss Twinkleton had graciously sanctioned fresh air, and thus the two young people slipped out together before either Miss Twinkleton or Mrs. Tisher could lay so much as a finger upon the shrine of Propriety.

“My dear Eddy,” said Rosa, once they had turned out of the High Street and had reached the quieter walks near the Cathedral and the river, “I want to say something very serious to you. I have been thinking about it for a long, long time.”

“I want to be serious with you too, Rosa dear. I mean to be serious and earnest.”

“Thank you, Eddy. And you won’t think me unkind because I begin, will you? You won’t think I speak for myself only, because I speak first? That wouldn’t be generous, would it? And I know you are generous.”

“I hope I am not ungenerous to you, Rosa.”

He called her Pussy no more. Never again.

“And there is no fear,” pursued Rosa, clasping her hand upon his arm, “of our quarrelling, is there? Because, Eddy, we have so much reason to be very lenient to each other!”

“We will be, Rosa.”

“That’s a dear good boy! Eddy, let us be courageous. Let us change to brother and sister from this day forth.”

“Never be husband and wife?”

“Never!”

Neither spoke for a little while. The river ran beside them with a low, continual voice, as if it had been speaking this sentence for centuries in a language that the banks understood better than the people. After the pause, Edwin said, with some effort:

“Of course I know this has been in both our minds, Rosa, and of course I am in honour bound to confess freely that it doesn’t originate with you.”

“No, nor with you, dear,” she returned, with pathetic earnestness. “That sprung up between us. You are not truly happy in our engagement; I am not truly happy in it. Oh, I am so sorry, so sorry!” And there she broke into tears.

“I am deeply sorry too, Rosa. Deeply sorry for you.”

“And I for you, poor boy! And I for you!”

This gentle forbearance—each thinking first of the other—brought its own reward. A softening light fell upon their position. Their engagement no longer looked wilful or capricious, or a mere failure; it became something more self-denying, honourable, affectionate, and true.

“If we knew yesterday,” said Rosa, drying her eyes, “and we did know yesterday, and on many, many yesterdays, that we were far from right together in those relations which were not of our own choosing, what better could we do to-day than change them? It is natural that we should be sorry, and you see how sorry we both are; but how much better to be sorry now than then!”

“When, Rosa?”

“When it would be too late. And then we should be angry besides.”

Another silence fell, with only the river’s steady undertone and, farther off, the town’s ruder noises drifting in—the grind of wheels, the shout that never quite becomes a song.

“And you know,” said Rosa, innocently, “you couldn’t like me then; and you can always like me now, for I shan’t be a drag upon you, or a worry to you. And I can always like you now, and your sister won’t tease or trifle with you. I often did when I was not your sister, and I beg your pardon for it.”

“Don’t let us come to that, Rosa; or I shall want more pardoning than I like to think of.”

“No indeed, Eddy; you are too hard—my generous boy—upon yourself. Let us sit down, brother, on these ruins, and let me tell you how it was with us. I think I know, for I have considered about it very much since you were here last time. You liked me, didn’t you? You thought I was a nice little thing?”

“Everybody thinks that, Rosa.”

“Do they?” She knitted her brow, musing, and then flashed out with her bright little induction: “Well, but say they do. Surely it wasn’t enough that you should think of me only as other people did; now, was it?”

It was not enough. The point held.

“And that is just what I mean; that is just how it was with us,” said Rosa. “You liked me very well, and you had grown used to me, and had grown used to the idea of our being married. You accepted the situation as an inevitable kind of thing, didn’t you? It was to be, you thought, and why discuss or dispute it?”

It was new and strange to Edwin to see himself held up so clearly in her glass. He had always patronised her a little, in his sense of superiority; and now, walking in the thin cold sunlight, he felt the patronage look shabby—like a grand word that, when repeated, has lost its meaning.

“All that I say of you is true of me as well, Eddy. Unless it was, I might not be bold enough to say it. Only the difference between us was that, by little and little, there crept into my mind a habit of thinking about it instead of dismissing it. My life is not so busy as yours, you see, and I haven’t so many things to think of. So I thought about it very much—and I cried about it very much too, though that wasn’t your fault, poor boy—when all at once my guardian came down to prepare for my leaving the Nuns’ House. I tried to hint to him that I wasn’t quite settled, but I hesitated and failed, and he didn’t understand me. But he is a good, good man. And he put before me so kindly, and yet so strongly, how seriously we ought to consider, in our circumstances, that I resolved to speak to you the next moment we were alone and grave. And if I seemed to come to it easily just now, because I came to it all at once, don’t think it was so really, Eddy, for oh, it was very, very hard, and oh, I am very, very sorry!”

Her full heart broke into tears again. Edwin put his arm about her waist, and they walked on by the river-side together, their breath visible now and then like words exhaled and lost.

“Your guardian has spoken to me too, Rosa dear. I saw him before I left London.”

His right hand sought, by habit, the little case in his breast. The ring was there—cold even through cloth, as if it kept the temperature of a past intention. He checked his hand. If he was to take it back, why speak of it?

“And that made you more serious about it, didn’t it, Eddy? And if I hadn’t spoken as I have, you would have spoken to me? I hope you can tell me so. I don’t like it to be all my doing, though it is so much better for us.”

“Yes, I should have spoken; I should have put everything before you; I came intending to do it. But I never could have spoken to you as you have spoken to me, Rosa.”

“Don’t say you mean so coldly or unkindly, Eddy, please, if you can help it.”

“I mean so sensibly and delicately, so wisely and affectionately.”

“That’s my dear brother!” She kissed his hand in a little rapture, as if the new name had taste and warmth in it. “The dear girls will be dreadfully disappointed,” she added, laughing with tears still bright upon her lashes. “They’ve looked forward to it so, poor pets!”

“Ah! but I fear it will be a worse disappointment to Jack,” said Edwin Drood, with a start. “I never thought of Jack!”

Her swift, intent look at him—gone as quickly as lightning—was so vivid that it seemed to leave a mark. At once she looked down, confused, and breathed quickly.

“You don’t doubt its being a blow to Jack, Rosa?”

She answered evasively and hurriedly: why should she? She had not thought about it. He seemed to have so little to do with it.

“My dear child! can you suppose that anyone so wrapped up in another—Mrs. Tope’s expression, not mine—as Jack is in me, could fail to be struck all of a heap by such a sudden and complete change in my life? I say sudden, because it will be sudden to him, you know.”

She nodded two or three times, lips parted as if assent were on them, yet no sound came. Her breathing did not slow.

“How shall I tell Jack?” Edwin ruminated, his thoughts busy with the practical difficulty. If he had been less occupied, he must have noticed her singular agitation. “I never thought of Jack. It must be broken to him, before the town-crier knows it. I dine with the dear fellow to-morrow and next day—Christmas Eve and Christmas Day—but it would never do to spoil his feast-days. He always worries about me, and moddley-coddleys in the merest trifles. The news is sure to overset him. How on earth shall this be broken to Jack?”

“He must be told, I suppose?” said Rosa.

“My dear Rosa! who ought to be in our confidence, if not Jack?”

“My guardian promised to come down, if I should write and ask him. I am going to do so. Would you like to leave it to him?”

“A bright idea!” cried Edwin. “The other trustee. Nothing more natural. He comes down, he goes to Jack, he relates what we have agreed upon, and he states our case better than we could. He has already spoken feelingly to you, he has already spoken feelingly to me, and he’ll put the whole thing feelingly to Jack. That’s it! I am not a coward, Rosa, but to tell you a secret, I am a little afraid of Jack.”

“No, no! you are not afraid of him!” cried Rosa, turning white and clasping her hands.

“Why, sister Rosa, sister Rosa, what do you see from the turret?” said Edwin, trying to rally her. “My dear girl!”

“You frightened me.”

“Most unintentionally, but I am as sorry as if I had meant to do it. Could you suppose for a moment, from any loose way of speaking, that I was literally afraid of the dear fond fellow? What I mean is, he is subject to a kind of paroxysm, or fit—I saw him in it once—and I don’t know but that so great a surprise, coming upon him direct from me whom he is so wrapped up in, might bring it on perhaps. Which—and this is the secret I was going to tell you—is another reason for your guardian’s making the communication. He is so steady, precise, and exact, that he will talk Jack’s thoughts into shape in no time; whereas with me Jack is always impulsive and hurried, and, I may say, almost womanish.”

Rosa seemed convinced. Perhaps because her own point of view of “Jack” was altogether different, she felt comforted—protected—by the interposition of Mr. Grewgious between herself and him.

Again Edwin’s hand closed upon the ring-case and again he checked it with the same thought: it was certain now he must give it back; why speak of it? That pretty sympathetic nature, so sorry for him in the blight of their childish hopes, so ready to stand alone in a new world and weave fresh wreaths from whatever flowers it might bear, would only be grieved by those sorrowful jewels. To what purpose? They were a sign of broken joys and baseless projects; in their very beauty they made a cruel satire on human loves and plans that forecast nothing and crumble like dust. Let them be. He would restore them to her guardian when he came down; he in his turn would restore them to the cabinet from which he had unwillingly taken them; and there—like old letters, old vows, records of aspirations come to nothing—they would lie disregarded until, being valuable, they were sold into circulation again, to repeat their former round. Let them be, and lie unspoken of, against his heart.

Among the countless chains that time and circumstance forge day and night, there was one chain forged in the instant of that small conclusion—riveted to the foundations of heaven and earth, and gifted with invincible force to hold and drag.

They walked on by the river, speaking now of separate plans: he would quicken his departure from England; she would remain where she was, at least as long as Helena remained. The poor dear girls should be prepared gently, and first of all Miss Twinkleton should be confided in by Rosa, even before Mr. Grewgious reappeared. It must be made clear everywhere that she and Edwin were the best of friends. There had never been so serene an understanding between them since they were first affianced. Yet each held a reservation: on Rosa’s side, that she intended—through her guardian—to withdraw herself immediately from the tuition of her music-master; on Edwin’s, that he had begun to entertain wandering speculations whether it might ever come to pass that he would know more of Miss Landless.

The bright frosty day declined. The sun dipped into the river far behind them, and the old city lay red before them, as their walk drew to a close. The moaning water cast its seaweed duskily at their feet when they turned from its margin, and rooks hovered above with hoarse cries—dark splashes in the darkening air, a harsh chorus that seemed part of Cloisterham’s permanent sound, older than any spoken promise.

“I will prepare Jack for my flitting soon,” said Edwin, in a low voice, “and I will but see your guardian when he comes, and then go before they speak together. It will be better done without my being by. Don’t you think so?”

“Yes.”

“We know we have done right, Rosa?”

“Yes.”

“We know we are better so, even now?”

“And shall be far, far better so by-and-by.”

Still, that lingering tenderness for what they relinquished made them prolong the parting. When they reached the elm-trees by the Cathedral, where they had last sat together, they stopped as if by consent, and Rosa raised her face to his as she had never raised it in the old days—for they were old already.

“God bless you, dear! Good-bye!”

“God bless you, dear! Good-bye!”

They kissed each other fervently.

“Now, please take me home, Eddy, and let me be by myself.”

“Don’t look round, Rosa,” he cautioned her, drawing her arm through his. “Didn’t you see Jack?”

“No! Where?”

“Under the trees. He saw us, as we took leave of each other. Poor fellow! He little thinks we have parted. This will be a blow to him, I am much afraid.”

She hurried on without resting, and hurried still until they passed under the gatehouse into the street. Once there, she asked, panting as if the very air had become difficult:

“Has he followed us? You can look without seeming to. Is he behind?”

“No. Yes, he is! He has just passed out under the gateway. The dear sympathetic old fellow likes to keep us in sight. I am afraid he will be bitterly disappointed!”

She pulled hurriedly at the handle of the hoarse old bell—the sound of it rough and antiquated, as if the metal itself spoke an old dialect—and the gate soon opened. Before going in, she gave Edwin one last wide, wondering look, imploring as if it would force understanding into him: Oh! don’t you understand?

And out of that look he vanished from her view.

Christmas Eve laid Cloisterham under its old spell, as if the year turned back upon itself and found the same marks cut in the same stone. Faces drifted in from the outer world—grown faces that had once been the town’s children—and the place seemed to have shrunk around them, like cloth badly washed, drawing tight at the seams. The Cathedral clock struck with the same deliberate authority it had in their nursery-days, and the rooks cawed from the tower in a harsh, homely chorus that belonged to no single season, but to all seasons at once. Even the air carried its own memory: leaf-rustle remembered by people who, in far-off sickrooms, had fancied their floors strewn with the Close’s autumn fall, the circle of their lives almost closed, beginning and end leaning toward each other until they touched.

Holly berries shone in lattices at Minor Canon Corner. Mr. and Mrs. Tope, careful as cooks pinning sprigs into a joint before it goes to table, were sticking bright leaves into the Cathedral carvings and sconces, dressing the stalls as if the wood itself were a dignitary needing his buttonhole. Shop windows made a lavish show of currants and raisins, nut-brown and glossy; of candied peel cut into jewel-like strips; of spices—cinnamon and clove—whose scent, crushed between the fingers, could warm a whole street; of moist sugar packed down like snow that had learned to melt. There was a holiday swagger too: mistletoe hung heavy in a greengrocer’s doorway, and a forlorn little Twelfth Cake—so stinted it looked as if it had been divided and divided again—balanced a Harlequin on its summit, offered for a shilling a chance at sweetness. The wax-work exhibition, once so admired by distant royalty, promised itself “by particular desire” on a bankrupt stable-keeper’s premises; and the Theatre shouted a new pantomime with a postered clown who asked the town how it did to-morrow, as large as life and as pitiable as paint could make him. Yet Cloisterham’s schools were quiet: the High School boys had fled home, each secretly in love with some young lady who knew nothing of it; and Miss Twinkleton’s windows fluttered only with handmaidens, who, left to represent their sex in the concrete, grew skittish within propriety like sparrows permitted a whole granary.

Three were to meet at the gatehouse that night. All day, as if in separate kitchens before a single meal, each made ready in his own fashion.

Neville Landless, though freed from books by Mr. Crisparkle’s holiday indulgence, worked in his quiet room until two hours after noon, reading and writing with a close, intent air. Then, with the thoroughness of a man scrubbing down his board after kneading, he cleared his table, set his books in strict order, and tore up every stray paper. He burned the scraps until no loose leaf remained but what bore directly upon his studies. Drawers were squared, corners made clean; nothing untidy survived.

Next he turned to his wardrobe and selected a few plain necessities: change of stout shoes and socks fit for walking, clothing chosen not for show but for endurance, like provisions chosen for a long road. These he packed into a knapsack—new, bought yesterday in the High Street. At the same time he had bought a heavy walking-stick, iron-shod and thick in the handle, made for a hand to grip as if it meant to hold its ground. He swung it, tried its balance, laid it beside the knapsack on the window-seat, and, his arrangements finished, dressed to go out.

He had left his room and met Mr. Crisparkle on the staircase, the Minor Canon coming out upon the same story, when Neville turned back for the stick, thinking to carry it now. When he reappeared with it, Mr. Crisparkle paused, saw it, and with a smile took it from him.

“How do you choose a stick, Neville?”

“Really I don’t know that I understand the subject,” Neville answered. “I chose it for its weight.”

“Much too heavy, Neville; much too heavy.”

“To rest upon in a long walk, sir?”

“Rest upon?” Mr. Crisparkle repeated, dropping his shoulders into an easy pedestrian poise. “You don’t rest upon it; you merely balance with it.”

“I shall know better with practice, sir. I have not lived in a walking country, you know.”

“True,” said Mr. Crisparkle. “Get into a little training, and we will have a few score miles together. I should leave you nowhere now. Do you come back before dinner?”

“I think not, as we dine early.”

Mr. Crisparkle gave him a bright nod—cheerful in manner, and meaning to be so, as if confidence itself were a remedy—and left him.

Neville went to the Nuns’ House and asked that Miss Landless be told her brother waited by appointment. He stood at the gate and did not cross the threshold; he was on his parole not to put himself in Rosa’s way.

Helena was as mindful as he. She came out at once. They met with quiet affection and, avoiding loitering, walked out toward the upper inland country where the air lay harder and clearer.

“I am not going to tread upon forbidden ground, Helena,” Neville said when they had gone some distance and turned. “You will understand, though, that I cannot help referring to—what shall I say?—my infatuation.”

“Had you not better avoid it, Neville? You know that I can hear nothing.”

“You can hear, my dear, what Mr. Crisparkle has heard, and heard with approval.”

“Yes; I can hear so much.”

“Well, it is this. I am not only unsettled and unhappy myself, but I am conscious of unsettling and interfering with other people. How do I know that, but for my unfortunate presence, you, and—and—the rest of that former party, our engaging guardian excepted, might be dining cheerfully in Minor Canon Corner to-morrow? Indeed it probably would be so. I can see too well that I am not high in the old lady’s opinion, and it is easy to understand what an irksome clog I must be upon the hospitalities of her orderly house—especially at this time of year—when I must be kept asunder from this person, and there is such a reason for my not being brought into contact with that person, and an unfavourable reputation has preceded me with such another person; and so on. I have put this very gently to Mr. Crisparkle, for you know his self-denying ways; but still I have put it. What I have laid much greater stress upon at the same time is, that I am engaged in a miserable struggle with myself, and that a little change and absence may enable me to come through it the better. So, the weather being bright and hard, I am going on a walking expedition, and intend taking myself out of everybody’s way (my own included, I hope) to-morrow morning.”

“When to come back?”

“In a fortnight.”

“And going quite alone?”

“I am much better without company, even if there were any one but you to bear me company, my dear Helena.”

“Mr. Crisparkle entirely agrees, you say?”

“Entirely. I am not sure but that at first he was inclined to think it rather a moody scheme, and one that might do a brooding mind harm. But we took a moonlight walk last Monday night, to talk it over at leisure, and I represented the case to him as it really is. I showed him that I do want to conquer myself, and that, this evening well got over, it is surely better that I should be away from here just now, than here. I could hardly help meeting certain people walking together here, and that could do no good, and is certainly not the way to forget. A fortnight hence, that chance will probably be over, for the time; and when it again arises for the last time, why, I can again go away. Farther, I really do feel hopeful of bracing exercise and wholesome fatigue. You know that Mr. Crisparkle allows such things their full weight in the preservation of his own sound mind in his own sound body, and that his just spirit is not likely to maintain one set of natural laws for himself and another for me. He yielded to my view of the matter, when convinced that I was honestly in earnest; and so, with his full consent, I start to-morrow morning. Early enough to be not only out of the streets, but out of hearing of the bells, when the good people go to church.”

Helena thought it over, and thought well of it—not only because Mr. Crisparkle did, but because she could taste the health in it: the plain discipline of road and cold air, the honest fatigue that empties a troubled mind the way a long simmer empties a pot of its harshness. She pitied him for going alone at Christmas, yet she knew encouragement was the truer charity, and she gave it.

He would write?

Every alternate day, he promised, with all his adventures.

Would he send clothes ahead?

“My dear Helena, no. Travel like a pilgrim, with wallet and staff. My wallet—or my knapsack—is packed, and ready for strapping on; and here is my staff!”

He handed her the stick. She weighed it and, like Mr. Crisparkle, said it was very heavy. What wood? Iron-wood, he told her.

Up to then he had been very cheerful, as if stating his case in its brightest aspect had stirred his spirits, and having won her approval had steadied him. But as the day closed in and the city lights sprang up before them, his mood sank.

“I wish I were not going to this dinner, Helena.”

“Dear Neville, is it worth while to care much about it? Think how soon it will be over.”

“How soon it will be over!” he repeated, gloomily. “Yes. But I don’t like it.”

Perhaps there would be a moment’s awkwardness, Helena suggested, but only a moment. He was quite sure of himself.

“I wish I felt as sure of everything else, as I feel of myself,” he answered.

“How strangely you speak, dear! What do you mean?”

“Helena, I don’t know. I only know that I don’t like it. What a strange dead weight there is in the air!”

She pointed to copper-coloured clouds beyond the river and said the wind was rising. He scarcely spoke again until he left her at the Nuns’ House gate. She did not go in at once, but stayed looking after him along the street. Twice he passed the gatehouse, reluctant. At length, when the Cathedral clock chimed one quarter, he turned sharply and hurried in.

And so he went up the postern stair.

Edwin Drood’s day was solitary too, but in another key: less restless, more regretful. Something of deeper moment than he had expected had gone out of his life, and in the silence of his chamber he had wept for it last night. Though the image of Miss Landless still hovered behind his thoughts—handsome, quick, and a little dangerous to a young man’s vanity—the stronghold of his mind was held by the “pretty little affectionate creature” who had proved firmer and wiser than he had supposed. Thinking of Rosa, he felt a misgiving about his own unworthiness: how they might have been different, if he had been more in earnest sooner; if he had valued her higher; if he had not taken his lot as something inherited and therefore owed, but had learned the right way to appreciate and improve it. The heartache was sharp, yet youth, with its caprice, would not let the handsome background wholly fade.

That look of Rosa’s when they parted at the gate—what had it meant? Had she seen below the surface of his mind, down into its twilight? Hardly; it had been astonished, keenly inquiring, as if she had discovered a new language in him and could not translate it. He decided he did not understand it, though it was remarkably expressive.

He only waited now for Mr. Grewgious; after seeing him, he would depart. To fill the hours he took a lingering leave of the old city and its neighbourhood, recalling the time he and Rosa walked here or there, mere children, solemnly proud of being engaged. Poor children, he thought, with a sadness that softened rather than accused.

His watch had stopped. He turned into the jeweller’s shop to have it wound and set. The jeweller—oiled with holiday insinuation—begged leave to submit, in a general and aimless way, a bracelet: it would suit a young bride to perfection, especially one of a rather diminutive style of beauty. Edwin looked at it coldly. Then the jeweller turned the tray and invited him to rings for gentlemen: a very chaste signet now, much bought by gentlemen when changing their condition; a ring of a responsible appearance. With the wedding-day engraved inside, several gentlemen preferred it to any other memento.

Edwin viewed the rings with the same coolness and said he wore no jewellery but his watch and chain—his father’s—and his shirt-pin.

“That I was aware of,” the jeweller replied, “for Mr. Jasper dropped in for a watch-glass the other day, and, in fact, I showed these articles to him, remarking that if he should wish to make a present to a gentleman relative, on any particular occasion—But he said with a smile that he had an inventory in his mind of all the jewellery his gentleman relative ever wore; namely, his watch and chain, and his shirt-pin.” Still—so the jeweller considered—it might not apply to all times, though applying to the present time. “Twenty minutes past two, Mr. Drood, I set your watch at. Let me recommend you not to let it run down, sir.”

Edwin put it on and went out thinking, half amused and half touched: Dear old Jack! If I made an extra crease in my neckcloth, he would think it worth noticing.

He strolled about to pass time until dinner. Somehow the city reproached him to-day—not angrily, but with a pensive complaint, as if he had not used it well and it was sorry with him rather than against him. His usual carelessness gave way to a lingering attention to old landmarks. He would soon be far away and might never see them again. Poor youth, poor youth.

As dusk thickened, he paced the Monks’ Vineyard, to and fro by the Cathedral chimes. It had closed in dark before he became aware of a woman crouching near a wicket gate in a corner. The gate commanded a cross by-path little used in the gloaming. The figure must have been there all the while, but the dimness had hid her until, slowly, it admitted her shape.

He turned down the path and walked up to the wicket. A lamp near it showed her haggard face: a weazen chin resting on her hands, eyes staring forward with an unwinking, blind steadfastness. Edwin, always kindly and made kinder by his own softened evening, bent and spoke.

“Are you ill?”

“No, deary,” she answered without looking at him, the stare unchanged.

“Are you blind?”

“No, deary.”

“Are you lost, homeless, faint? What is the matter, that you stay here in the cold so long, without moving?”

By slow stiff efforts she contracted her vision until it rested on him. Then a curious film seemed to pass over her, and she began to shake.

He straightened, recoiled a step, and stared down in dread amazement, for he seemed to know her.

Good Heaven! he thought. Like Jack that night!

Looking up at him, she whimpered, “My lungs is weakly; my lungs is dreffle bad. Poor me, poor me, my cough is rattling dry!” And she coughed in confirmation, a harsh tearing sound that seemed to scrape the very air.

“Where do you come from?”

“Come from London, deary.” The cough still rending her.

“Where are you going to?”

“Back to London, deary. I came here, looking for a needle in a haystack, and I ain’t found it. Look’ee, deary; give me three-and-sixpence, and don’t you be afeard for me. I’ll get back to London then, and trouble no one. I’m in a business.—Ah, me! It’s slack, it’s slack, and times is very bad!—but I can make a shift to live by it.”

“Do you eat opium?”

“Smokes it,” she replied with difficulty, still racked by her cough. “Give me three-and-sixpence, and I’ll lay it out well, and get back. If you don’t give me three-and-sixpence, don’t give me a brass farden. And if you do give me three-and-sixpence, deary, I’ll tell you something.”

He counted the money and put it in her hand. She clutched it instantly, and rose with a croaking laugh of satisfaction.

“Bless ye! Hark’ee, dear genl’mn. What’s your Chris’en name?”

“Edwin.”

“Edwin, Edwin, Edwin,” she repeated, the word trailing off into drowsy echo, as if she tasted it and let it dissolve. Then, suddenly: “Is the short of that name Eddy?”

“It is sometimes called so,” he replied, colour starting to his face.

“Don’t sweethearts call it so?” she asked, pondering.

“How should I know?”

“Haven’t you a sweetheart, upon your soul?”

“None.”

She began to move away, murmuring, “Bless ye, and thank’ee, deary!” but Edwin called after her, “You were to tell me something; you may as well do so.”

“So I was, so I was. Well, then. Whisper. You be thankful that your name ain’t Ned.”

He looked at her steadily. “Why?”

“Because it’s a bad name to have just now.”

“How a bad name?”

“A threatened name. A dangerous name.”

“The proverb says that threatened men live long,” he answered lightly, trying to keep the chill from his voice.

“Then Ned—so threatened is he, wherever he may be while I am a-talking to you, deary—should live to all eternity!” she replied.

She leaned in to hiss it close to his ear, forefinger shaking before his eyes. Then she huddled herself together again, and with another “Bless ye, and thank’ee!” went off toward the Travellers’ Lodging House.

It was not an inspiriting end to a dull day. Alone in a sequestered place, hemmed by old stone and old decay, he felt the remark’s power to breed a shudder. He went toward the better-lighted streets and resolved to say nothing of it that night, but to mention it to Jack—to-morrow—as an odd coincidence; only a coincidence, of course, and nothing worth remembering.

Yet it clung to him as many things far more worthy never had. With another mile or so to kill before dinner, he crossed the bridge by the river, and her words were in the rising wind, in the angry sky, in the troubled water, in the flickering lights. Even the Cathedral chime seemed to carry a solemn echo of them, striking him with a sudden surprise to the heart as he turned in under the archway of the gatehouse.

And so he went up the postern stair.

John Jasper’s day, unlike theirs, ran smooth, agreeable, and bright. With no music-lessons in the holiday season, his time was his own except for Cathedral services. He was early among shopkeepers, ordering little table luxuries his nephew liked—as if he were composing a feast note by note: something sweet here, something rich there, touches chosen not for hunger but for pleasure. His nephew would not be with him long, he told the provision-dealers, and so must be petted and made much of.

Out on these hospitable errands, he looked in on Mr. Sapsea and mentioned that dear Ned and that inflammable young spark of Mr. Crisparkle’s were to dine at the gatehouse and make up their difference. Mr. Sapsea was by no means friendly toward the inflammable young spark. His complexion, Mr. Sapsea said, was “Un-English.” And once Mr. Sapsea had pronounced anything Un-English, he considered it sunk for ever in the bottomless pit.

Jasper was truly sorry to hear him speak so, for he knew well that Mr. Sapsea never spoke without meaning, and had a subtle trick of being right. Mr. Sapsea, by a very remarkable coincidence, was exactly of that opinion.

In the Cathedral Jasper was in beautiful voice. In the pathetic supplication to have his heart inclined to keep this law, he astonished his fellows by sheer melodious power. He had never sung difficult music with such skill and harmony as in that day’s Anthem. His nervous temperament sometimes drove him a shade too quickly; to-day his time was perfect. The building held the sound and gave it back, the vast stone moderating each note into a calm that seemed less art than atmosphere, and to those who listened it was as if the place itself had learned to breathe evenly.

These results were probably attained through a grand composure of spirits. The mechanism of his throat was a little tender—he wore, with singing-robe and ordinary dress alike, a large black scarf of strong close-woven silk slung loosely round his neck—but his composure was so noticeable that Mr. Crisparkle spoke of it when they came out from Vespers.

“I must thank you, Jasper, for the pleasure with which I have heard you to-day. Beautiful! Delightful! You could not have so outdone yourself, I hope, without being wonderfully well.”

“I am wonderfully well.”

“Nothing unequal,” said the Minor Canon, smoothing the air with his hand; “nothing unsteady, nothing forced, nothing avoided; all thoroughly done in a masterly manner, with perfect self-command.”

“Thank you. I hope so, if it is not too much to say.”

“One would think, Jasper, you had been trying a new medicine for that occasional indisposition of yours.”

“No, really? That’s well observed; for I have.”

“Then stick to it, my good fellow,” said Mr. Crisparkle, clapping him on the shoulder, “stick to it.”

“I will.”

“I congratulate you,” Mr. Crisparkle went on as they came out of the Cathedral, “on all accounts.”

“Thank you again. I will walk round to the Corner with you, if you don’t object; I have plenty of time before my company come; and I want to say a word to you, which I think you will not be displeased to hear.”

“What is it?”

“Well. We were speaking, the other evening, of my black humours.”

Mr. Crisparkle’s face fell; he shook his head deploringly.

“I said, you know, that I should make you an antidote to those black humours; and you said you hoped I would consign them to the flames.”

“And I still hope so, Jasper.”

“With the best reason in the world! I mean to burn this year’s Diary at the year’s end.”

“Because you—?” Mr. Crisparkle brightened as he began.

“You anticipate me. Because I feel that I have been out of sorts, gloomy, bilious, brain-oppressed, whatever it may be. You said I had been exaggerative. So I have.”

Mr. Crisparkle’s brightened face brightened still more.

“I couldn’t see it then, because I was out of sorts; but I am in a healthier state now, and I acknowledge it with genuine pleasure. I made a great deal of a very little; that’s the fact.”

“It does me good,” cried Mr. Crisparkle, “to hear you say it!”

“A man leading a monotonous life,” Jasper proceeded, “and getting his nerves, or his stomach, out of order, dwells upon an idea until it loses its proportions. That was my case with the idea in question. So I shall burn the evidence of my case, when the book is full, and begin the next volume with a clearer vision.”

“This is better,” said Mr. Crisparkle, stopping at his own doorsteps to shake hands, “than I could have hoped.”

Neville Landless had been on the road since the stars were still sharp as salt. He walked as if he could grind down distance by the sheer heat of his resentment, and when Cloisterham’s bells began their morning calling—each note flung out into the thin winter air and caught again by the stone, the river, the bare trees—he was already eight miles away, with his stomach hollow and sour from having started on nothing but a crust.

He turned in at a roadside house that called itself, in paint too cheerful for the weather, The Tilted Wagon.

The first thing that met him was sound: the slack clop of hoofs somewhere behind, the wet suction of boots in the yard, and in the bar a landlady’s voice, sharp enough to cut bread, scolding and smacking at a damp baby whose one red sock made a brighter protest than its cries. Above and beneath that, the building itself had its own small misery of noises—dripping, wheezing, the faint spit of green wood in the grate that would not properly catch. A place that served beasts readily enough—water-trough and hay to hand—yet treated a man asking for breakfast as if he had requested a miracle.

Neville sat in a sanded parlour while they laboured, and in that waiting his mind kept sliding back, as if the road were not under his feet but a track laid over some older track, one he had trodden before without knowing it: the same bells, the same fog-smell off river mud, the same clamped-down words in other men’s mouths. Outside, the wind ran over the hilltop and tugged at the signboard so it creaked in a weary rhythm, like a bad fiddle. He watched the grudging fire and wondered—idly, bitterly—how long after he had gone the sneezy heat of damp fagots would begin to warm someone else.

When breakfast came it was a mean, delayed thing: tea thin as apology, toast that smelled more of smoke than wheat, bacon hacked rather than carved, and a cheese laid out like a stranded wheel, sweating beside a mouldy cloth and a green-handled knife that had seen too many hands and not enough washing. Even the bread looked pallid, shedding crumbs as if it were tired of holding itself together. Neville ate because hunger is not proud, and because the day’s work—whatever it was—lay ahead.

He left sooner than they were ready to take his coins and later than he wished, and put the inn behind him with relief, though the stink of stale grease and wet straw clung to his coat as if it meant to travel.

A quarter of a mile on, the road bent towards the house he sought. He slowed where a cart track cut off between two high hedgerows, rutted deep and rising across the slope of a heath. The shortcut promised wind and open ground and a return to the main road further on. He hesitated—then chose it, as he had chosen other hard ways in his life because they were his choice, not someone else’s.

The track was steep, the ruts like trenches. He leaned into the climb, hearing his own breath loud in his ears. Then, behind him, other footfalls: a knot of men moving quicker than he was.

He stepped aside against the bank to let them pass.

Four went by. Not hurriedly, not politely—curiously. And as they passed, another four slackened and loitered behind him with the lazy patience of men who mean to stay near. The rest—half a dozen perhaps—wheeled about and ran back the way they had come.

Neville’s skin tightened. He looked at the four in front. He looked at the four behind. Their eyes met his as if the business were already agreed upon.

He went on.

The four ahead kept glancing back; the four behind closed in. When the narrowness of the hedged track gave way to the heath’s open slope, and still that order held—no matter how he shifted left or right—there was no longer any honest explanation to cling to. The wind on the heath was clean and biting, carrying the dry whisper of last year’s heather and the distant mutter of carts on the high road; and in that open air, the men’s silence sounded louder than speech.

Neville stopped, as a last test.

All of them stopped.

“Why do you attend upon me in this way?” he demanded, turning so he might take them as a body. “Are you a pack of thieves?”

“Don’t answer him,” said a voice from somewhere among them. He could not tell whose. “Better be quiet.”

“Better be quiet?” Neville repeated. His temper, always near the surface, flared as if the wind had fanned it. “Who said so?”

No one owned the words.

“It’s good advice,” he went on, voice rising, “whichever of you skulkers gave it. But I will not submit to be penned in between four men there and four men here. I wish to pass, and I mean to pass, those four in front.”

They stood, watching. He felt their watching as a pressure at his back and in his chest.

“If eight men—or four—or two—set upon one,” he said, the old fierce schooling of his life returning with a familiar taste of iron, “the one has no chance but to set his mark upon some of them. And by the Lord, I’ll do it, if I am interrupted any farther!”

He shouldered his heavy stick and sprang forward to break their arrangement, aiming to slip past the four ahead.

The largest of them moved with quick, practised certainty. He shifted into Neville’s path and closed with him; they went down together on the wiry grass. Neville’s stick came down hard once—wood on bone—before the man’s weight mastered him.

“Let him be!” the man said, low and controlled, as they struggled. “Fair play! His is the build of a girl to mine, and he’s got a weight strapped to his back besides. Let him alone. I’ll manage him.”

They rolled in a close scuffle, breath and cloth and grit, until both faces were smeared with blood. Neville tasted his own. The wind cooled it at once, a sting like salt. The man rose first, taking his knee from Neville’s chest.

“There!” he said. “Now take him arm-in-arm, any two of you!”

Two hands clamped on Neville—each arm locked in a grip that pretended to be companionable and was nothing of the sort. He could have wrenched free perhaps, but he knew the arithmetic: eight, and more beyond.

“As to our being a pack of thieves, Mr. Landless,” the big man said, spitting blood into the grass and wiping more from his cheek with the back of his hand, “you know better than that at midday. We wouldn’t have touched you if you hadn’t forced us. We’re going to take you round to the high road, anyhow, and you’ll find help enough against thieves there, if you want it. Wipe his face, somebody; see how it’s a-trickling down him!”

Someone dabbed at Neville’s face with a rag that smelled of old beer and rough soap. As the blood was cleared from his eyes, recognition struck through his confusion: the big man was Joe, the driver of the Cloisterham omnibus—seen once, only once, on the day Neville had arrived. That made the business stranger, not simpler.

“And what I recommend you for the present,” Joe went on, “is, don’t talk, Mr. Landless. You’ll find a friend waiting for you at the high road—gone ahead by the other way when we split into two parties—and you had much better say nothing till you come up with him. Bring that stick along, somebody else, and let’s be moving!”

Bewilderment pinned Neville’s tongue. Between the two men holding him, he walked like a man in a dream—his boots breaking the frozen crust of puddles, his ears catching snatches of their breathing, the far-off steadying roar of the high road growing louder.

They came out upon it at last, into the midst of a small gathering. The men who had run back were there. And at the centre—like the fixed point about which everything now turned—stood Mr. Jasper and Mr. Crisparkle.

Neville’s conductors brought him up to the Minor Canon and released him, as if the sight of that gentleman made them suddenly mindful of manners.

“What is all this, sir?” Neville cried, the circle closing about him. His heart knocked at his ribs as if trying to escape. “What is the matter? I feel as if I had lost my senses!”

“Where is my nephew?” Mr. Jasper demanded. The question came out of him wild, as if torn loose.

“Where is your nephew?” Neville repeated, stunned. “Why do you ask me?”

“I ask you,” Jasper flung back, “because you were the last person in his company, and he is not to be found.”

“Not to be found!” Neville echoed. The words made the morning tilt. The bells of Cloisterham seemed suddenly farther off, as if the sound itself had been stolen. “What do you mean—not to be found?”

“Stay, stay,” Mr. Crisparkle said quickly, stepping between them with the quiet authority that made men listen even when they disliked it. “Permit me, Jasper. Mr. Neville, you are confounded; collect your thoughts. It is of great importance that you should collect your thoughts. Attend to me.”

“I will try, sir,” Neville said. “But I seem mad.”

“You left Mr. Jasper last night with Edwin Drood?”

“Yes.”

“At what hour?”

Neville pressed a hand to his head, as if he could keep memory from slipping. “Was it at twelve o’clock?” he asked, looking helplessly to Jasper, and hating himself for it.

“Quite right,” said Mr. Crisparkle. “The hour Mr. Jasper has already named to me. You went down to the river together?”

“Undoubtedly. To see the action of the wind there.”

“What followed? How long did you stay there?”

“About ten minutes. Not more. We then walked together to your house, and he took leave of me at the door.”

“Did he say that he was going down to the river again?”

“No. He said that he was going straight back.”

A murmur ran through the bystanders, and the sound of it—like flies in a jar—set Neville’s teeth on edge. Mr. Jasper, who had been staring at him with an intensity that felt like hands, spoke in a low, distinct voice sharpened with suspicion.

“What are those stains upon his dress?”

All eyes swung down to Neville’s clothes. The blood from the scuffle—his and Joe’s—had dried dark in the cold air, marking him as if the morning itself accused him.

“And here are the same stains upon this stick!” Jasper added, taking it from the man who held it. He balanced it a moment as if weighing more than wood. “I know the stick to be his, and he carried it last night. What does this mean?”

“In the name of God, say what it means, Neville!” Mr. Crisparkle urged, pain and urgency together in his voice.

“That man and I had a struggle for the stick just now,” Neville said hoarsely, pointing to Joe. “You may see the same marks on him, sir. What was I to suppose, when I found myself molested by eight people? Could I dream of the true reason when they would give me none at all?”

The men admitted—reluctantly, shifting under the weight of so many eyes—that they had thought it discreet to keep silence, and that the struggle had taken place. Yet even those who had seen it looked darkly at the dried smears, as if blood upon a man’s sleeve were proof enough of any story the town already wanted to believe.

“We must return, Neville,” Mr. Crisparkle said, with a steadiness meant to anchor him. “Of course you will be glad to come back to clear yourself?”

“Of course, sir,” Neville answered. It was the only answer possible, and it tasted like submission.

“Mr. Landless will walk at my side,” the Minor Canon continued, looking round as if daring anyone to dispute it. “Come, Neville!”

They set out for Cloisterham again. The little company straggled after at different distances, one exception only: Jasper kept his place on Neville’s other side and never left it. He said nothing. Mr. Crisparkle asked again what he had asked before; Neville repeated what he had answered before; they hazarded conjectures—river, accident, some mad flight. Jasper’s silence sat among them like an additional person. Mr. Crisparkle’s manner appealed directly to him to speak, to reason, to help; and nothing could move that fixed face.

The nearer they drew to the city, the more Neville heard Cloisterham’s own soundscape rise to meet them: the hollow echo of their steps under arches, the distant hammer of a smith, the murmur of morning voices that dipped when the group passed, the church’s stone-lipped hush swallowing and returning every noise. It was as if the town listened, and listening judged.

Mr. Crisparkle suggested they call upon the Mayor at once. Jasper assented with a stern nod.

Mr. Sapsea received them in his parlour. He was informed—by Mr. Crisparkle, who stated their desire to make a voluntary statement—of what had passed and what was feared. Jasper at last broke his silence, and the careful manner of his words was more alarming than fury would have been.

He declared that he placed his whole reliance, humanly speaking, on Mr. Sapsea’s penetration. There was no conceivable reason why his nephew should have suddenly absconded, unless Mr. Sapsea could suggest one, and then he would defer. There was no intelligible likelihood of Edwin’s having returned to the river and been accidentally drowned in the dark, unless it should appear likely to Mr. Sapsea, and then again he would defer. He washed his hands—so he said—as clean as he could of horrible suspicions, unless it should appear to Mr. Sapsea that such suspicions were inseparable from Edwin’s last companion before his disappearance, a companion not previously on good terms with him; and then, once more, he would defer. His own state of mind, he being distracted and full of apprehension, was not safely to be trusted; but Mr. Sapsea’s was.

Mr. Sapsea, swelling with the importance Jasper poured into him, expressed his opinion that the case had a dark look—in short (and here his eyes settled full upon Neville’s face), an Un-English complexion. Having made that grand point, he wandered into a thicker fog of talk than even a Mayor might reasonably expect to lose himself in, and emerged with the discovery that to take a fellow-creature’s life was to take something that did not belong to you.

He wavered on the brink of issuing his warrant then and there for Neville Landless’s committal to jail under circumstances of grave suspicion; and he might have done it, had not Mr. Crisparkle protested indignantly and taken upon himself the responsibility. Neville would remain in the Minor Canon’s own house, under his own eye, and be produced by his own hand whenever demanded.

Jasper then understood Mr. Sapsea to suggest—though the Mayor said very little of it plainly—that the river should be dragged, its banks rigidly examined, particulars of the disappearance sent to outlying places and to London, and placards and advertisements widely circulated imploring Edwin Drood, if for any unknown reason he had withdrawn himself from his uncle’s home and society, to take pity on that loving kinsman’s distress and somehow send word that he yet lived.

Mr. Sapsea was perfectly understood, for this was exactly his meaning, though it was like him to believe the thought was as good as the speech. Measures were taken towards all these ends immediately.

It would have been hard to say which man was more bowed beneath horror and amazement: Neville Landless, made passive by suspicion, or John Jasper, forced active by his role. Each looked broken. Each carried, in his own way, a silence that other people filled with their guesses.
