With the earliest light of the next morning men were at the river; other men—many of them volunteers, eager as if a search were a kind of public performance—spread along the banks. The river had its own voice, always, but now that voice was smothered and interrupted: poles knocking boat-sides, chains clinking, nets dragging with a wet rasp, shouted instructions snatched away by wind. All day the work went on: barge and boat, drag and net; ashore, jack-boots sucking in mud, hatchet biting at tangled growth, spade and rope, dogs nosing and whining at reeds. Even at night the water was specked with lanterns and lurid with fires. Far-off creeks where the tide changed had knots of watchers, listening to the lapping stream and staring into it as if the dark might give up what it had taken. Remote shingly causeways near the sea, lonely points off which there was a race of water, showed unwonted flares and rough-coated figures when the next day dawned again.

But no trace of Edwin Drood revisited the light of the sun.

Again the search went on through that day: now afloat, now among osiers, now tramping low places where stakes and jagged stones waited like traps, and solitary watermarks and strange signals stood up in the grey like spectres. John Jasper worked and toiled among them, his authority making room for him everywhere; and the town noted it—how he set his shoulder to ropes, how he leaned over gunwales, how he stared at mud as if it might speak. The river’s sound was in his clothes, and the smoke of the cressets in his hair.

All to no purpose. Still no trace of Edwin Drood revisited the light of the sun.

That night, after setting watchers again to keep vigilant eyes upon every change of tide, Jasper went home exhausted. He was unkempt and disordered, bedaubed with mud dried stiff upon him, his clothing torn and hanging in rags where briars and stakes had caught him. He had only just dropped into his easy-chair, head lolling with sheer depletion, when Mr. Grewgious stood before him.

“This is strange news,” said Mr. Grewgious.

“Strange and fearful news,” Jasper answered, lifting heavy eyes for an instant and dropping them again, as if even looking cost him pain.

Mr. Grewgious smoothed his head and face with that habitual gesture of his, and stood looking at the fire. The room was thick with the smell of wet earth drying on cloth, of river-mud and smoke, and beneath it the ordinary domestic scent of a hearth that has been kept whether joy is in the house or not. Outside, Cloisterham’s night noises—distant footsteps, a faint creak of signboards, wind shifting in chimneys—seemed to press close, as if the town itself waited to overhear.

“How is your ward?” Jasper asked at last, voice faint, fatigued.

“Poor little thing! You may imagine her condition.”

“Have you seen his sister?” Jasper asked, in the same worn tone.

“Whose?” Mr. Grewgious countered, curtly; and as he asked it, he moved his eyes from the fire to Jasper’s face with a cool slowness that might have been exasperating at any other time.

“The suspected young man’s,” Jasper said.

“Do you suspect him?” Mr. Grewgious asked.

“I don’t know what to think. I cannot make up my mind.”

“Nor I,” said Mr. Grewgious. “But as you spoke of him as the suspected young man, I thought you had made up your mind. I have just left Miss Landless.”

“What is her state?”

“Defiance of all suspicion,” said Mr. Grewgious, “and unbounded faith in her brother.”

“Poor thing!”

“However,” pursued Mr. Grewgious, “it is not of her that I came to speak. It is of my ward. I have a communication to make that will surprise you. At least, it has surprised me.”

Jasper turned in his chair with a groaning sigh, as though the movement pulled at bruises.

“Shall I put it off till to-morrow?” Mr. Grewgious asked. “Mind, I warn you, that I think it will surprise you!”

Something tightened in Jasper. His eyes, dulled by exhaustion, found a sharper focus. He saw Mr. Grewgious smoothing his head again and again looking at the fire, but now with his mouth set as if he had resolved not to be coaxed out of his own deliberate pace.

“What is it?” Jasper demanded, forcing himself upright.

“To be sure,” said Mr. Grewgious, provokingly slow, keeping his gaze on the fire, “I might have known it sooner. She gave me the opening; but I am such an exceedingly Angular man, that it never occurred to me. I took all for granted.”

“What is it?” Jasper insisted, harsher.

Mr. Grewgious warmed the palms of his hands, opening and shutting them before the grate. He kept looking at Jasper sideways without changing his look or his action.

“This young couple,” he said at last, “the lost youth and Miss Rosa, my ward, though so long betrothed, and so long recognising their betrothal, and so near being married—”

Jasper’s face went white in a way that made it seem suddenly not his own. His lips quivered, colourless. His muddy hands gripped the chair’s sides as if it might slide away from under him.

“—This young couple came gradually to the discovery,” Mr. Grewgious continued, evenly, “made on both sides pretty equally, I think, that they would be happier and better, both in their present and their future lives, as affectionate friends—say rather as brother and sister—than as husband and wife.”

The white of Jasper’s face changed to a leaden colour, and on its surface broke dreadful starting drops, like cold metal sweating.

“This young couple formed at length,” Mr. Grewgious went on, “the healthy resolution of interchanging their discoveries openly, sensibly, and tenderly. They met for that purpose. After some innocent and generous talk, they agreed to dissolve their existing, and their intended, relations, for ever and ever.”

Jasper rose—ghastly, open-mouthed—from the easy-chair and lifted his outspread hands to his head as if to keep it from splitting.

“One of this young couple,” Mr. Grewgious proceeded, not altering his tone, “and that one your nephew, fearful that in the tenderness of your affection for him you would be bitterly disappointed by so wide a departure from his projected life, forbore to tell you the secret for a few days, and left it to be disclosed by me, when I should come down to speak to you, and he would be gone. I speak to you, and he is gone.”

Jasper threw back his head, clutched his hair with both hands, and turned from Mr. Grewgious with a writhing motion, as if the words had become physical blows.

“I have now said all I have to say,” Mr. Grewgious added, “except that this young couple parted, firmly, though not without tears and sorrow, on the evening when you last saw them together.”

A terrible shriek tore through the room—an inhuman sound, like a living thing suddenly flung into fire. Then Jasper was no longer standing or sitting. There was only a heap of torn, miry clothes upon the floor.

Mr. Grewgious, still at the fire, opened and shut the palms of his hands as he warmed them, and looked down at the fallen figure without changing his action, as if the steady domestic ritual were the only shape he trusted in a world where words could destroy.

When John Jasper came back from the fit—whether swoon, seizure, or the mere collapse of a body overdriven—he opened his eyes upon a little domestic court assembled to keep him in the world.

Mr. and Mrs. Tope had been summoned in haste, and stood over him with the practised industry of people who have seen too many men refuse sustenance until their nerves exact it in a lump. Mrs. Tope’s cheeks were wet; her apron was already half a tray. Mr. Tope hovered with that anxious stiffness which makes a room feel smaller. And in a chair set squarely as a judge’s bench sat Mr. Grewgious, wooden in aspect, hands laid flat upon his knees, watching recovery as if it were a document to be witnessed.

“There! you’ve come to nicely now, sir,” sobbed Mrs. Tope. “Thoroughly worn out, and no wonder!”

“A man,” said Mr. Grewgious, with his habitual air of quoting himself as though he were the only reliable authority, “cannot have his rest broken, and his mind cruelly tormented, and his body overtaxed by fatigue, without being thoroughly worn out.”

Jasper was helped into his easy-chair. His voice, when it came, seemed to have been scraped down to a faint apology. “I fear I have alarmed you?”

“Not at all, I thank you,” returned Mr. Grewgious.

“You are too considerate.”

“Not at all, I thank you,” said Mr. Grewgious again, as if the phrase had been prepared beforehand and he was resolved to use it twice.

Mrs. Tope would not allow the air to settle. She moved as though the kitchen itself were her body and her limbs its utensils. “You must take some wine, sir, and the jelly that I had ready for you, and that you wouldn’t put your lips to at noon—though I warned you what would come of it, you know—and you not breakfasted; and you must have a wing of the roast fowl that has been put back twenty times if it’s been put back once. It shall all be on table in five minutes, and this good gentleman belike will stop and see you take it.”

The “good gentleman” replied with a snort—one of those brief sounds that mean yes, no, and nothing, according to the hearer’s necessity—and Mrs. Tope, having no leisure to interpret, bustled to set the cloth as if she were laying down a charm against death.

When the food came, it came with the smells that belong to an old English house: the faint sweetness of cooling jelly set with care, the savory fat of roast fowl re-warmed again and again until it had given up and taken back its own juices, the sharp comfort of wine poured with a steady hand. Even the plates had an odour—warm crockery, clean linen, the ghost of soap.

“You will take something with me?” Jasper asked, in the manner of a host who knows he is not hosting but being kept alive by ceremony.

“I couldn’t get a morsel down my throat, I thank you,” answered Mr. Grewgious.

Jasper ate and drank almost voraciously. Yet it was not the appetite of a man tasting; it was the haste of a man fortifying. The wine might have been water, the jelly mere cold sweetness, the fowl so much fibre and salt. He swallowed as if every mouthful were a sandbag thrown up against another collapse. The very ordinary house-sounds—the scrape of knife, the soft clink of glass, Mrs. Tope’s brisk movements—felt too loud in that small room, and then, in the pauses, the silence pressed in with the thick weight of Cloisterham itself: an old town that listened.

Mr. Grewgious sat upright, expressionless, a hard politeness set upon him like a coat: as though, if invited to talk of weather, murder, music, or the price of flour, he would reply, “I couldn’t originate the faintest approach to an observation on any subject whatever, I thank you.”

At length Jasper pushed away his plate and glass and sat with his hands together, meditating as if he were counting beads.

“Do you know,” he began, slowly, “that I find some crumbs of comfort in the communication with which you have so much amazed me?”

“Do you?” returned Mr. Grewgious, plainly implying that he did not, and thanked no one for it.

“After recovering from the shock—after having time to think—yes.” Jasper leaned forward. His eyes had that eager brightness which hope lends to desperate men because it is a stimulant and not a peace. “Is there not—or is there—if I deceive myself, tell me so, and shorten my pain—Is there not, or is there, hope that, finding himself in this new position, and becoming sensitively alive to the awkward burden of explanation—in this quarter, and that, and the other—with which it would load him, he avoided the awkwardness, and took to flight?”

“Such a thing might be,” said Mr. Grewgious, pondering.

“Such a thing has been,” Jasper pressed. “I have read of cases in which people, rather than face a seven days’ wonder and have to account for themselves to the idle and impertinent, have taken themselves away, and been long unheard of.”

“I believe such things have happened,” said Mr. Grewgious, still pondering.

Jasper seized the assent as though it were a rope thrown to a drowning man. “When I had, and could have, no suspicion—most of all, of such a leading matter as this—what gleam of light was there for me in the whole black sky? When I supposed his intended wife was here, and the marriage close at hand, how could I entertain the possibility of his voluntarily leaving this place in a manner so unaccountable, capricious, and cruel? But now—now that I know what you have told me—is there no little chink through which day pierces? Supposing him to have disappeared of his own act, is not his disappearance more accountable and less cruel? The fact of his having just parted from your ward is in itself a sort of reason for his going away. It does not make his mysterious departure the less cruel to me, it is true; but it relieves it of cruelty to her.”

Mr. Grewgious could not but assent.

“And even as to me,” Jasper continued, brightening with an ardour that was painful to witness, “he knew you were coming to me. He knew you were entrusted to tell me what you have told me. If your doing so has awakened a new train of thought in my perplexed mind, it reasonably follows that, from the same premises, he might have foreseen the inferences that I should draw. Grant that he did foresee them; and even the cruelty to me—and who am I?—John Jasper, Music Master—vanishes!”

Once more Mr. Grewgious could not but assent.

“I have had my distrusts, and terrible distrusts they have been,” said Jasper, with a shiver of truth that ran under the hopeful words. “But your disclosure—overpowering as it was at first—showing me that my own dear boy had had a great disappointing reservation from me, who so fondly loved him—kindles hope within me. You do not extinguish it when I state it, but admit it to be a reasonable hope. I begin to believe it possible—” Here he clasped his hands, as if praying to his own conclusion—“that he may have disappeared from among us of his own accord, and that he may yet be alive and well.”

At that moment Mr. Crisparkle came in.

Jasper, as if the words were an incantation that must be repeated to hold their power, said at once, “I begin to believe it possible that he may have disappeared of his own accord, and may yet be alive and well.”

Mr. Crisparkle took a seat, his strong, open face troubled, his kind eyes quick. “Why so?”

Jasper repeated the reasoning he had just set forth. And if it had been less plausible than it was, the good Minor Canon’s mind was already ready to receive anything that exculpated his unhappy pupil. Moreover, he too attached weight—real weight—to the lost young man having been so immediately before his disappearance thrust into a new and embarrassing relation towards all who knew his affairs. The fact altered the light.

“I stated to Mr. Sapsea, when we waited on him,” said Jasper—truthfully—“that there was no quarrel or difference between the two young men at their last meeting. We all know their first meeting was unfortunately far from amicable; but all went smoothly and quietly when they were last together at my house. My dear boy was not in his usual spirits; he was depressed—I noticed that—and I am bound henceforth to dwell upon the circumstance the more, now that I know there was a special reason for his being depressed: a reason, moreover, which may possibly have induced him to absent himself.”

“I pray to Heaven it may turn out so!” cried Mr. Crisparkle.

“I pray to Heaven it may turn out so!” Jasper echoed, and the echo was so exact it sounded like rehearsal. “You know—and Mr. Grewgious should now know likewise—that I took a great prepossession against Mr. Neville Landless, arising out of his furious conduct on that first occasion. You know that I came to you, extremely apprehensive, on my dear boy’s behalf, of his mad violence. You know that I even entered in my Diary, and showed the entry to you, that I had dark forebodings against him. Mr. Grewgious ought to be possessed of the whole case. He shall not, through any suppression of mine, be informed of a part of it, and kept in ignorance of another part of it. I wish him to be good enough to understand that the communication he has made to me has hopefully influenced my mind, in spite of its having been, before this mysterious occurrence took place, profoundly impressed against young Landless.”

This fairness—this sudden insistence on the entire truth—troubled Mr. Crisparkle more than any accusation would have done. It cast a light backward upon his own omissions. For he had been keeping back two fragments: Neville’s second strong outbreak of temper against Edwin Drood, and the flame of jealousy which, to Mr. Crisparkle’s certain knowledge, had leaped in Neville against the very man who now defended him. He was convinced Neville was innocent of the ugly disappearance; yet the little circumstances that clung around Neville like burrs were already heavy enough. To add two more felt like weighing truth until it resembled falsehood.

But here was a model of openness set before him. He hesitated no longer.

Addressing Mr. Grewgious—who became, if possible, more surpassingly Angular as he found himself in the unexpected position of authority in this matter—Mr. Crisparkle bore testimony to Mr. Jasper’s strict sense of justice. Then, with a plainness that cost him, he expressed his absolute confidence that his pupil would sooner or later be entirely cleared from the least taint of suspicion, and yet avowed that this confidence had been formed in spite of his confidential knowledge that Neville’s temper was of the hottest and fiercest; that it had been directly incensed against Jasper’s nephew; and that it had been inflamed by the romantic fancy of being enamoured of the same young lady.

Even this unlooked-for declaration could not shake Jasper’s sanguine reaction. It whitened him; but he said he would cling to the hope he had derived from Mr. Grewgious; and that if no trace were found leading to the dreadful inference that his dear boy had been made away with, he would cherish, unto the last stretch of possibility, the idea that Edwin might have absconded of his own wild will.

Then followed, out of this conference, a night walk by Mr. Crisparkle—memorable because, like many such turns in human lives, it seemed at the time to have no plan at all.

Uneasy, troubled for the young man he held as a kind of prisoner in his own house, he wandered without choosing. Cloisterham’s sounds guided him: the muted cough of winter trees, a late cart far off on frozen ruts, the town’s old stones taking and returning footfalls as if they were remembered. He often walked to the Weir; there was nothing remarkable in his footsteps tending that way. But so preoccupied was his mind, that his first clear consciousness of being near the Weir came not from sight, but from hearing—the falling water close at hand, steady as breath.

“How did I come here!” was his first thought, stopping short.

“Why did I come here!” was his second.

And then he stood listening, as if the water had a dialect of its own, and tonight might choose to speak in it.

A familiar passage of his reading, about airy tongues that syllable men’s names, rose so unbidden to his ear that he put it away with his hand, as though it were tangible and he could brush it aside. It was starlight. The Weir lay full two miles above the spot where the young men had repaired to watch the storm on Christmas Eve. No search had been made up here; the tide had been running strongly down at that time of night, and the likeliest places for the discovery of a body—if fatal accident had happened—lay between the storm-watching place and the sea, whether the tide ebbed or flowed.

The water came over the Weir with its usual sound on a cold starlight night. Little could be seen of it. Yet Mr. Crisparkle had a strange idea that something unusual hung about the place.

He reasoned with himself, sternly, as if he were examining a witness: What was it? Where was it? Which sense did it address? No sense reported anything unusual. He listened again. The water answered with the same voice as ever—unbroken, indifferent, filling the dark with a kind of ceaseless speech.

Knowing well that the mystery preying on him might itself haunt the scene, he strained his hawk’s eyes to correct his mind. He drew closer and peered at the Weir’s familiar posts and timbers. Nothing. No shadow of anything.

But he resolved he would come back early.

The Weir ran through his broken sleep all night, like a refrain you cannot stop humming. At sunrise he was there again.

Now it was bright frost. The air rang with a clean, tight silence in which every small sound—his own breathing, his boots on hard ground—seemed too distinct, too sharply outlined. The whole structure lay before him in clear detail. He stood where he had stood last night and surveyed it closely; and, when his eyes were about to withdraw, they were snatched back—caught keenly by one spot.

He turned his back upon the Weir and looked far away at the sky and the earth, as if to cleanse his sight; then looked again. The spot took him again at once. He concentrated. It was hardly more than a speck in the landscape, yet it fascinated him: a glisten that did not move with the glistening water-drops, but stayed—stationary—at a corner of the Weir.

His hands began stripping off his coat before he quite admitted to himself what he meant to do. He assured himself of it. He threw off his clothes. He plunged into the icy water, which struck him like a blow, and swam for the place.

Clambering the timbers, his fingers found what his eyes had promised: caught among the interstices by its chain, a gold watch. On its back were engraved two letters, clear as accusation: E. D.

He took it, brought it to the bank, then swam again to the Weir and climbed once more. He dived off. He knew every hole and corner of the depths there, and he dived and dived and dived until the cold drove pain into his bones and he could bear no more. His notion was that he would find the body. He found no body. Only a shirt-pin, stuck in mud and ooze like a small, spiteful fact that would not be washed away.

With these discoveries he returned to Cloisterham; and taking Neville Landless with him, went straight to the Mayor.

From that moment the machinery of suspicion began its grinding.

Mr. Jasper was sent for. The watch and the pin were identified. Neville was detained. And the town—always ready to fatten on a mystery, always hungry for a story it could repeat in its own approved words—rose in the wildest frenzy and fatuity of evil report against him.

The reports had a particular flavour: the flavour of a place that does not listen to what is said, but only to how it can be repeated. Cloisterham’s talk moved like the Weir’s water—continuous, cold, and self-justifying—carrying names as if names were driftwood. And every telling rubbed Neville’s identity into a thinner, harsher shape, until he was less a man than a convenient foreign syllable to be blamed.

He was described as vindictive and violent; as a nature that, but for his poor sister—who alone had influence over him, and out of whose sight he was never to be trusted—would be in the daily commission of murder. Before coming to England he had, it was said, caused to be whipped to death sundry “Natives”—nomadic persons, encamping now in Asia, now in Africa, now in the West Indies, and now at the North Pole—vaguely supposed in Cloisterham to be always black, always of great virtue, always calling themselves Me, and everybody else Massa or Missie (according to sex), and always reading tracts of the obscurest meaning in broken English, but always accurately understanding them in the purest mother tongue. He had nearly brought Mrs. Crisparkle’s grey hairs with sorrow to the grave. He had repeatedly said he would have Mr. Crisparkle’s life. He had repeatedly said he would have everybody’s life, and become, in effect, the last man. He had been brought down to Cloisterham, from London, by an eminent Philanthropist, and why? Because that Philanthropist had expressly declared: “I owe it to my fellow-creatures that he should be, in the words of BENTHAM, where he is the cause of the greatest danger to the smallest number.”

Those were Mr. Sapsea’s original expressions; and they were flung about the town like kitchen-salt—cheap, sharp, and making everything taste the same.

These dropping shots from the blunderbusses of blunderheadedness might not have struck Neville vitally; but there was a trained and well-directed fire of arms of precision too.

He had notoriously threatened the lost young man; and by the showing of his own faithful tutor—who strove hard for him—he had had a cause of bitter animosity, created by himself and stated by himself, against that ill-starred fellow. He had armed himself with an offensive weapon for the fatal night. He had gone off early in the morning after making preparations for departure. He had been found with traces of blood upon him—traces that might have been wholly caused as he represented, but might not also. When a search-warrant issued for the examination of his room, clothes, and so forth, it was discovered he had destroyed all his papers and rearranged all his possessions on the very afternoon of the disappearance.

The watch found at the Weir was challenged by the jeweller as one he had wound and set for Edwin Drood at twenty minutes past two on that same afternoon. It had run down before being cast into the water; and it was the jeweller’s positive opinion it had never been re-wound. That justified an ugly hypothesis: that the watch was taken from Edwin not long after he left Mr. Jasper’s house at midnight, in company with the last person seen with him, and was thrown away after being kept some hours.

Why thrown away? If he had been murdered, and so artfully disfigured or concealed—or both—that the murderer hoped identification would be impossible except by something he wore, assuredly the murderer would remove from the body the most lasting, the best known, the most easily recognisable things upon it. Those things would be the watch and the shirt-pin.

As to opportunities of casting them into the river: if Neville were the suspected man, they were easy. He had been seen by many persons wandering about on that side of the city—indeed on all sides—in a miserable, seemingly half-distracted manner. As to the choice of the spot, obviously such criminating evidence had better take its chance of being found anywhere rather than upon him or in his possession.

Concerning the reconciliatory nature of the appointed meeting between the two young men, very little could be made of that in Neville’s favour. It distinctly appeared that the meeting originated not with him but with Mr. Crisparkle, and was urged on by Mr. Crisparkle; and who could say how unwillingly, or in what ill-conditioned mood, his enforced pupil had gone to it?

So, the more the case was looked into, the weaker it became at every point.

Even the broad suggestion that Edwin Drood had absconded was rendered additionally improbable on the showing of the young lady from whom he had so lately parted. When interrogated, what did she say—earnestly and sorrowfully? That he had expressly and enthusiastically planned with her that he would await the arrival of her guardian, Mr. Grewgious. And yet he had disappeared before that gentleman appeared.

On these suspicions, urged and supported, Neville was detained and re-detained. The search was pressed on every hand. Jasper laboured night and day. But nothing more was found.

No discovery proving the lost man dead could be made; and at length it became necessary to release the person suspected of having made away with him. Neville was set at large.

Then came the consequence Mr. Crisparkle had too well foreseen. Neville must leave the place. The place shunned him and cast him out. Even had it not been so, Mrs. Crisparkle—dear old china shepherdess—would have worried herself to death with fears for her son and with general trepidation occasioned by such an inmate. Even had that not been so, the authority to which the Minor Canon deferred officially would have settled the point.

“Mr. Crisparkle,” quoth the Dean, “human justice may err, but it must act according to its lights. The days of taking sanctuary are past. This young man must not take sanctuary with us.”

“You mean that he must leave my house, sir?” asked Mr. Crisparkle.

“Mr. Crisparkle,” returned the prudent Dean, “I claim no authority in your house. I merely confer with you on the painful necessity you find yourself under of depriving this young man of the great advantages of your counsel and instruction.”

“It is very lamentable, sir,” Mr. Crisparkle represented.

“Very much so,” the Dean assented.

“And if it be a necessity—” Mr. Crisparkle faltered.

“As you unfortunately find it to be,” returned the Dean.

Mr. Crisparkle bowed submissively. “It is hard to prejudge his case, sir, but I am sensible that—”

“Just so. Perfectly. As you say, Mr. Crisparkle,” interposed the Dean, nodding smoothly. “There is nothing else to be done. No doubt, no doubt. There is no alternative, as your good sense has discovered.”

“I am entirely satisfied of his perfect innocence, sir, nevertheless.”

“We-e-ell!” said the Dean, dropping his voice and glancing slightly round as if innocence were a dangerous word to let out of doors, “I would not say so, generally. Not generally. Enough of suspicion attaches to him to—no, I think I would not say so, generally.”

Mr. Crisparkle bowed again.

“It does not become us, perhaps,” pursued the Dean, “to be partisans. Not partisans. We clergy keep our hearts warm and our heads cool, and we hold a judicious middle course.”

“I hope you do not object, sir, to my having stated in public, emphatically, that he will reappear here, whenever any new suspicion may be awakened, or any new circumstance may come to light in this extraordinary matter?”

“Not at all,” returned the Dean. “And yet, do you know, I don’t think”—with a very nice and neat emphasis on those two words—“I don’t think I would state it emphatically. State it? Ye-e-es! But emphatically? No-o-o. I think not. In point of fact, Mr. Crisparkle, keeping our hearts warm and our heads cool, we clergy need do nothing emphatically.”

So Minor Canon Row knew Neville Landless no more; and the young man went whithersoever he would, or could, with a blight upon his name and fame—a stain compounded not only of what he had done, but of what could be easily said of him in the town’s dominant tongue.

It was not until then that John Jasper silently resumed his place in the choir.

He came back among the ancient harmonies like a man returning to a trade after bereavement. Haggard and red-eyed, he bore on his face the mark of nights without sleep, and in his posture the drag of an inward weight. Whatever hope had lit him in that small room with the Tope’s supper smells and Mr. Grewgious’s wooden presence had deserted him now. His sanguine mood was gone; all his worst misgivings had returned, and sat with him like a second self.

A day or two afterwards, while unrobing, he took his Diary from the pocket of his coat. He turned the leaves. Then, with an impressive look, and without one spoken word, he handed Mr. Crisparkle an entry to read:

“My dear boy is murdered. The discovery of the watch and shirt-pin convinces me that he was murdered that night, and that his jewellery was taken from him to prevent identification by its means. All the delusive hopes I had founded on his separation from his betrothed wife, I give to the winds. They perish before this fatal discovery. I now swear, and record the oath on this page, That I nevermore will discuss this mystery with any human creature until I hold the clue to it in my hand. That I never will relax in my secrecy or in my search. That I will fasten the crime of the murder of my dear dead boy upon the murderer. And, That I devote myself to his destruction.”

Half a year had sifted away, grain by grain, since the trouble began; and in that time Mr. Crisparkle had grown practised at the patience of waiting—waiting upon law, upon rumour, upon the slow digestion of events in other men’s minds. He sat now in a London antechamber belonging to the chief offices of the Haven of Philanthropy, where the air had the peculiar odour of much paper handled in haste: ink newly dried; sealing-wax warmed and pressed; and, beneath it, the tired breath of too many bodies kept too long under one roof.

It was not a quiet place. Doors clicked; feet scuffed; voices rose and fell like bad singing in a corridor; and the soundscape—harsh with echoes and command—seemed devised to keep the nerves alert in the wrong way, as a factory keeps its wheels awake. Men passed in and out with the forward, shoulder-first manner of those who are always about to “set upon” somebody; and it struck the Minor Canon, remembering certain college days when he had looked on at gloved encounters for sport, that these professional benefactors carried the same combative shape in the back of the head as the professors of fist-work. The Haven might have been training a set of moral bruisers. They moved as if every errand were a snatch, every message a grapple, and every interview a round.

There was a stir over some coming rural demonstration—some “little Mill” of virtue, to be held on a circuit of villages—and the talk about it had the flavour of betting: this man “good” for such blows of rhetoric, that man likely to “finish” a resolution with a flourish. An official manager, black-coated and eminent for platform stratagems, carried himself with the practised air of one who could rope off a ring and keep the crowd in order. Only, for all their likeness to the Fancy, these philanthropists wanted three things the prize-men commonly had. They were not in training—too much soft flesh, too much of that suet-pudding condition that sits heavily on the spirit as well as the waistcoat. They had, next, less good temper; and, lastly, a worse code: a manner of fighting that allowed them to bore their man not merely to the ropes but to distraction—hitting when he was down, stamping, gouging, worrying him out of sight and behind his back. In that, the old brutal art looked almost clean by comparison.

So occupied was Mr. Crisparkle with these observations—and with the ugly, jarring music of the place, as if charity must always be beaten out upon a drum—that he did not hear his name until it had been called twice. Then, answering, he was guided by a shabby, underpaid stipendiary of the Haven—one of those sorry servants who, being ill-used by their own cause, might have seemed engaged by its enemy—into the room of Mr. Honeythunder.

“Sir,” said Mr. Honeythunder, in the tremendous voice with which he habitually scolded a meeting into admiration, “sit down.”

Mr. Crisparkle sat.

Mr. Honeythunder signed, with a ponderous haste, the tail-end of a few score circulars—part of a few thousand—calling upon a corresponding number of families without means to come forward instantly, find money they had not, and be Philanthropists, or go to the Devil. Another shabby stipendiary gathered the heap into a basket and carried it away like a man removing dinner from a table at which he would never eat.

“Now, Mr. Crisparkle,” said Mr. Honeythunder, wheeling half round and planting his hands on his knees, brows knitted as if the room were a platform and the Minor Canon an opposition to be shouted down, “we entertain different views, you and I, sir, of the sanctity of human life.”

“Do we?” returned Mr. Crisparkle.

“We do, sir.”

“Might I ask,” said the Minor Canon, “what your views are?”

“That human life is a thing to be held sacred, sir.”

“Might I ask,” pursued Mr. Crisparkle, just as quietly, “what you suppose mine to be?”

“By George, sir!” cried the Philanthropist, tightening his posture, “they are best known to yourself.”

“Readily admitted. But you began by saying we differ; therefore you must have set up some views as mine. Pray, what views have you set up as mine?”

“Here is a man—and a young man,” said Mr. Honeythunder, as if youth were an aggravation and age a mitigation, “swept off the face of the earth by a deed of violence. What do you call that?”

“Murder,” said Mr. Crisparkle.

“What do you call the doer of that deed, sir?”

“A murderer.”

“I’m glad to hear you admit so much, sir,” returned Mr. Honeythunder, lowering upon him with offensive satisfaction, “and I candidly tell you I didn’t expect it.”

“Be so good as to explain what you mean by those expressions.”

“I don’t sit here, sir,” roared Mr. Honeythunder, “to be browbeaten.”

“As the only other person present, no one can possibly know that better than I do,” returned Mr. Crisparkle. “But I interrupt your explanation.”

“Murder!” proceeded Mr. Honeythunder, relapsing into a kind of declamatory trance, folding his arms as on the platform, nodding after each exploded syllable as if an audience were taking notes. “Bloodshed! Abel! Cain! I hold no terms with Cain. I repudiate with a shudder the red hand when it is offered me.”

Mr. Crisparkle, instead of leaping up to cheer, merely changed the quiet crossing of his legs. “Don’t let me interrupt your explanation—when you begin it.”

“The Commandments say, no murder. NO murder, sir!” pursued Mr. Honeythunder, pausing as if Mr. Crisparkle had openly maintained the opposite: You may do a little murder, and then leave off.

“And they also say, you shall bear no false witness,” observed Mr. Crisparkle.

“Enough!” bellowed Mr. Honeythunder, with that solemnity which would have brought down applause in a hall. “E—e—nough! My late wards being now of age, and I being released from a trust I cannot contemplate without a thrill of horror, there are the accounts which you have undertaken to accept on their behalf; and there is a statement of the balance which you have undertaken to receive, and which you cannot receive too soon. And let me tell you, sir, I wish that, as a man and a Minor Canon, you were better employed.” Nod. “Better employed.” Another nod. “Bet—ter em—ployed!” A third; and the three together made a kind of blowsy cadence.

Mr. Crisparkle rose with a little colour in his face, but with his temper held as firmly as a cook holds a pan from boiling over by lifting it a hair from the fiercest flame.

“Mr. Honeythunder,” he said, taking up the papers, “my being better or worse employed than I am is a matter of taste and opinion. You might think me better employed in enrolling myself a member of your Society.”

“Ay, indeed, sir!” retorted Mr. Honeythunder, shaking his head as if to threaten.

“I think otherwise.”

“Or,” said Mr. Honeythunder, shaking it again, “I might think one of your profession better employed in devoting himself to the discovery and punishment of guilt than in leaving that duty to be undertaken by a layman.”

“I may regard my profession from a point of view which teaches me that its first duty is towards those in necessity and tribulation—desolate and oppressed,” answered Mr. Crisparkle. “However, as I have quite clearly satisfied myself that it is no part of my profession to make professions, I say no more of that. But I owe it to Mr. Neville, and to Mr. Neville’s sister—and in a much lower degree to myself—to say to you that I know I was in the full possession and understanding of Mr. Neville’s mind and heart at the time of this occurrence; and that, without in the least colouring or concealing what was to be deplored in him and required to be corrected, I feel certain that his tale is true. Feeling that certainty, I befriend him. As long as that certainty shall last, I will befriend him. And if any consideration could shake me in this resolve, I should be so ashamed of myself for my meanness that no man’s good opinion—no, nor no woman’s—so gained, could compensate me for the loss of my own.”

There was no flourish in it: no stage-smack of the palm, no turning to the gallery. It was the plain courage that belongs to an honest man, and it had the weight of plain bread—made of common flour, well kneaded, and baked through.

“Then who do you make out did the deed?” demanded Mr. Honeythunder, turning on him abruptly.

“Heaven forbid,” returned Mr. Crisparkle, “that in my desire to clear one man I should lightly criminate another! I accuse no one.”

“Tcha!” ejaculated Mr. Honeythunder, with disgust; for it was not the usual method of the Brotherhood, who liked their enemies served up ready-trussed.

“And, sir, you are not a disinterested witness, we must bear in mind.”

“How am I an interested one?” asked the Minor Canon, smiling in simple surprise.

“There was a certain stipend, sir, paid to you for your pupil, which may have warped your judgment a bit,” said Mr. Honeythunder, coarsely.

“Perhaps I expect to retain it still?” Mr. Crisparkle returned, newly enlightened. “Do you mean that too?”

“Well, sir,” said Mr. Honeythunder, rising and thrusting his hands deep into his trouser pockets, “I don’t go about measuring people for caps. If people find I have any about me that fit ’em, they can put ’em on and wear ’em, if they like. That’s their look out: not mine.”

Mr. Crisparkle eyed him with just indignation—steady as a man listening for truth in a room full of noise.

“Mr. Honeythunder, I hoped when I came in here that I might be under no necessity of commenting on the introduction of platform manners among the decent forbearances of private life. But you have given me such a specimen of both, that I should be a fit subject for both if I remained silent. They are detestable.”

“They don’t suit you, I dare say, sir.”

“They are,” repeated Mr. Crisparkle, without noticing the interruption, “detestable. They violate equally the justice that should belong to Christians and the restraints that should belong to gentlemen. You assume a great crime to have been committed by one whom I, acquainted with the attendant circumstances and having reasons on my side, devoutly believe to be innocent. Because I differ from you on that vital point, what is your platform resource? Instantly to turn upon me, charging that I have no sense of the enormity of the crime itself, but am its aider and abettor! So, another time—taking me as representing your opponent in other cases—you set up a platform credulity; a moved and seconded and carried-unanimously profession of faith in some ridiculous delusion or mischievous imposition. I decline to believe it, and you fall back upon your resource of proclaiming that I believe nothing; that because I will not bow down to a false god of your making, I deny the true God! Another time you discover that War is a calamity, and propose to abolish it by a string of twisted resolutions tossed into the air like the tail of a kite. I do not admit the discovery to be yours, and I have not a grain of faith in your remedy. Again, you represent me as revelling in the horrors of a battlefield like a fiend incarnate! Another time, in another of your undiscriminating rushes, you would punish the sober for the drunken. I claim consideration for the comfort, convenience, and refreshment of the sober; and you presently proclaim that I have a depraved desire to turn Heaven’s creatures into swine and wild beasts! In all such cases your movers, and seconders, and supporters—your regular Professors of all degrees—run amuck like so many mad Malays; habitually attributing the lowest motives with the utmost recklessness—let me call your attention to a recent instance in yourself for which you should blush—and quoting figures which you know to be as wilfully one-sided as any complicated account that should be all Creditor and no Debtor, or all Debtor and no Creditor. Therefore it is, Mr. Honeythunder, that I consider the platform a sufficiently bad example and a sufficiently bad school, even in public life; but, carried into private life, it becomes an unendurable nuisance.”

“These are strong words, sir!” cried Mr. Honeythunder.

“I hope so,” said Mr. Crisparkle. “Good morning.”

He walked out of the Haven at a great rate, as if he would leave its din behind by speed alone. Then his breath fell into its usual wholesome rhythm, and he resumed his brisk pace with a smile that came, half in amusement, half in that harmless vanity which hopes one’s blow has told. In his mind’s ear he heard, as a faint counter-music to the Haven’s brazen clamour, the quiet domestic voice of that china shepherdess at Cloisterham who had once been the witness of his gentler moods; and he wondered what she would have said if she had seen him pounding Mr. Honeythunder in that late lively affair, trimming the Philanthropic Jacket to his own satisfaction.

From the loud streets he took himself to Staple Inn; but not to the chambers of P. J. T. and Mr. Grewgious. He climbed many a stair that creaked as if it complained in a dialect of old wood—each step a different note, each landing a change of pitch—until he reached some attic rooms in a corner. The door stood unbolted. He lifted the latch and went in, and there, beside a table, stood Neville Landless.

An air of retreat lay upon the rooms, like flour-dust after a day’s baking: it dulled the edges of things, softened the light, and yet spoke of long labour. The ceilings sloped; the locks and grates were rusty; heavy bins and beams, slowly mouldering, gave the place a prisonous look. The young man wore it too: worn in face, haggard as a prisoner who measures time by shadows on a wall. But the sunlight made its own stubborn entrance at the ugly garret-window that thrust a little penthouse among the tiles; and beyond, on the smoke-blackened parapet, some sparrows hopped with rheumatic perseverance, like small feathered invalids who had left their crutches in their nests. Nearby there was a play of living leaves—some neighbour’s tree finding a way upward—making an imperfect music, not the full country melody, but enough to thin the stale air and remind one what quiet can be.

The rooms were sparely furnished, but they held a good store of books. They had the look of a poor student’s larder: plain staples, well chosen, meant to keep mind and hope alive. Mr. Crisparkle’s eyes rested on them with the friendly beam that betrayed him as chooser, lender, donor—or all three.

“How goes it, Neville?”

“I am in good heart, Mr. Crisparkle, and working away.”

“I wish your eyes were not quite so large and not quite so bright,” said the Minor Canon, slowly releasing the hand he had taken.

“They brighten at the sight of you,” returned Neville. “If you were to fall away from me, they would soon be dull enough.”

“Rally, rally!” urged Mr. Crisparkle, with the stimulating tone of one who would rather call courage up than pity down. “Fight for it, Neville!”

“If I were dying, I feel as if a word from you would rally me; if my pulse had stopped, I feel as if your touch would make it beat again,” said Neville. “But I have rallied, and am doing famously.”

Mr. Crisparkle turned him a little towards the light, as if he were gauging colour in a cheek the way a careful cook gauges the first returning blush in meat set too near the fire.

“I want to see a ruddier touch here,” he said, indicating his own healthy cheek by way of pattern. “I want more sun to shine upon you.”

Neville’s spirit drooped suddenly; his voice sank. “I am not hardy enough for that, yet. I may become so, but I cannot bear it yet. If you had gone through those Cloisterham streets as I did; if you had seen, as I did, those averted eyes, and the better sort of people silently giving me too much room to pass, that I might not touch them—you wouldn’t think it unreasonable that I cannot go about in the daylight.”

“My poor fellow!” said Mr. Crisparkle, and the pity in it was so unfeigned that Neville caught his hand. “I never said it was unreasonable; never thought so. But I should like you to do it.”

“And that would give me the strongest motive. But I cannot yet. I cannot persuade myself that the eyes of even the stream of strangers I pass in this vast city look at me without suspicion. I feel marked and tainted, even when I go out—as I do only—at night. But the darkness covers me then, and I take courage from it.”

Mr. Crisparkle laid a hand upon his shoulder and looked down at him.

“If I could have changed my name,” said Neville, “I would have done so. But as you wisely pointed out, I can’t—for it would look like guilt. If I could have gone to some distant place, I might have found relief; but it is not to be thought of, for the same reason. Hiding and escaping would be the construction. It seems a little hard to be so tied to a stake, and innocent; but I don’t complain.”

“And you must expect no miracle to help you,” said the Minor Canon, compassionately.

“No, sir. The ordinary fulness of time and circumstances is all I have to trust to.”

“It will right you at last, Neville.”

“So I believe, and I hope I may live to know it.”

Yet he saw the shadow his words cast on the other’s face, and—perhaps feeling that the broad hand upon his shoulder was not quite so steady now as its strength should have made it—he roused himself.

“Excellent circumstances for study, anyhow! And you know what need I have of study in all ways. Not to mention that you have advised me to study for the difficult profession of the law, specially, and that of course I am guiding myself by the advice of such a friend and helper. Such a good friend and helper!”

He took the fortifying hand from his shoulder and kissed it. Mr. Crisparkle smiled towards the books, but not with the earlier brightness.

“I gather from your silence on the subject that my late guardian is adverse, Mr. Crisparkle?”

“Your late guardian,” returned the Minor Canon, “is a—most unreasonable person, and it signifies nothing to any reasonable person whether he is ad-verse, per-verse, or the re-verse.”

“Well for me,” sighed Neville, half wearily and half cheerily, “that I have enough with economy to live upon, while I wait to be learned, and wait to be righted! Else I might prove the proverb, that while the grass grows, the steed starves.”

As he spoke, he opened his books, and soon was deep among interleaved pages and pencilled notes; and Mr. Crisparkle sat beside him, expounding, correcting, advising—turning dry matter into something that could be chewed and swallowed, and might one day sustain a career. The Minor Canon’s Cathedral duties made such visits difficult and rare—coming only at intervals of many weeks—but they were precious and serviceable, as bread is to a hungry man.

When their work for that time was done, they stood leaning on the window-sill, looking down upon a patch of garden that seemed a small green afterthought in the brick and slate.

“Next week,” said Mr. Crisparkle, “you will cease to be alone, and will have a devoted companion.”

“And yet,” returned Neville, “this seems an uncongenial place to bring my sister to.”

“I don’t think so,” said the Minor Canon. “There is duty to be done here; and there are womanly feeling, sense, and courage wanted here.”

“I meant,” explained Neville, “that the surroundings are so dull and unwomanly, and that Helena can have no suitable friend or society here.”

“You have only to remember,” said Mr. Crisparkle, “that you are here yourself, and that she has to draw you into the sunlight.”

They were silent a little; the leaves made their thin music again, and the city’s larger roar, muffled by height and distance, came and went like a far-off, unkind sea.

“When we first spoke together,” Mr. Crisparkle began anew, “you told me that your sister had risen out of the disadvantages of your past lives as superior to you as the tower of Cloisterham Cathedral is higher than the chimneys of Minor Canon Corner. Do you remember that?”

“Right well!”

“I was inclined to think it at the time an enthusiastic flight. No matter what I think it now. What I would emphasise is, that under the head of Pride your sister is a great and opportune example to you.”

“Under all heads included in the composition of a fine character, she is.”

“Say so; but take this one. Your sister has learnt how to govern what is proud in her nature. She can dominate it even when it is wounded through her sympathy with you. No doubt she has suffered deeply in those same streets where you suffered. No doubt her life is darkened by the cloud that darkens yours. But bending her pride into a grand composure—not haughty, not aggressive, but a sustained confidence in you and in the truth—she has won her way through those streets until she passes along them as high in the general respect as any one who treads them. Every day and hour of her life since Edwin Drood’s disappearance, she has faced malignity and folly—for you—as only a brave nature, well directed, can. So it will be with her to the end. Another and weaker kind of pride might sink broken-hearted, but never such a pride as hers: which knows no shrinking, and can get no mastery over her.”

The pale cheek beside him flushed at the comparison and at the sting of its implied rebuke.

“I will do all I can to imitate her,” said Neville.

“Do so, and be a truly brave man, as she is a truly brave woman,” answered Mr. Crisparkle stoutly. “It is growing dark. Will you go my way with me, when it is quite dark? Mind! it is not I who wait for darkness.”

Neville said he would accompany him directly. But Mr. Crisparkle, remembering courtesies owed even when one would rather avoid certain thresholds, said he had a moment’s call to make on Mr. Grewgious, and would run across to that gentleman’s chambers, rejoining Neville on his own doorstep if Neville would come down to meet him.

Mr. Grewgious, bolt upright as usual, sat taking his wine in the dusk at his open window: wineglass and decanter on the round table at his elbow; himself and his legs on the window-seat; only one hinge in his whole body, like a bootjack. The sound outside was quieter here—Staple Inn keeping, as it could, a certain old-fashioned restraint upon the street’s incessant tongue.

“How do you do, reverend sir?” said Mr. Grewgious, offering hospitality with as much seriousness as if he meant it, and receiving the decline as cordially as he made the offer. “And how is your charge getting on over the way in the set that I had the pleasure of recommending to you as vacant and eligible?”

Mr. Crisparkle replied that he was improving himself steadily.

“I am glad you approve of them,” said Mr. Grewgious, “because I entertain a sort of fancy for having him under my eye.”

As Mr. Grewgious would have had to turn his eye up a great deal to see those attic windows, the phrase was plainly figurative.

“And how did you leave Mr. Jasper, reverend sir?” asked Mr. Grewgious.

Mr. Crisparkle had left him pretty well.

“And where did you leave Mr. Jasper, reverend sir?”

At Cloisterham.

“And when did you leave Mr. Jasper, reverend sir?”

That morning.

“Umps!” said Mr. Grewgious, his preoccupied glance fixed out at the window. “He didn’t say he was coming, perhaps?”

“Coming where?”

“Anywhere, for instance?” returned Mr. Grewgious.

“No.”

“Because here he is,” said Mr. Grewgious, without shifting that outward look, “and he don’t look agreeable, does he?”

Mr. Crisparkle craned towards the window to see.

A stranger came into Cloisterham as quietly as flour sifts through a sieve, and yet he was the sort of stranger whose very stillness rearranged a room.

He was white-haired—abundantly, astonishingly so—yet with eyebrows black as char. Buttoned close in a tightish blue surtout above a buff waistcoat and grey trousers, he wore a faint military set about the shoulders, as if he had once stood to attention for the mere pleasure of the posture. But he announced himself at the Crozier—Cloisterham’s orthodox inn, and as drowsy an establishment as if it had been laid in a pantry and forgotten—as an idle dog living upon his means; and he added, to the waiter and the empty fire-grate and whatever ghosts of travellers might be listening, that he had a mind to take a lodging in the old city for a month or two, and perhaps to settle there altogether.

He made these declarations with his back to the cold hearth, waiting for dinner: fried sole, veal cutlet, and a pint of sherry. The Crozier’s business being chronically slack, the waiter served as audience, recorder, and committee of inquiry. The sole arrived with that faint, sea-wet perfume that clings to a fish even after the pan has kissed it; the cutlet hissed when the knife found its browned edge; the sherry lifted a warm, nutty note into the stale air of the room and made it briefly more hospitable.

“I suppose, waiter,” said the gentleman, shaking out his great shock of hair as a Newfoundland might shake off river-water before applying itself to its supper, “that a fair lodging for a single buffer might be found in these parts, eh?”

The waiter had no doubt of it.

“Something old,” pursued the gentleman. “Take my hat down for a moment from that peg, will you? No, I don’t want it; look into it. What do you see written there?”

The waiter peered into the crown and read, as if it were a label on a jar: “Datchery.”

“Now you know my name,” said the stranger. “Dick Datchery. Hang it up again. I was saying, something old is what I should prefer—something odd and out of the way; something venerable, architectural, and inconvenient.”

“We have a good choice of inconvenient lodgings in the town, sir,” returned the waiter, with modest confidence in Cloisterham’s ability to discomfort. “Indeed, I’ve no doubt we could suit you that far, however particular you might be. But a architectural lodging!” He shook his head, as if architecture were an ingredient his kitchen had never stocked.

“Anything Cathedraly, now,” suggested Mr. Datchery.

“Mr. Tope,” said the waiter, brightening, rubbing his chin as a man rubs at a memory like a stubborn stain, “would be the likeliest party to inform in that line.”

“Who is Mr. Tope?” asked Mr. Datchery.

The waiter explained: Mr. Tope was the Verger; Mrs. Tope had once let lodgings, or at least had offered them—so long, indeed, that the bill in her window had become a local landmark, until one day it had likely tumbled down and, being nobody’s profit to replace, had never returned.

“I’ll call on Mrs. Tope,” said Mr. Datchery, “after dinner.”

So when he had finished—when the last of the sole’s crisped edge had yielded, and the cutlet’s pale centre had confessed its hot juices, and the sherry had left a faint sweetness on his tongue—he was directed to the spot and sallied out.

But the Crozier was an inn of the most retiring disposition, set in the town like an old pot set back on the shelf; and the waiter’s directions, being fatally precise in the way that only a man unused to being asked can be precise, soon bewildered him. He went boggling about and about the Cathedral Tower whenever he could catch sight of it, with a child’s-game logic in his mind: warm when the Tower was in view, cold when it vanished behind houses and angles. The sound of Cloisterham went with him: the disciplined hush of old stone, broken by a cart’s iron tyre grating too sharply, by a distant hammer striking a note too modern against the ancient air, by the little scuffle and squeal of boys—always too loud in places that have learnt to be quiet.

He was getting very cold indeed when he came upon a fragment of burial-ground in which an unhappy sheep was grazing.

Unhappy—because a hideous small boy was stoning it through the railings. One leg of the creature was already lamed; it shifted its weight in little panicked jolts, making no proper music of its hooves at all, only a dull scrape. The boy was much excited by the benevolent sportsmanlike purpose of breaking its other three legs and bringing it down.

“’It ’im agin!” cried the boy, as the poor creature leaped. “And made a dint in his wool.”

“Let him be!” said Mr. Datchery. “Don’t you see you have lamed him?”

“Yer lie,” returned the sportsman. “’E went and lamed isself. I see ’im do it, and I giv’ ’im a shy as a Widdy-warning to ’im not to go a-bruisin’ ’is master’s mutton any more.”

The phrase sat in the air like bad smoke: “Widdy-warning.” A child’s corruption of words, perhaps; yet Mr. Datchery lingered a moment on it, as one might on a mispronounced herb-name that threatens to take the place of the true one in a kitchen’s speech.

“Come here,” said Mr. Datchery.

“I won’t; I’ll come when yer can ketch me.”

“Stay there then, and show me which is Mr. Tope’s.”

“Ow can I stay here and show you which is Topeseses, when Topeseses is t’other side the Kinfreederal, and over the crossings, and round ever so many comers? Stoo-pid! Ya-a-ah!”

“Show me where it is, and I’ll give you something.”

“Come on, then.”

Thus concluded, the boy led the way at a trot, and the stranger followed at a measured pace, listening as much as looking—listening to how the stone corridors of the Close swallowed footsteps, how certain corners magnified a laugh, how the Cathedral’s presence seemed to lower the voice of the air itself, while the town’s small cruelties still managed to sound bright and sharp.

By-and-by, the boy stopped at some distance from an arched passage and pointed.

“Lookie yonder. You see that there winder and door?”

“That’s Tope’s?”

“Yer lie; it ain’t. That’s Jarsper’s.”

“Indeed?” said Mr. Datchery, looking again, with a second glance that had more of a reckoning in it.

“Yes, and I ain’t a-goin’ no nearer ’IM, I tell yer.”

“Why not?”

“’Cos I ain’t a-goin’ to be lifted off my legs and ’ave my braces bust and be choked; not if I knows it, and not by ’Im. Wait till I set a jolly good flint a-flyin’ at the back o’ ’is jolly old ’ed some day! Now look t’other side the harch; not the side where Jarsper’s door is; t’other side.”

“I see.”

“A little way in, o’ that side, there’s a low door, down two steps. That’s Topeseses with ’is name on a hoval plate.”

“Good. See here,” said Mr. Datchery, producing a shilling. “You owe me half of this.”

“Yer lie! I don’t owe yer nothing; I never seen yer.”

“I tell you you owe me half of this, because I have no sixpence in my pocket. So the next time you meet me you shall do something else for me, to pay me.”

“All right, give us ’old.”

“What is your name, and where do you live?”

“Deputy. Travellers’ Twopenny, ’cross the green.”

The boy darted off with the shilling at once, and then, at a safe distance, began a demon dance as if to nail down the fact of his possession. Mr. Datchery took off his hat and gave his white hair another shake—so that it rose and fell like a soft tide—and seemed resigned, as if he had paid for information the way one pays for pepper: too much, always, but necessary if you want the dish to speak.

He went where he had been directed.

Mr. Tope’s official dwelling, communicating by an upper stair with Mr. Jasper’s gatehouse—hence Mrs. Tope’s attendance on that gentleman—was of very modest proportions, and had the character of a cool dungeon. Its ancient walls were so massive that the rooms seemed not built so much as excavated from the thickness. The main door opened at once on a chamber of no describable shape, with a groined roof; that opened on another chamber equally without description, equally groined. Their windows were small and set deep, like eyes in a heavy brow. The air was close; the light was swarthy; the place held a persistent smell of cold stone and old damp, as if the Cathedral kept its own cellar here.

These were the apartments Mrs. Tope had long offered to an unappreciative city.

Mr. Datchery proved more appreciative. He found that if he sat with the main door open, he would enjoy the passing society of all comers to and fro by the gateway and have light enough to read by. He found that if Mr. and Mrs. Tope, living overhead, used for themselves a little side stair that came plump into the Precincts by a door opening outward—surprising and inconveniencing pedestrians in the narrow way—he would be alone, as if in a separate residence. He found the rent moderate, and everything as quaintly inconvenient as he could desire.

He agreed, therefore, to take the lodging then and there, money down, possession to be had next evening, on condition that reference was permitted him to Mr. Jasper as occupying the gatehouse of which, on the other side of the gateway, the Verger’s hole-in-the-wall was an appanage.

“The poor dear gentleman is very solitary and very sad,” said Mrs. Tope, “but I have no doubt he will speak for me.”

Perhaps, she added, Mr. Datchery had heard something of what had occurred there last winter?

Mr. Datchery had as confused a knowledge of the event, upon trying to recall it, as he could well manage. He begged Mrs. Tope’s pardon when she found it incumbent upon her to correct him in every detail of his muddled summary, but pleaded that he was merely a single buffer getting through life upon his means as idly as he could, and that so many people were so constantly making away with so many other people, as to render it difficult for a buffer of an easy temper to keep the circumstances of the several cases unmixed.

Mr. Jasper proving willing to speak for Mrs. Tope, Mr. Datchery, who sent up his card, was invited to ascend the postern staircase. Mr. Tope mentioned, as they went, that the Mayor was there; but he was not to be regarded in the light of company, as he and Mr. Jasper were great friends.

“I beg pardon,” said Mr. Datchery—hat under his arm, making a leg as he addressed himself equally to both gentlemen when admitted—“a selfish precaution on my part, and not personally interesting to anybody but myself. But as a buffer living on his means, and having an idea of doing it in this lovely place in peace and quiet, for remaining span of life, I beg to ask if the Tope family are quite respectable?”

Mr. Jasper answered for that without the slightest hesitation.

“That is enough, sir,” said Mr. Datchery.

“My friend the Mayor,” added Mr. Jasper, with a courtly motion of his hand towards that potentate, “whose recommendation is actually much more important to a stranger than that of an obscure person like myself, will testify in their behalf, I am sure.”

“The Worshipful the Mayor,” said Mr. Datchery, with a low bow, “places me under an infinite obligation.”

“Very good people, sir, Mr. and Mrs. Tope,” said Mr. Sapsea, with condescension measured out like spice from a tin. “Very good opinions. Very well behaved. Very respectful. Much approved by the Dean and Chapter.”

“The Worshipful the Mayor gives them a character,” said Mr. Datchery, “of which they may indeed be proud. I would ask His Honour—if I might be permitted—whether there are not many objects of great interest in the city which is under his beneficent sway?”

“We are, sir,” returned Mr. Sapsea, “an ancient city, and an ecclesiastical city. We are a constitutional city, as it becomes such a city to be, and we uphold and maintain our glorious privileges.”

“His Honour,” said Mr. Datchery, bowing, “inspires me with a desire to know more of the city, and confirms me in my inclination to end my days in the city.”

“Retired from the Army, sir?” suggested Mr. Sapsea, taking the stranger’s air for an advertisement.

“His Honour the Mayor does me too much credit,” returned Mr. Datchery.

“Navy, sir?” suggested Mr. Sapsea.

“Again,” repeated Mr. Datchery, “His Honour the Mayor does me too much credit.”

“Diplomacy is a fine profession,” said Mr. Sapsea, as one lays down a maxim.

“There, I confess, His Honour the Mayor is too many for me,” said Mr. Datchery, with an ingenious smile and bow; “even a diplomatic bird must fall to such a gun.”

This was very soothing to Mr. Sapsea. There was something in being addressed through the third person—His Honour, the Worshipful—that tasted to him like being served first at table. He warmed to it.

“But I crave pardon,” said Mr. Datchery. “His Honour the Mayor will bear with me, if for a moment I have been deluded into occupying his time, and have forgotten the humble claims upon my own, of my hotel, the Crozier.”

“Not at all, sir,” said Mr. Sapsea. “I am returning home, and if you would like to take the exterior of our Cathedral in your way, I shall be glad to point it out.”

“His Honour the Mayor is more than kind and gracious.”

As Mr. Datchery could not be induced to go out of the room before the Worshipful, the Worshipful led the way down-stairs; Mr. Datchery following, hat under his arm, his white hair streaming in the evening breeze like a banner that had forgotten its pole.

“Might I ask His Honour,” said Mr. Datchery as they went, “whether that gentleman we have just left is the gentleman of whom I have heard in the neighbourhood as being much afflicted by the loss of a nephew, and concentrating his life on avenging the loss?”

“That is the gentleman. John Jasper, sir.”

“Would His Honour allow me to inquire whether there are strong suspicions of any one?”

“More than suspicions, sir,” returned Mr. Sapsea; “all but certainties.”

“Only think now!” cried Mr. Datchery, as if the words were a seasoning he had not expected to find in so mild a broth.

“But proof, sir, proof must be built up stone by stone,” said the Mayor.

“As I say, the end crowns the work. It is not enough that justice should be morally certain; she must be immorally certain—legally, that is.”

“His Honour,” said Mr. Datchery, “reminds me of the nature of the law. Immoral. How true!”

“As I say, sir,” pompously went on Mr. Sapsea, “the arm of the law is a strong arm, and a long arm. That is the way I put it. A strong arm and a long arm.”

“How forcible!—And yet, again, how true!” murmured Mr. Datchery.

“And without betraying what I call the secrets of the prison-house,” pursued Mr. Sapsea—“the secrets of the prison-house is the term I used on the bench—without, I say, betraying them, I predict to you, knowing the iron will of the gentleman we have just left (I take the bold step of calling it iron, on account of its strength), that in this case the long arm will reach, and the strong arm will strike.—This is our Cathedral, sir. The best judges are pleased to admire it, and the best among our townsmen own to being a little vain of it.”

All this time Mr. Datchery walked with his hat under his arm and his hair to the breeze. When Mr. Sapsea touched that hat now, he had for an odd instant the look of having forgotten it altogether; he clapped his hand up to his head as if expecting to find another hat there, as one might reach for a missing word in a deadened dialect and find only the habit of speech.

“Pray be covered, sir,” entreated Mr. Sapsea, magnificently. “I shall not mind it, I assure you.”

“His Honour is very good, but I do it for coolness,” said Mr. Datchery.

Then he admired the Cathedral, and Mr. Sapsea pointed it out as if he himself had invented and built it—pausing where the evening made the stone look like baked bread, crisped at the edges by centuries; glossing over details he did not approve, as if the workmen had made mistakes in his absence. The place had its own hush, a kind of regulated quiet that seemed to ask men to keep their inner noise to themselves; but beyond it, in the town, there was always the other sound—the clatter, the call, the small shrill triumph of boys—pressing in.

The Cathedral disposed of, Mr. Sapsea led the way by the churchyard and stopped—by chance—in the immediate vicinity of Mrs. Sapsea’s epitaph, as if the path of every evening must at last circle back to a monument one has composed oneself.

“And by the by,” said Mr. Sapsea, appearing to descend from an elevation to remember it suddenly, like Apollo shooting down from Olympus to pick up a forgotten lyre, “that is one of our small lions. The partiality of our people has made it so, and strangers have been seen taking a copy of it now and then. I am not a judge of it myself, for it is a little work of my own. But it was troublesome to turn, sir; I may say, difficult to turn with elegance.”

Mr. Datchery became so ecstatic over the composition that—though intending to end his days in Cloisterham and therefore to have many opportunities—he would have transcribed it into his pocket-book on the spot, but for the slouching approach of its material producer and perpetuator, Durdles, whom Mr. Sapsea hailed; not sorry to show him a bright example of how to behave to superiors.

“Ah, Durdles! This is the mason, sir; one of our Cloisterham worthies; everybody here knows Durdles. Mr. Datchery—Durdles; a gentleman who is going to settle here.”

“I wouldn’t do it if I was him,” growled Durdles. “We’re a heavy lot.”

“You surely don’t speak for yourself, Mr. Durdles,” returned Mr. Datchery, “any more than for His Honour.”

“Who’s His Honour?” demanded Durdles.

“His Honour the Mayor.”

“I never was brought afore him,” said Durdles, with anything but loyalty in his look, “and it’ll be time enough for me to Honour him when I am. Until which, and when, and where, ‘Mister Sapsea is his name, England is his nation, Cloisterham’s his dwelling-place, Aukshneer’s his occupation.’”

At this moment Deputy—preceded by a flying oyster-shell—appeared and requested that Mr. Durdles would instantly “chuck” him threepence, being lawful wages overdue. While Durdles, with his bundle under his arm, slowly found and counted out the money—penny between his teeth, halfpence in his hands—Mr. Sapsea informed the new settler of Durdles’s habits, pursuits, abode, and reputation, as if giving the inventory of a local curiosity.

“I suppose a curious stranger might come to see you, and your works, Mr. Durdles, at any odd time?” said Mr. Datchery.

“Any gentleman is welcome to come and see me any evening if he brings liquor for two with him,” returned Durdles; “or if he likes to make it twice two, he’ll be doubly welcome.”

“I shall come. Master Deputy, what do you owe me?”

“A job.”

“Mind you pay me honestly with the job of showing me Mr. Durdles’s house when I want to go there.”

Deputy, with a piercing broadside of whistle through the whole gap in his mouth—as if the town’s small wild noise wanted to answer the Cathedral’s measured hush—vanished.

The Worshipful and the Worshipper passed on together until they parted with many ceremonies at the Worshipful’s door. Even then, Mr. Datchery carried his hat under his arm and gave his streaming hair to the breeze, as if he meant to let the city’s air—and its talk, its mispronunciations, its lingering old phrases and its new harsh ones—settle into it.

Said Mr. Datchery to himself that night, looking at his white hair in the gas-lighted looking-glass over the Crozier coffee-room chimneypiece, and shaking it out again:

“For a single buffer, of an easy temper, living idly on his means, I have had a rather busy afternoon!”

Once more Miss Twinkleton had poured out her valedictory periods as smoothly as her white-wine, and once more had pressed pound-cake upon lips that smiled because it was expected of them to smile. Once more the young ladies had fluttered away to their several homes, like ribbons loosened from a bonnet-string. Helena Landless had gone too, following her brother’s fortunes with that firm, bright fidelity of hers; and Rosa Bud was left at the Nuns’ House with no companion but her own thoughts, which were worse than solitude.

Cloisterham, in these midsummer days, wore a look so clear, so steeped in light, that the Cathedral and the monastery-ruin seemed less like stone than like some pale honeycombed substance through which the sun had learned to shine. The heat lay on the corn-fields beyond, and the distant roads smoked with a fine white dust that drifted at every passing cart-wheel. In the gardens the fruit was turning—plums blushing into their bloom, pears swelling with a weight that promised juice, currants hanging like bright beads, and the sharp green scent of crushed leaf and warmed earth rising whenever a foot brushed the borders.

The town’s old story seemed to move about in the day’s sounds. There had been a time when travel-stained pilgrims rode through in clattering parties, and the ring of hoofs and harness had been a kind of music. Now there were other wayfarers: reapers and haymakers living between two seasons like wandering tribes, so thick with road-dust that they appeared made of it. They lounged on cool doorsteps, bending over boots with broken soles, worrying at knots and stitches as if to mend what could not be mended, then flinging the hopeless things aside and rummaging for others in the straw-bound bundles where their yet unused sickles lay swathed. At the public pumps there was much bubbling, gurgling, and splashing; bare feet were held under the spouts; hands were cupped for drinking, and the water struck the basin with a bright, quick chatter. The Cloisterham police, set in their beats like ornaments of authority, watched askance and wished the intruders gone, as if the very human noise of thirst and travel offended the town’s measured hush.

On such an afternoon, when the last Cathedral service was done and the shaded side of the High Street lay gratefully cool, with only the Nuns’ House garden open to the west where sunlight sifted between boughs, a servant came to Rosa with news that turned her blood cold.

“Mr. Jasper desires to see you, miss.”

The words fell into the quiet like a dropped knife. It was the worst time he could have chosen to find her unguarded; and she had a frantic sense that he had chosen it for that very reason. Helena was gone. Mrs. Tisher had leave. Miss Twinkleton, in her amateur state of being, had contributed herself and a veal pie to a picnic. Even the house seemed emptied of its little protections.

“O why, why, why, did you say I was at home!” Rosa cried, clasping her hands.

The maid, surprised, answered that Mr. Jasper had never asked. He said he knew she was at home, and begged she might be told that he asked to see her.

What shall I do? what shall I do? The question beat in Rosa’s head as fast as the pump’s gurgle in the street. Desperation, and that instinct which seeks air when danger presses, made her speak.

“I will come to Mr. Jasper in the garden.”

The house, with its corridors and shutting doors, was a trap. Out there, at least, there were windows—eyes. Out there she could be seen as well as heard; out there she could shriek in free air and run. It was a wild plan, but it fluttered through her like a bird seeking escape.

She had not seen him since the fatal night—except when she had been questioned before the Mayor, and Jasper had been present, in gloomy watchfulness, representing his lost nephew and burning, as he said, to avenge him. Edwin Drood, the lost, had long been given up and mourned for as dead; and deep mourning now clothed both the man and the girl, black upon black, as if grief had made a uniform of them.

Rosa took her garden-hat upon her arm and went out. The moment she saw him from the porch—leaning upon the sun-dial—an old, horrible sensation closed round her: the sense of being compelled. It was as if his will drew her feet across the gravel. She felt she would even then turn back; yet she could not. She went to the seat beside the dial and sat with her head bent, seeing nothing but the grass, unable to lift her eyes to him for abhorrence.

He would have begun by touching her hand. She felt it before it happened, as one feels thunder in the air; she drew her hand away. She knew his eyes were fixed on her. She kept her own eyes on the blades at her feet.

“I have been waiting,” he began, “for some time, to be summoned back to my duty near you.”

She formed answers and unformed them. At last, in a voice that seemed not her own: “Duty, sir?”
