“The duty of teaching you, serving you as your faithful music-master.”

“I have left off that study.”

“Not left off, I think. Discontinued. I was told by your guardian that you discontinued it under the shock that we have all felt so acutely. When will you resume?”

“Never, sir.”

“Never?” His tone sharpened, and a cruel little edge of reproach slid into it. “You could have done no more if you had loved my dear boy.”

“I did love him!” she cried, and anger, like a sudden flame in a lantern, lit her for an instant.

“Yes; but not quite—not quite in the right way, shall I say? Not in the intended and expected way. Much as my dear boy was, unhappily, too self-conscious and self-satisfied”—he paused, as if tasting the words—“(I’ll draw no parallel between him and you in that respect) to love as he should have loved, or as any one in his place would have loved—must have loved!”

She did not change her attitude, but she shrank a little more, as if the air itself pressed on her.

“Then, to be told that you discontinued your study with me,” he pursued, “was to be politely told that you abandoned it altogether?”

“Yes,” said Rosa, with a flash of spirit. “The politeness was my guardian’s, not mine. I told him that I was resolved to leave off, and that I was determined to stand by my resolution.”

“And you still are?”

“I still am, sir. And I beg not to be questioned any more about it. At all events, I will not answer any more; I have that in my power.”

She felt, with a sickening certainty, that he looked at her with a gloating admiration of the colour in her cheek and the quickness in her breath; that her anger was a spectacle he enjoyed, as some coarse palate enjoys too hot a sauce. Even while her spirit rose, it fell again into shame, affront, and fear, as it had fallen that night at the piano when his presence had made music feel like a snare.

“I will not question you any more, since you object to it so much; I will confess—”

“I do not wish to hear you, sir,” she cried, starting up.

This time he did touch her, with an outstretched hand. The contact was not made, for she shrank; but in shrinking she shrank into her seat again, as if the very act of escape folded her back upon herself.

“We must sometimes act in opposition to our wishes,” he said in a low voice. “You must do so now, or do more harm to others than you can ever set right.”

“What harm?”

“Presently, presently. You question me, you see, and surely that’s not fair when you forbid me to question you. Nevertheless, I will answer the question presently. Dearest Rosa! Charming Rosa!”

She sprang up again. He did not touch her now. But his face—wicked, menacing—made the sunny garden seem suddenly without sun. Leaning against the sun-dial-setting, he looked like a black mark struck upon the very face of day. Horror arrested her flight.

“I do not forget how many windows command a view of us,” he said, glancing towards them. “I will not touch you again; I will come no nearer to you than I am. Sit down, and there will be no mighty wonder in your music-master’s leaning idly against a pedestal and speaking with you, remembering all that has happened, and our shares in it. Sit down, my beloved.”

She would have gone—was all but gone—and then his face, darkly threatening what would follow if she went, stopped her as surely as a hand. With the expression of that instant frozen upon her features, she sat down again.

“Rosa,” he said, and though his posture remained composed, there was something in his voice like a knife drawn along a stone, “even when my dear boy was affianced to you, I loved you madly; even when I thought his happiness in having you for his wife was certain, I loved you madly; even when I strove to make him more ardently devoted to you, I loved you madly; even when he gave me the picture of your lovely face so carelessly traduced by him, which I feigned to hang always in my sight for his sake, but worshipped in torment for years, I loved you madly; in the distasteful work of the day, in the wakeful misery of the night, girded by sordid realities, or wandering through Paradises and Hells of visions into which I rushed, carrying your image in my arms, I loved you madly.”

His words were hideous enough; but worse was the contrast—his violence of look and delivery held under an assumed ease, as if he wished the watching windows to read their scene as light talk. Faces did indeed come and go behind panes: the domestic life of the house flowed on, innocent and ignorant, like broth simmering in a kitchen while some poison is mixed in a corner.

“I endured it all in silence,” he went on. “So long as you were his, or so long as I supposed you to be his, I hid my secret loyally. Did I not?”

The lie in his mouth—gross and brazen—was more than Rosa could bear. Indignation broke through her fear like a cry forced out of a throat held down.

“You were as false throughout, sir, as you are now. You were false to him, daily and hourly. You know that you made my life unhappy by your pursuit of me. You know that you made me afraid to open his generous eyes, and that you forced me, for his own trusting, good, good sake, to keep the truth from him, that you were a bad, bad man!”

He kept his easy attitude still; but his features worked and his hands convulsed upon the dial as if the stone burnt him. The restraint made him seem less human than if he had raged openly.

“How beautiful you are!” he returned, with a fierce extreme of admiration. “You are more beautiful in anger than in repose. I don’t ask you for your love; give me yourself and your hatred; give me yourself and that pretty rage; give me yourself and that enchanting scorn; it will be enough for me.”

Tears—impatient, angry tears—rose to her eyes. Her face flamed; her breath came quick and shallow. She rose again to go, to seek protection within the house; and he stretched out his hand towards the porch, not to touch her but as though he invited her to enter it—like a host offering the door with dreadful courtesy.

“I told you, you rare charmer, you sweet witch, that you must stay and hear me, or do more harm than can ever be undone. You asked me what harm. Stay, and I will tell you. Go, and I will do it!”

She did not understand the meaning, but she understood the menace. It struck her with the certainty of thunder in the oppressive air. She quailed before his face and remained. Her breathing fluttered as if it would choke her; she pressed her hand upon her bosom, trying to still it.

“I have made my confession that my love is mad,” he said. “It is so mad, that had the ties between me and my dear lost boy been one silken thread less strong, I might have swept even him from your side, when you favoured him.”

A film swam before her eyes, as if his words had turned her faint.

“Even him,” he repeated. “Yes, even him! Rosa, you see me and you hear me. Judge for yourself whether any other admirer shall love you and live, whose life is in my hand.”

“What do you mean, sir?” she whispered.

“I mean to show you how mad my love is. It was hawked through the late inquiries by Mr. Crisparkle, that young Landless had confessed to him that he was a rival of my lost boy. That is an inexpiable offence in my eyes. The same Mr. Crisparkle knows under my hand that I have devoted myself to the murderer’s discovery and destruction, be he whom he might, and that I determined to discuss the mystery with no one until I should hold the clue in which to entangle the murderer as in a net. I have since worked patiently to wind and wind it round him; and it is slowly winding as I speak.”

There was something sickening in the calm with which he spoke of patience, of winding, of nets—like a cook describing the slow binding of a joint with twine, the careful trussing that makes escape impossible. Outside the garden, Cloisterham’s familiar hush held—only the faint noises of summer: a distant cart, a pump’s last drip, a bird’s sharp call. Those ordinary sounds made his threat more monstrous, as if brutality could hide itself in the town’s measured quiet.

“Your belief, if you believe in the criminality of Mr. Landless,” Rosa retorted, finding her voice in her fear, “is not Mr. Crisparkle’s belief, and he is a good man.”

“My belief is my own,” Jasper answered, “and I reserve it, worshipped of my soul! Circumstances may accumulate so strongly even against an innocent man, that directed, sharpened, and pointed, they may slay him. One wanting link discovered by perseverance against a guilty man proves his guilt, however slight its evidence before, and he dies. Young Landless stands in deadly peril either way.”

“If you really suppose,” Rosa pleaded, turning paler, “that I favour Mr. Landless, or that Mr. Landless has ever in any way addressed himself to me, you are wrong.”

He put it from him with a slighting action and a curled lip.

“I was going to show you how madly I love you,” he said. “More madly now than ever, for I am willing to renounce the second object that has arisen in my life to divide it with you; and henceforth to have no object in existence but you only. Miss Landless has become your bosom friend. You care for her peace of mind?”

“I love her dearly.”

“You care for her good name?”

“I have said, sir, I love her dearly.”

“I am unconsciously,” he observed, and folded his hands upon the sun-dial, leaning his chin on them so that, from the windows, he might appear the very pattern of idle friendliness—“I am unconsciously giving offence by questioning again. I will simply make statements, therefore, and not put questions. You do care for your bosom friend’s good name, and you do care for her peace of mind. Then remove the shadow of the gallows from her, dear one!”

“You dare propose to me to—”

“Darling, I dare propose to you. Stop there. If it be bad to idolise you, I am the worst of men; if it be good, I am the best. My love for you is above all other love, and my truth to you is above all other truth. Let me have hope and favour, and I am a forsworn man for your sake.”

Rosa put her hands to her temples and pushed back her hair, staring at him as if trying to piece together his purpose from the fragments he flung at her. The words he used—hope, favour, truth—were the same bright coins respectable men pass from hand to hand; in his mouth they were clipped and false, a dominant lexicon used to command and purchase. She could not translate what she felt into any phrase that would make it less vile; her very terror seemed to have no clean language.

“Reckon up nothing at this moment, angel,” he continued, “but the sacrifices that I lay at those dear feet, which I could fall down among the vilest ashes and kiss, and put upon my head as a poor savage might. There is my fidelity to my dear boy after death. Tread upon it!” He flung down the invisible offering with his hands. “There is the inexpiable offence against my adoration of you. Spurn it!” Another cast-down. “There are my labours in the cause of a just vengeance for six toiling months. Crush them!” Again. “There is my past and my present wasted life. There is the desolation of my heart and my soul. There is my peace; there is my despair. Stamp them into the dust; so that you take me, were it even mortally hating me!”

The vehemence that had been held back now surged up to its full height, and it terrified her so much that the spell which had pinned her broke at last. She moved swiftly towards the porch.

In an instant he was at her side, and his voice, close to her ear, changed—smoothed—self-repressed again, as if he had put a lid on boiling.

“Rosa, I am self-repressed again. I am walking calmly beside you to the house. I shall wait for some encouragement and hope. I shall not strike too soon. Give me a sign that you attend to me.”

Her hand moved—slightly, constrainedly—the smallest motion that could pass for obedience.

“Not a word of this to any one,” he breathed, “or it will bring down the blow, as certainly as night follows day. Another sign that you attend to me.”

Her hand moved again, as if it were not her hand.

“I love you, love you, love you! If you were to cast me off now—but you will not—you would never be rid of me. No one should come between us. I would pursue you to the death.”

The handmaid came out to open the gate. Jasper, as if nothing had occurred but ordinary civil speech in a garden, quietly pulled off his hat in parting salute, and went away with no more show of agitation than could be seen in the stony effigy of Mr. Sapsea’s father opposite.

Rosa, going up-stairs, fainted. They carried her carefully to her room and laid her on her bed. A thunderstorm was coming on, the maids said; the air was hot and stifling; no wonder the pretty dear was overset. They had felt their own knees all of a tremble all day long.

Outside, the summer hush thickened, and the sky gathered its heavy soundless weight—one more shadow moving toward the sun-dial of Cloisterham.

Rosa came back to herself with no blessed blank between. The interview was there entire—word, look, and that sickly warmth of breathless insistence—pursuing her as if her swoon had been only a darker room to be haunted in. She lay for a moment as one who has been wakened by a hand upon the throat, and the single thought that could hold its shape was: I must get away from him.

But where? And by what safe path?

She had never spoken her fear—its very name seemed to summon it—to any one save Helena. To run to Helena now and pour out all might be to loose the very mischief he had threatened, the mischief he boasted he could do and that she knew he would choose to do if thwarted. The more her imagination, excited and raw, made him loom, the heavier the charge upon her seemed; for a slip—one rash word, one delay—might turn his malice full upon Helena’s brother.

All these six months her mind had been like weather over harvest-fields—gust and lull, cloud and sudden glare—never settling. A suspicion, half-formed and never spoken, rose like a bubble from deep water and burst into nothing, then rose again with a more hateful roundness. Everyone in Cloisterham had been so full of Jasper’s absorption in his nephew while he lived, and of his relentless pursuit of the inquiry into the disappearance after, that it seemed to Rosa no one could conceive foul play at his hands. She asked herself, shamed and shivering, Am I so wicked as to imagine a wickedness others cannot? Then she asked if her recoil from him, even before Edwin vanished, had bred the suspicion—if so, might not that prove it baseless? Then she pressed herself with motive. And the answer, when it came, made her hide her face as if it were her own crime: the motive of gaining me. To found murder on such vanity seemed monstrous; the very thought left a smear, as though the idea itself had dirtied her.

She ran again over what he had said by the sun-dial in the garden. He had insisted—always—on murder, and that insistence was in keeping with his public zeal since the watch and pin had been found. If he feared discovery, would he not rather encourage the notion of Edwin’s voluntary flight? He had even declared that, if the ties between him and his nephew had been less strong, he might have swept “even him” away from her side. Was that like a man who had truly done it? He had spoken of laying six months’ labour for a just vengeance at her feet; he had ranged his efforts with his desolate soul, his wasted life, his peace and his despair. The first sacrifice he claimed to make for her was fidelity to his dear boy after death. These things stood up like witnesses against the fancy she scarcely dared let breathe.

And yet—he was terrible. Terrible in a way no neat common rule could measure. She, with her young heart and its honest scales, tried to weigh him as if he were an average man, and the weights would not answer; he belonged to some darker kind, a wonder apart.

So she could come by no other road to the same conclusion: he must be fled from.

She had been Helena’s support through all the dreary interval; had assured her, again and again, of her belief in Neville’s innocence and her sorrow for his misery. Yet Rosa had never once seen Neville since the disappearance; Helena had never breathed a word to her of what he had confessed to Mr. Crisparkle concerning Rosa—though it was talked of far and wide as part of the case. To Rosa, he was still only Helena’s unfortunate brother. The assurance she had flung at Jasper—that she believed Neville innocent—was strictly true; but now she could have bitten her tongue for having given him that triumph: for however frightened she was, her spirit swelled at the thought of his knowing her fear from her own lips.

Anywhere beyond his reach—yes; but “anywhere” was no place. Somewhere must be named, a door must be found.

She resolved to go to her guardian, at once.

The old dread she had whispered to Helena on the night of their first confidence—the dread of not being safe from him, of the thick convent walls being helpless against his ghostly following—returned and took command. No reasoning could quiet it. The fascination of repulsion that had long held her reached its black height now, and she felt as if he could bind her by spell. When she rose to dress and glanced from the window, the sight of the sun-dial—where he had leaned when he declared himself—turned her cold. It stood in its usual place, a homely stone circle telling honest time to honest flowers, yet she shrank from it as if he had breathed into it something baleful that would cling there.

She wrote a hurried note to Miss Twinkleton: she had sudden reason for wishing to see her guardian immediately, had gone to him, and begged the good lady not to be uneasy, for all was well. Then she crammed a few quite useless little articles into her very little bag, left the note where it must be found, and slipped out, softly closing the gate behind her.

It was the first time she had ever been alone in Cloisterham High Street. She knew its turns as well as she knew the folds of her own ribbons, but now every familiar corner had a different sound. The Cathedral’s presence was behind everything, as always; yet the street-noise—wheels over uneven stones, voices rebounding between walls—seemed sharper, less human, as if the town itself were listening with her.

She hurried to the corner where the omnibus started. It was moving off at that moment.

“Stop and take me, if you please, Joe. I am obliged to go to London.”

In less than another minute she was under Joe’s protection, jolting away to the railway. He waited on her at the station, found her a place, got her safely into the carriage, and handed in the little bag as though it were a great trunk of iron.

“Can you go round when you get back, and tell Miss Twinkleton that you saw me safely off, Joe?”

“It shall be done, Miss.”

“With my love, please, Joe.”

“Yes, Miss—and I wouldn’t mind having it myself!” Joe thought, but did not say.

Now, as the wheels took their steady hold of the rails and the country began to slide away, Rosa had leisure again for thought. For a while indignation bore her up—the sense that Jasper’s love had soiled her; that only an appeal to the honest and true could wash off the touch of it. But as the evening darkened and the great city drew near, doubts gathered like gnats about a lamp. Was this wild? How would Mr. Grewgious take it? Would he be there? What if he were absent? What could become of her—alone—in a place so strange and crowded? If she had waited for counsel first—if she could go back now, would she not do it gladly? Such questions multiplied in the rhythmic clatter, each one keeping time with the next.

At length the train came into London over the housetops, and the gritty streets lay below with lamps already burning though the hot summer light had not yet failed. Heat seemed to have baked the very air into powder. “Hiram Grewgious, Esquire, Staple Inn, London.” That was all she knew; but it was enough to send her rattling away again in a cab, deeper and deeper into the city.

They drove through long deserts of dingy streets where people crowded at the mouths of courts to catch a breath of air that had been breathed too many times already; where others shuffled along the hot paving-stones with a weary sameness, as if the town had taught their feet one dull tune. Music sounded here and there—barrel-organs, a big drum, chapel bells—but none of it cheered. It all seemed trapped between bricks, striking dust from every ledge and cornice. The wind-instruments were worst: flat, reedy notes that sounded as if their hearts had cracked from longing for open fields. Art, sold by the corner like roasted chestnuts, could not buy a clean breath; and the city’s great din, made of wheels and cries and clanging timepieces, lay on Rosa’s nerves like a hand.

At last the cab stopped before a fast-closed gateway, as if the place belonged to someone who went to bed early and mistrusted all mankind. Rosa paid and got down, very little bag and all, and knocked timidly. A watchman let her in.

“Does Mr. Grewgious live here?”

“Mr. Grewgious lives there, Miss,” said the watchman, pointing further in.

She went on, and when the clocks were striking ten, stood on P. J. T.’s doorsteps, wondering what P. J. T. had done with his street-door. Guided by the painted name, she climbed the stairs and tapped softly—once, twice, again. No one answered. The handle yielded to her touch. She went in.

Mr. Grewgious sat on a window-seat at an open window, with a shaded lamp set far from him on a table in a corner, so that the room was more twilight than light. The city’s sound crept in—far-off wheels, a call flung and caught and flung again—yet the chamber held a hush, like a pantry late at night when the household sleeps.

Rosa drew nearer. He saw her and said under his breath:

“Good Heaven!”

She fell upon his neck with tears. He held her, and in the same instant cried, startled out of himself:

“My child, my child! I thought you were your mother!—But what, what, what,” he added, softening, “has happened? My dear, what has brought you here? Who has brought you here?”

“No one. I came alone.”

“Lord bless me!” ejaculated Mr. Grewgious. “Came alone! Why didn’t you write to me to come and fetch you?”

“I had no time. I took a sudden resolution. Poor, poor Eddy!”

“Ah, poor fellow, poor fellow!”

“His uncle has made love to me. I cannot bear it,” said Rosa, with a burst of tears and a stamp of her little foot. “I shudder with horror of him, and I have come to you to protect me and all of us from him, if you will?”

“I will,” cried Mr. Grewgious, with a rush of energy that was astonishing in so angular a man. “Damn him! ‘Confound his politics! Frustrate his knavish tricks! On Thee his hopes to fix? Damn him again!’”

After this extraordinary outbreak he plunged about the room, as if uncertain whether he was rousing himself to loyal fervour or to personal combat. He stopped, wiped his face, and said:

“I beg your pardon, my dear, but you will be glad to know I feel better. Tell me no more just now, or I might do it again. You must be refreshed and cheered. What did you take last? Was it breakfast, lunch, dinner, tea, or supper? And what will you take next? Shall it be breakfast, lunch, dinner, tea, or supper?”

He went down on one knee before her with respectful tenderness, helped her remove her hat, and gently disentangled her pretty hair from it with hands that looked made for parchments, not for such work. Yet the care was true chivalry—quiet and exact—of a sort not to be expected from Mr. Grewgious by those who knew only his surface.

“Your rest too must be provided for,” he went on, “and you shall have the prettiest chamber in Furnival’s. Your toilet must be provided for, and you shall have everything that an unlimited head chambermaid—by which expression I mean a head chambermaid not limited as to outlay—can procure. Is that a bag?” He looked hard at it; it took hard looking at, in that dim room. “And is it your property, my dear?”

“Yes, sir. I brought it with me.”

“It is not an extensive bag,” said Mr. Grewgious, candidly, “though admirably calculated to contain a day’s provision for a canary-bird. Perhaps you brought a canary-bird?”

Rosa smiled and shook her head.

“If you had, he should have been made welcome,” said Mr. Grewgious, “and I think he would have been pleased to be hung upon a nail outside and pit himself against our Staple sparrows; whose execution must be admitted to be not quite equal to their intention. Which is the case with so many of us! You didn’t say what meal, my dear. Have a nice jumble of all meals.”

Rosa thanked him, but could only manage the idea of a cup of tea. Mr. Grewgious, after several hurried starts out and back again to mention supplementary items—marmalade, eggs, watercresses, salted fish, frizzled ham—ran across to Furnival’s without his hat to give directions.

Soon afterward those directions arrived made real, and the board was spread in that bachelor room, made suddenly domestic by steam and fragrance. When the lamp was set upon the table, the tea gave out a clean, comforting breath that seemed to soften even the London dust. The hot water hissed faintly, a small homely sound against the city’s distant grinding; and the scent of toasted bread and salt and ham rose like a promise that the body could be steadied when the mind would not.

“Lord bless my soul,” cried Mr. Grewgious, sitting opposite her, “what a new sensation for a poor old Angular bachelor, to be sure!”

Rosa’s eyebrows asked him what he meant.

“The sensation of having a sweet young presence in the place, that whitewashes it, paints it, papers it, decorates it with gilding, and makes it Glorious!” said Mr. Grewgious. “Ah me! Ah me!”

There was something mournful in his sigh. Rosa, touching him with her teacup in a little toast, ventured also to touch him with her small hand.

“Thank you, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious. “Ahem! Let’s talk!”

“Do you always live here, sir?” asked Rosa.

“Yes, my dear.”

“And always alone?”

“Always alone; except that I have daily company in a gentleman by the name of Bazzard, my clerk.”

“He doesn’t live here?”

“No, he goes his way after office hours. In fact, he is off duty here, altogether, just at present; and a firm down-stairs, with which I have business relations, lend me a substitute. But it would be extremely difficult to replace Mr. Bazzard.”

“He must be very fond of you,” said Rosa.

“He bears up against it with commendable fortitude if he is,” returned Mr. Grewgious, considering. “But I doubt if he is. Not particularly so. You see, he is discontented, poor fellow.”

“Why isn’t he contented?”

“Misplaced,” said Mr. Grewgious, with great mystery.

Rosa’s eyebrows resumed their inquisitive perplexity.

“So misplaced,” Mr. Grewgious went on, “that I feel constantly apologetic towards him. And he feels (though he doesn’t mention it) that I have reason to be.”

Mr. Grewgious had grown so very mysterious that Rosa did not know how to go on; and while she hesitated, his manner suddenly jerked itself into frankness again.

“Let’s talk. We were speaking of Mr. Bazzard. It’s a secret, and moreover it is Mr. Bazzard’s secret; but the sweet presence at my table makes me so unusually expansive, that I feel I must impart it in inviolable confidence. What do you think Mr. Bazzard has done?”

“O dear!” cried Rosa, drawing her chair a little nearer, her mind leaping back—by some cruel habit—to Jasper. “Nothing dreadful, I hope?”

“He has written a play,” said Mr. Grewgious, in a solemn whisper. “A tragedy.”

Rosa looked quite relieved.

“And nobody,” pursued Mr. Grewgious in the same tone, “will hear, on any account whatever, of bringing it out.”

Rosa nodded slowly, reflective, as if to say, Such things are, and why are they!

“Now, you know,” said Mr. Grewgious, “I couldn’t write a play.”

“Not a bad one, sir?” said Rosa, innocently, her eyebrows flickering again.

“No. If I was under sentence of decapitation, and was about to be instantly decapitated, and an express arrived with a pardon for the condemned convict Grewgious if he wrote a play, I should be under the necessity of resuming the block, and begging the executioner to proceed to extremities—meaning,” said Mr. Grewgious, passing his hand under his chin, “the singular number, and this extremity.”

Rosa tried to imagine herself in so awkward a case.

“Consequently,” said Mr. Grewgious, “Mr. Bazzard would have a sense of my inferiority to himself under any circumstances; but when I am his master, you know, the case is greatly aggravated.”

He shook his head gravely, as if the offence—his own—were indeed heavy.

“How came you to be his master, sir?” asked Rosa.

“A question that naturally follows,” said Mr. Grewgious. “Let’s talk. Mr. Bazzard’s father, being a Norfolk farmer, would have furiously laid about him with a flail, a pitch-fork, and every agricultural implement available for assaulting purposes, on the slightest hint of his son’s having written a play. So the son, bringing to me the father’s rent (which I receive), imparted his secret, and pointed out that he was determined to pursue his genius, and that it would put him in peril of starvation, and that he was not formed for it.”

“For pursuing his genius, sir?”

“No, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious, “for starvation. It was impossible to deny the position, that Mr. Bazzard was not formed to be starved, and Mr. Bazzard then pointed out that it was desirable that I should stand between him and a fate so perfectly unsuited to his formation. In that way Mr. Bazzard became my clerk, and he feels it very much.”

“I am glad he is grateful,” said Rosa.

“I didn’t quite mean that, my dear. I mean, that he feels the degradation. There are some other geniuses that Mr. Bazzard has become acquainted with, who have also written tragedies, which likewise nobody will on any account whatever hear of bringing out, and these choice spirits dedicate their plays to one another in a highly panegyrical manner. Mr. Bazzard has been the subject of one of these dedications. Now, you know, I never had a play dedicated to me!”

Rosa looked as if she would have liked him to have a thousand dedications, bound in gold.

“Which again, naturally, rubs against the grain of Mr. Bazzard,” said Mr. Grewgious. “He is very short with me sometimes, and then I feel that he is meditating, ‘This blockhead is my master! A fellow who couldn’t write a tragedy on pain of death, and who will never have one dedicated to him with the most complimentary congratulations on the high position he has taken in the eyes of posterity!’ Very trying, very trying. However, in giving him directions, I reflect beforehand: ‘Perhaps he may not like this,’ or ‘He might take it ill if I asked that;’ and so we get on very well. Indeed, better than I could have expected.”

“Is the tragedy named, sir?” asked Rosa.

“Strictly between ourselves,” answered Mr. Grewgious, “it has a dreadfully appropriate name. It is called The Thorn of Anxiety. But Mr. Bazzard hopes—and I hope—that it will come out at last.”

The little history, with its dry turns and its kindly intention, had done its work. It had taken Rosa, for a space, from the edge of her own terror, as a cup of warm tea takes the trembling from the hands. The city still made its ceaseless, dusty roar outside; but within, the smaller sounds—the clink of a spoon, the fold of a napkin—were like a remembered language spoken quietly in the midst of a crowd that shouts in a harsher tongue.

“And now, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious at length, “if you are not too tired to tell me more of what passed to-day—but only if you feel quite able—I should be glad to hear it. I may digest it the better, if I sleep on it to-night.”

Rosa, steadier now, told him faithfully all that had happened—Jasper’s words, his threats, his dreadful certainty, and the shadow they cast towards Helena and Neville. Mr. Grewgious smoothed his head often as she spoke, and begged her to repeat those parts that bore on Helena and on Helena’s brother. When she had finished, he sat grave and silent for a time, as if he were tasting and testing each ingredient of the story, turning it in his mind as a careful cook turns a sauce, lest it split.

“Clearly narrated,” was his only remark at last, “and, I hope, clearly put away here,” smoothing his head again.

He took her to the open window.

“See, my dear,” he said, “where they live! The dark windows over yonder.”

The distant windows, black squares in a blacker mass, gave no sign; yet the very direction of them seemed to tug at Rosa’s heart.

“I may go to Helena to-morrow?” she asked.

“I should like to sleep on that question to-night,” he answered, doubtfully. “But let me take you to your own rest, for you must need it.”

He helped her put on her hat again, took the very little bag upon his arm as though it were precious, and led her by the hand—stately and awkward, like a man about to walk a minuet—across Holborn into Furnival’s Inn.

At the hotel door he confided her to the Unlimited head chambermaid, saying he would remain below while she went up to see her room, in case she wished it changed or found anything wanting.

Rosa’s room was airy, clean, comfortable, almost gay. The Unlimited had supplied all that the little bag could never have held—that is to say, everything. The bed looked cool; the linen smelled of honest soap; and after London’s grit the mere notion of a basin of clean water was like mercy.

Rosa tripped down the many stairs again to thank her guardian for his thoughtfulness.

“Not at all, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious, infinitely gratified. “It is I who thank you for your charming confidence and for your charming company. Your breakfast will be provided for you in a neat, compact, and graceful little sitting-room (appropriate to your figure), and I will come to you at ten o’clock in the morning. I hope you don’t feel very strange indeed, in this strange place.”

“O no, I feel so safe!”

“Yes, you may be sure that the stairs are fire-proof,” said Mr. Grewgious, “and that any outbreak of the devouring element would be perceived and suppressed by the watchmen.”

“I did not mean that,” Rosa replied. “I mean, I feel so safe from him.”

“There is a stout gate of iron bars to keep him out,” said Mr. Grewgious, smiling; “and Furnival’s is fire-proof, and specially watched and lighted, and I live over the way!”

In the stoutness of his knight-errantry, he seemed to believe that last protection sufficient against any darkness. In the same spirit he said to the gate-porter as he went out:

“If some one staying in the hotel should wish to send across the road to me in the night, a crown will be ready for the messenger.”

And in the same spirit he walked up and down outside the iron gate for the best part of an hour, listening to the city’s harsh orchestra—wheels, calls, distant music bruised against brick—while now and then he peered between the bars as if he had placed a dove in a high roost in a cage of lions, and feared she might tumble out.

The night passed without so much as a moth’s flutter at the window; and in the morning Rosa rose as if sleep had been a cool cloth laid over a fevered brow. London, already awake, murmured beyond the panes—wheels worrying the stones, hawkers pitching their cries to the height of the houses, a hammer somewhere striking too early and too often. It was the kind of noise that did not belong to any one life, but to a thousand lives pressed together; and yet, in the rooms where she waited, there was a pocket of hush, like a cloistered garden set behind a market.

At ten, precisely as the clock gave out its strokes with a dry little authority, Mr. Grewgious arrived—and with him Mr. Crisparkle, fresh from his morning plunge in the river at Cloisterham, as if he had carried the sound of running water in his very voice.

“Miss Twinkleton was so uneasy, Miss Rosa,” he explained, warmth and apology in the same breath, “and came round to Ma and me with your note, in such a state of wonder, that, to quiet her, I volunteered on this service by the very first train to be caught in the morning. I wished at the time that you had come to me; but now I think it best that you did as you did, and came to your guardian.”

“I did think of you,” Rosa answered; “but Minor Canon Corner was so near him—”

“I understand. It was quite natural.”

“I have told Mr. Crisparkle,” said Mr. Grewgious, “all that you told me last night, my dear. Of course I should have written it to him immediately; but his coming was most opportune. And it was particularly kind of him to come, for he had but just gone.”

Rosa, who could not keep her thoughts from Helena even in the safe company of these two, brought the question back as one brings a kettle back to the fire.

“Have you settled what is to be done for Helena and her brother?”

Mr. Crisparkle’s open face clouded with honest perplexity.

“Why really, I am in great perplexity. If even Mr. Grewgious, whose head is much longer than mine, and who is a whole night’s cogitation in advance of me, is undecided, what must I be!”

At that moment, the door gave a cautious rap, and the Unlimited—having been authorised to present herself—put in her head with the air of one announcing weather.

“A gentleman wishes for a word with another gentleman named Crisparkle, if any such gentleman be here. If no such gentleman be here, he begs pardon for being mistaken.”

“Such a gentleman is here,” said Mr. Crisparkle, “but is engaged just now.”

Rosa, whose nerves had learned to start at the smallest change in air, retreated instinctively upon her guardian.

“Is it a dark gentleman?” she asked.

“No, Miss, more of a brown gentleman.”

“You are sure not with black hair?” Rosa persisted, taking a little courage from the answer.

“Quite sure of that, Miss. Brown hair and blue eyes.”

“Perhaps,” hinted Mr. Grewgious, with his habitual caution, “it might be well to see him, reverend sir, if you don’t object. When one is in a difficulty or at a loss, one never knows in what direction a way out may chance to open. It is a business principle of mine, in such a case, not to close up any direction, but to keep an eye on every direction that may present itself. I could relate an anecdote in point, but that it would be premature.”

“If Miss Rosa will allow me, then? Let the gentleman come in,” said Mr. Crisparkle.

The gentleman entered with a frank modesty, apologised for not finding Mr. Crisparkle alone, and then—turning on him with a smile that belonged to sunshine and out-of-doors—asked, without preface, the oddest question:

“Who am I?”

“You are the gentleman I saw smoking under the trees in Staple Inn, a few minutes ago,” returned Mr. Crisparkle, blinking as if to focus his memory as he would focus his sight.

“True. There I saw you. Who else am I?”

Mr. Crisparkle fixed his attention upon the sunburnt, handsome face. Something rose in him—some faded schoolroom, some echo of boys’ voices, some smell of old books and damp playing-fields—like a ghost of a departed boy that would not quite come clear in the daylight.

The stranger watched the struggle, and, smiling again, struck the flint that made the memory blaze.

“What will you have for breakfast this morning? You are out of jam.”

“Wait a moment!” cried Mr. Crisparkle, lifting his right hand as if to command the past to stand still. “Give me another instant! Tartar!”

They shook hands with a heartiness that made the room warmer. Then—going the wonderful length, for Englishmen—each laid a hand upon the other’s shoulder, and they looked, joyfully and squarely, face to face, as if time had merely stepped out for a moment and stepped in again.

“My old fag!” said Mr. Crisparkle.

“My old master!” said Mr. Tartar.

“You saved me from drowning!” cried Mr. Crisparkle.

“After which you took to swimming, you know!” returned Mr. Tartar.

“God bless my soul!”

“Amen!”

And again they fell to shaking hands, as if there were no other language that could bear the press of what they felt.

“Imagine,” exclaimed Mr. Crisparkle, his eyes bright, “Miss Rosa Bud and Mr. Grewgious—imagine Mr. Tartar, when he was the smallest of juniors, diving for me, catching me, a big heavy senior, by the hair of the head, and striking out for the shore with me like a water-giant!”

“Imagine my not letting him sink, as I was his fag!” said Mr. Tartar. “But the truth being that he was my best protector and friend, and did me more good than all the masters put together, an irrational impulse seized me to pick him up, or go down with him.”

“Hem! Permit me, sir, to have the honour,” said Mr. Grewgious, advancing with extended hand, “for an honour I truly esteem it. I am proud to make your acquaintance. I hope you didn’t take cold. I hope you were not inconvenienced by swallowing too much water. How have you been since?”

It was not at all clear that Mr. Grewgious knew exactly what he said, but it was perfectly clear he meant friendship in every syllable. Rosa, watching Mr. Tartar’s steady, capable figure, had one of those sharp thoughts that cut without warning: if Heaven had sent such courage and such handiness to her poor mother’s aid!—and he had been so slight and young then.

Mr. Grewgious, after a pause, began to walk across the room with sudden jog-trots so abrupt that they all stared, half thinking he was choking or seized with cramp. He trotted twice, stopped, and announced, with an air of discovery:

“I don’t wish to be complimented upon it, I thank you; but I think I have an idea. I think I have an idea. I believe I have had the pleasure of seeing Mr. Tartar’s name as tenant of the top set in the house next the top set in the corner?”

“Yes, sir,” returned Mr. Tartar. “You are right so far.”

“I am right so far,” repeated Mr. Grewgious. “Tick that off;” which he did, gravely, with his right thumb upon his left.

“Might you happen to know the name of your neighbour in the top set on the other side of the party-wall?” He came very close to Mr. Tartar, to lose nothing of his face, his short sight making nearness a kind of scrutiny.

“Landless.”

“Tick that off,” said Mr. Grewgious, and trotted again, and came back. “No personal knowledge, I suppose, sir?”

“Slight, but some.”

“Tick that off,” said Mr. Grewgious, and took another trot, and returned. “Nature of knowledge, Mr. Tartar?”

“I thought he seemed to be a young fellow in a poor way, and I asked his leave—only within a day or so—to share my flowers up there with him; that is to say, to extend my flower-garden to his windows.”

Mr. Grewgious’s face took on the composed look of a man laying papers in order.

“Would you have the kindness to take seats?” said he. “I have an idea!”

They sat: Mr. Crisparkle attentive as ever, Mr. Tartar good-humouredly at sea, and Rosa poised between dread and relief. Mr. Grewgious, in the centre, hands upon knees, delivered his “idea” as if he had learnt it by heart overnight and would not vary a word.

“I cannot as yet make up my mind whether it is prudent to hold open communication under present circumstances, and on the part of the fair member of the present company, with Mr. Neville or Miss Helena. I have reason to know that a local friend of ours”—here his thin mouth tightened—“(on whom I beg to bestow a passing but a hearty malediction, with the kind permission of my reverend friend) sneaks to and fro, and dodges up and down. When not doing so himself, he may have some informant skulking about, in the person of a watchman, porter, or such-like hanger-on of Staple. On the other hand, Miss Rosa very naturally wishes to see her friend Miss Helena, and it would seem important that at least Miss Helena (if not her brother too, through her) should privately know from Miss Rosa’s lips what has occurred, and what has been threatened. Am I agreed with generally in the views I take?”

“I entirely coincide with them,” said Mr. Crisparkle.

“As I have no doubt I should,” added Mr. Tartar, smiling, “if I understood them.”

“Fair and softly, sir,” returned Mr. Grewgious; “we shall fully confide in you directly, if you will favour us with your permission. Now, if our local friend should have any informant on the spot, it is tolerably clear that such informant can only be set to watch the chambers in the occupation of Mr. Neville. He reporting, to our local friend, who comes and goes there, our local friend would supply for himself, from his own previous knowledge, the identity of the parties. Nobody can be set to watch all Staple, or to concern himself with comers and goers to other sets of chambers: unless, indeed, mine.”

“I begin to understand to what you tend,” said Mr. Crisparkle, and the approving note in his voice sounded like a steadying hand laid upon a door that might otherwise slam.

“I needn’t repeat that I know nothing yet of the why and wherefore,” said Mr. Tartar; “but I also understand to what you tend, so let me say at once that my chambers are freely at your disposal.”

“There!” cried Mr. Grewgious, smoothing his head with triumph. “Now we have all got the idea. You have it, my dear?”

“I think I have,” said Rosa, blushing a little as Mr. Tartar looked quickly towards her, his blue eyes attentive in a way that made her feel both seen and safe.

“You see, you go over to Staple with Mr. Crisparkle and Mr. Tartar,” said Mr. Grewgious; “I going in and out, and out and in alone, in my usual way; you go up with those gentlemen to Mr. Tartar’s rooms; you look into Mr. Tartar’s flower-garden; you wait for Miss Helena’s appearance there, or you signify to Miss Helena that you are close by; and you communicate with her freely, and no spy can be the wiser.”

Rosa hesitated, and the hesitation was not fear exactly—more a scruple, like feeling one has entered a kitchen and touched what ought to be handled by the cook alone.

“I am very much afraid I shall be—”

“Be what, my dear?” asked Mr. Grewgious.

“Not frightened?” Mr. Crisparkle suggested.

“No, not that,” said Rosa, shyly; “in Mr. Tartar’s way. We seem to be appropriating Mr. Tartar’s residence so very coolly.”

“I protest to you,” Mr. Tartar returned, with a warmth that had nothing theatrical in it, “that I shall think the better of it for evermore, if your voice sounds in it only once.”

Rosa did not quite know what to say to that, and so did what she could do: she cast down her eyes, and turned to her guardian with dutiful practicality.

“Should I put my hat on?”

Mr. Grewgious, being of opinion she could not do better, nodded her away. While she withdrew, Mr. Crisparkle gave Mr. Tartar, in brief, the distress of Neville and his sister—how suspicion and solitude had closed about them like a net, and how necessity now demanded not merely kindness, but cautious contrivance. The summary took some minutes; Rosa’s hat, requiring a little extra fitting, took as many.

They went out together into London’s great restless mouth of sound. Mr. Tartar offered his arm; Rosa took it, and Mr. Crisparkle walked detached in front, as if to meet the stream and break it for them. Wheels rasped, voices clashed and overlapped, iron rang on iron; and through it all, Rosa’s mind kept returning, like a tongue to a sore place.

Poor, poor Eddy!

Mr. Tartar talked in an animated way, and as he did so he waved his right hand—broad and browned, a hand that looked made for rope and oar, for holding fast in wet weather. Rosa glanced at it.

It was not so powerful or so sun-browned when it saved Mr. Crisparkle; but it must have been very steady and determined even then.

He told her he had been a sailor, roving everywhere for years and years.

“When are you going to sea again?” she asked.

“Never!”

She wondered, with a sudden childish fancy, what the girls would say if they could see her crossing the wide street on a sailor’s arm. And she fancied the passers-by must think her very little and very helpless beside that strong figure—a figure that could have caught her up and carried her out of danger, miles and miles without resting. His far-seeing blue eyes looked as if they had been trained to watch peril come on from a long way off, and to watch it without flinching as it drew nearer and nearer.

Thinking that, she happened to raise her own eyes and found that he seemed—confusingly—to be thinking something about them.

After that, Rosebud never afterwards quite knew how she ascended (with his help) to his garden in the air; how the stair’s city echoes fell away; how the harsher sounds of London were screened and softened; and how, at the top, there opened a little miracle of green—a sudden bloom, as if a marvellous country had sprung up there like the country on the summit of a magic bean-stalk.

May it flourish for ever.

Mr. Tartar’s chambers had the wholesome severity of a well-found ship where every rope has its place and every man his duty. Even before one crossed his threshold, the house itself seemed to listen and then subside; the corridor’s fuss of footsteps and doors became, within those rooms, a softened, better-governed sound—as if the very air had been trained not to clatter. The floor-boards were scrubbed to that pale, honest cleanness that smells faintly of soap and warm wood, and the brass—hinges, knobs, little fittings you would never think to admire—answered the sun with a steady, disciplined gleam. It was not the glitter of ostentation; it was the shine of care repeated daily until it became a kind of language.

Everything had quarters. Charts lay flat and obedient in their drawers. Books stood in line like a watch relieved. Brushes, boots, clothes, bottles in their cases, instruments of glass and metal—each was stowed so that a hand could find it without searching, and so that nothing had to scrape against anything else. Even the room’s small noises were arranged: a drawer ran out without a squeal; a latch met its plate without a snap. In the sitting-room—his “admiral’s cabin”—the stillness was of the sort that lets you hear a sparrow in the square outside, or the faint, far complaint of London’s wheels, and be thankful you are not inside their grinding.

His bath-room was clean as a dairy—cool, bright, and smelling of water and rubbed stone—and his sleeping-chamber, with its lockers and cunning little contrivances, looked like a seedsman’s shop turned nautical: everything sorted, everything labelled by habit if not by ink. In the midst of it, his narrow cot moved almost imperceptibly, as if breathing with the house.

On that bright summer day an awning was rigged over his strip of flower-garden with sailorly neatness—tight as a sail, angled to make a cool shadow—and the whole place had a sea-going air, as though the stern-windows of a vessel had somehow docked among the dingy gables and chimneypots of the law’s neighbourhood. The flowers, too, seemed to have been persuaded into order: their colours were vivid, but not riotous; their scents rose cleanly through the shade—sweet-pea and mignonette, a sharp pinch of crushed green where leaves had been handled, and beneath it all the faint salt suggestion of shells and seaweed from his curiosities.

Mr. Tartar, doing the honours, matched his rooms: sunburnt, hearty, exact, and amused by his own exactness. He guided Rosa through each contrivance with a half-laugh that made it charming rather than fussy—showing her how a locker sprang at a touch, how a bracket folded, how a space that looked too narrow suddenly became useful. If he had an indulgence, it was not for luxury but for good condition; he took pleasure in things that were kept right.

When the inspection ended, he drew back with a quick delicacy, as if remembering that even a friendly ship’s captain must know when to quit the cabin.

“Consider it your own,” he said, with a frank gravity that made it feel—absurdly—true. “You are Queen there.” And with the same hand that had once held Mr. Crisparkle’s life steady in rough water, he waved her free of his little garden.

Rosa, left among the leaves and blossoms, leaned nearer to the shade where the flowers made a private bower. She lifted her voice, not loud—London punished loud voices with echo and eavesdropping.

“Helena! Helena Landless! Are you there?”

“Who speaks to me? Not Rosa?” came the answer, and Helena’s second handsome face appeared, dark-eyed and bright, as if the day’s heat had only brought more life into her.

“Yes, my darling!”

“Why, how did you come here, dearest?”

“I—I don’t quite know,” Rosa said, colouring, with that blush that springs up like a sudden heat from the heart—“unless I am dreaming!”

Helena smiled. “I am not dreaming. I should take more for granted if I were. How do we come together—or so near together—so very unexpectedly?”

Unexpected indeed: here, among P. J. T.’s smoky connection, in a garden that smelled faintly of salt voyages and newly watered earth. Rosa, waking as she spoke, poured out in a hurried stream how she had come—what she had been made to hear, what she had been made to feel, what she had fled from. The telling, even between flowers, had the rawness of a wound uncovered. Yet the place itself, with its regulated quiet, seemed to hold her up; it was easier to speak where doors did not slam and floors did not creak a lie.

“And Mr. Crisparkle is here,” Rosa ended quickly; “and, could you believe it? long ago he saved his life!”

“I could believe any such thing of Mr. Crisparkle,” Helena returned, and for a moment her face warmed with a look that was more than gratitude.

“Yes, but it wasn’t Mr. Crisparkle,” Rosa broke in, eager to be exact. “It was very nice of Mr. Crisparkle to be saved, and he couldn’t have shown his high opinion of Mr. Tartar more expressively. But it was Mr. Tartar who saved him.”

Helena’s eyes steadied on Rosa’s face among the leaves. “Is Mr. Tartar with you now, dear?” she asked, slower.

“No; because he has given up his rooms to me—to us, I mean. It is such a beautiful place!”

“Is it?”

“It is like the inside of the most exquisite ship that ever sailed. It is like—it is like—”

“Like a dream?” Helena suggested.

Rosa nodded a little and, to hide her confusion, bent her face to the flowers and breathed their scent, as if smell could be a sort of courage.

After a pause—during which Helena, looking outward, seemed to soften as though pitying someone not present—she said, “My poor Neville is reading in his own room, the sun being so very bright on this side just now. I think he had better not know that you are so near.”

“Oh, I think so too!” Rosa cried readily, with a start as if she had heard Neville’s footstep through the wall.

“I suppose,” Helena pursued, doubtfully, “that he must know by-and-by all you have told me; but I am not sure. Ask Mr. Crisparkle’s advice, my darling. Ask him whether I may tell Neville as much or as little of what you have told me as I think best.”

Rosa slipped back into her “state-cabin”—for so the little inner room felt now—and propounded it. Mr. Crisparkle, grave and tender, left the decision to Helena’s judgment.

“I thank him very much,” Helena said when Rosa returned. “Ask him whether it would be best to wait until any more maligning and pursuing of Neville on the part of this wretch shall disclose itself, or to try to anticipate it: I mean, so far as to find out whether any such goes on darkly about us?”

That question, poised between prudence and danger, troubled Mr. Crisparkle. He tried to shape an answer, failed, tried again, and at last—honest enough to admit difficulty—suggested referring it to Mr. Grewgious.

Helena assented. Mr. Crisparkle crossed the quadrangle to P. J. T.’s with a most unsuccessful pretence of lounging indifference (for even his boots, in that place, sounded like a man on urgent business). He stated the case. Mr. Grewgious, concise as a clerk’s note and as firm as a closed ledger, held to the principle that if you can steal a march upon a brigand or a wild beast, you should do it; and he held, with equal decisiveness, that John Jasper was a brigand and a wild beast combined.

Thus armed, Mr. Crisparkle returned; Rosa carried the counsel back to Helena. Helena, still at her window, followed her own thread of thought as if she were tracing a seam in cloth.

“We may count on Mr. Tartar’s readiness to help us, Rosa?” she asked.

“Oh yes!” Rosa shyly thought so; shyly believed she could almost answer for it; and then, in her old habit of leaning on another’s authority, faltered about asking Mr. Crisparkle.

“I think your authority on the point as good as his, my dear,” Helena said sedately. “And you needn’t disappear again for that.” It was odd of Helena—oddly confident in Rosa’s power to judge a man.

“You see, Neville,” Helena continued after more reflection, “knows no one else here: he has not so much as exchanged a word with any one else here. If Mr. Tartar would call to see him openly and often; if he would spare a minute for the purpose, frequently; if he would even do so, almost daily; something might come of it.”

“Something might come of it, dear?” Rosa repeated, perplexed. “Something might?”

“If Neville’s movements are really watched, and if the purpose really is to isolate him from all friends and acquaintance and wear his daily life out grain by grain—which would seem to be the threat to you—does it not appear likely,” Helena said, “that his enemy would in some way communicate with Mr. Tartar to warn him off from Neville? In which case, we might not only know the fact, but might know from Mr. Tartar what the terms of the communication were.”

“I see!” cried Rosa. And in a flash she darted back into the cabin.

She returned with colour heightened, her breath a little quick. She had told Mr. Crisparkle; Mr. Crisparkle had fetched Mr. Tartar; and Mr. Tartar—“who is waiting now, in case you want him,” Rosa added, half turning her head, confused between the cabin and the flowers—had declared his readiness to do as Helena suggested, and to begin that very day.

“I thank him from my heart,” Helena said. “Pray tell him so.”

Again Rosa dipped in with her message and dipped out with more assurances, standing for a moment as if she belonged equally to both places and to both people—Helena’s grave purpose and Mr. Tartar’s cheerful alacrity—so that her confusion looked less like awkwardness than like the innocent difficulty of being cared for.

“And now, darling,” Helena said, “we will be mindful of the caution that has restricted us to this interview for the present, and will part. I hear Neville moving too. Are you going back?”

“To Miss Twinkleton’s?” Rosa asked.

“Yes.”

“Oh, I could never go there any more. I couldn’t indeed, after that dreadful interview!” Rosa cried, the very name seeming to rasp like a harsh note in her ear.

“Then where are you going, pretty one?”

“Now I come to think of it, I don’t know,” Rosa admitted. “I have settled nothing at all yet, but my guardian will take care of me. Don’t be uneasy, dear. I shall be sure to be somewhere.”

It did seem likely.

“And I shall hear of my Rosebud from Mr. Tartar?” Helena asked.

“Yes, I suppose so; from—” Rosa looked back again in a flutter instead of saying Mr. Crisparkle’s name or any other. Then, with a sudden need that was half shame and half hunger for absolution, she caught Helena’s hand. “But tell me one thing before we part, dearest Helena. Tell me—that you are sure, sure, sure, I couldn’t help it.”

“Help it, love?”

“Help making him malicious and revengeful. I couldn’t hold any terms with him, could I?”

“You know how I love you, darling,” Helena answered, with indignation that had steel beneath it; “but I would sooner see you dead at his wicked feet.”

“That’s a great comfort to me! And you will tell your poor brother so, won’t you? And you will give him my remembrance and my sympathy? And you will ask him not to hate me?”

Helena shook her head mournfully, as if that entreaty were needless; Neville, whatever he suffered, was not one to waste hatred on Rosa. She kissed her two hands to her friend; Rosa kissed her hands back; and then Rosa saw a third hand—brown and strong—part the leaves and help Helena down and out of sight.

Mr. Tartar, by merely touching a spring knob here and a drawer-handle there, produced in the admiral’s cabin a repast that might have belonged to enchantment and yet was as practical as his stowage: macaroons crisp and fragrant with almond; little glasses of liqueur that caught the light like amber; tropical spices sealed up as carefully as evidence—cloves, cinnamon, nutmeg—each releasing, when opened, a sudden breath of far-off markets; and jellies of fruits that tasted of sun and sea-crossing, cool on the tongue, trembling with a delicate exactness. It was luxury, yes—but ordered, like everything else, so that nothing was wasted and nothing coarse intruded. Even the small sounds of eating—crack, sip, the faint tap of spoon on glass—seemed hushed by that disciplined peace of the rooms.

But Mr. Tartar could not make time stand still. Time, hard-hearted and fleet, pressed on. Rosa had to come down from the awning-shaded bean-stalk country of flowers and ship-shape wonder to the earth of her guardian’s chambers.

“And now, my dear,” said Mr. Grewgious, “what is to be done next? To put the same thought in another form; what is to be done with you?”

Rosa could only look apologetically sensible of being in her own way and everybody else’s. A faint, absurd idea of living fireproof up a good many stairs in Furnival’s Inn for the rest of her life was the only plan that presented itself.

“It has come into my thoughts,” said Mr. Grewgious, “that as the respected lady, Miss Twinkleton, occasionally repairs to London in the recess, with the view of extending her connection, and being available for interviews with metropolitan parents, if any—whether, until we have time in which to turn ourselves round, we might invite Miss Twinkleton to come and stay with you for a month?”

“Stay where, sir?” Rosa asked.

“Whether,” Mr. Grewgious explained, “we might take a furnished lodging in town for a month, and invite Miss Twinkleton to assume the charge of you in it for that period?”

“And afterwards?” Rosa hinted.

“And afterwards,” said Mr. Grewgious, “we should be no worse off than we are now.”

“I think that might smooth the way,” Rosa assented.

“Then let us,” said Mr. Grewgious, rising, “go and look for a furnished lodging. Nothing could be more acceptable to me than the sweet presence of last evening, for all the remaining evenings of my existence; but these are not fit surroundings for a young lady. Let us set out in quest of adventures, and look for a furnished lodging. In the meantime, Mr. Crisparkle here, about to return home immediately, will no doubt kindly see Miss Twinkleton, and invite that lady to co-operate in our plan.”

Mr. Crisparkle took the commission willingly and departed. Mr. Grewgious and Rosa set forth.

Mr. Grewgious’s method of looking at a furnished lodging was to place himself on the opposite side of the street from a house with a suitable bill and stare at it as if the bill might change under pressure of thought; then to work his way tortuously to the back and stare at that; then not go in, but repeat the ceremony elsewhere. The city’s noise—carts, calls, the occasional harsh whistle of some hurried modern contrivance—seemed to Rosa more troubling now that she had lately been in Mr. Tartar’s quiet order. The streets had their own dialect, loud and careless, grinding down distinctions; it made her long, oddly, for the soft click of Mr. Tartar’s drawers and the gentle hush of his awning.

At length Mr. Grewgious bethought himself of a widowed cousin, divers times removed, of Mr. Bazzard’s, who had once solicited his influence in the lodger world, and who lived in Southampton Street, Bloomsbury Square. On a brass door-plate, in uncompromising capitals of considerable size—and yet not lucid as to sex or condition—was the name BILLICKIN.

Personal faintness and overpowering candour were the distinguishing features of Mrs. Billickin’s organisation. She came languishing out of her own exclusive back parlour with the air of having been expressly brought-to for the purpose from an accumulation of several swoons; and even her voice sounded as if it had to travel through layers of shawl and sensibility.

“I hope I see you well, sir,” said Mrs. Billickin, recognising Mr. Grewgious with a bend.

“Thank you, quite well. And you, ma’am?” returned Mr. Grewgious.

“I am as well,” said Mrs. Billickin, becoming aspirational with excess of faintness, “as I hever ham.”

“My ward and an elderly lady,” said Mr. Grewgious, “wish to find a genteel lodging for a month or so. Have you any apartments available, ma’am?”

“Mr. Grewgious,” returned Mrs. Billickin, “I will not deceive you; far from it. I have apartments available.”

She spoke as if truth were a physical burden she had sworn never to set down.

“And now, what apartments, ma’am?” asked Mr. Grewgious, as though to soften a certain severity in her moral posture.

“There is this sitting-room—which, call it what you will, it is the front parlour, Miss,” said Mrs. Billickin, drawing Rosa into the matter with a reproachful air: “the back parlour being what I cling to and never part with; and there is two bedrooms at the top of the ’ouse with gas laid on. I do not tell you that your bedroom floors is firm, for firm they are not. The gas-fitter himself allowed, that to make a firm job, he must go right under your jistes, and it were not worth the outlay as a yearly tenant so to do. The piping is carried above your jistes, and it is best that it should be made known to you.”

Mr. Grewgious and Rosa exchanged looks of dismay, though neither had the least idea what particular catastrophe “above your jistes” might promise. Mrs. Billickin put her hand to her heart as if relieved to have confessed.

“Well! The roof is all right, no doubt,” said Mr. Grewgious, plucking up a little.

“Mr. Grewgious,” returned Mrs. Billickin, “if I was to tell you, sir, that to have nothink above you is to have a floor above you, I should put a deception upon you which I will not do. No, sir. Your slates WILL rattle loose at that elewation in windy weather, do your utmost, best or worst! I defy you, sir, be you what you may, to keep your slates tight, try how you can.”

Even her indignation had a sound—an airy quaver, a kind of moral creak like a stair protesting under virtue.

“Consequent,” she proceeded, cooling herself, “consequent it would be worse than of no use for me to trapse and travel up to the top of the ’ouse with you, and for you to say, ‘Mrs. Billickin, what stain do I notice in the ceiling, for a stain I do consider it?’ and for me to answer, ‘I do not understand you, sir.’ No, sir, I will not be so underhand. I do understand you before you pint it out. It is the wet, sir. It do come in, and it do not come in. You may lay dry there half your lifetime; but the time will come, and it is best that you should know it, when a dripping sop would be no name for you.”

Mr. Grewgious looked much disgraced by the prospect of being prefigured in that pickle.

“Have you any other apartments, ma’am?” he asked.

“Mr. Grewgious,” returned Mrs. Billickin solemnly, “I have. You ask me have I, and my open and my honest answer air, I have. The first and second floors is wacant, and sweet rooms.”

“Come, come! There’s nothing against them,” said Mr. Grewgious, comforting himself.

“Mr. Grewgious,” replied Mrs. Billickin, “pardon me, there is the stairs. Unless your mind is prepared for the stairs, it will lead to inevitable disappointment. You cannot, Miss,” said Mrs. Billickin, turning upon Rosa as if Rosa had personally invented gravity, “place a first floor, and far less a second, on the level footing of a parlour. No, you cannot do it, Miss, it is beyond your power, and wherefore try?”

“Can we see these rooms, ma’am?” asked Mr. Grewgious.

“Mr. Grewgious,” returned Mrs. Billickin, “you can. I will not disguise it from you, sir; you can.”

She sent into her back parlour for her shawl—because she could never, by an ancient household fiction, go anywhere without being wrapped up—then, having been enrolled by her attendant, led the way. She made various genteel pauses on the stairs for breath, and clutched at her heart in the drawing-room as if it had nearly got loose and she had caught it just as it took wing.

The first floor proved satisfactory.

“And the second floor?” said Mr. Grewgious.

“Mr. Grewgious,” replied Mrs. Billickin, turning with ceremony as if an important doctrine must now be established, “the second floor is over this.”

“Can we see that too, ma’am?”

“Yes, sir,” returned Mrs. Billickin. “It is open as the day.”

That also proved satisfactory. Mr. Grewgious retired into a window with Rosa for a few words, then asked for pen and ink and sketched out a line or two of agreement.

Mrs. Billickin, seated, delivered an abstract of the general question as if reading out conditions of weather.

“Five-and-forty shillings per week by the month certain at the time of year is only reasonable to both parties. It is not Bond Street nor yet St. James’s Palace; but it is not pretended that it is. Neither is it attempted to be denied—for why should it?—that the Arching leads to a mews. Mewses must exist. Respecting attendance; two is kep’, at liberal wages. Words has arisen as to tradesmen, but dirty shoes on fresh hearth-stoning was attributable, and no wish for a commission on your orders. Coals is either by the fire, or per the scuttle.”

She laid such weight upon those prepositions that they sounded like law.

“Dogs is not viewed with favour. Besides litter, they gets stole, and sharing suspicions is apt to creep in, and unpleasantness takes place.”

By then Mr. Grewgious had his agreement-lines and his earnest-money ready.

“I have signed it for the ladies, ma’am,” he said, “and you’ll have the goodness to sign it for yourself, Christian and Surname, there, if you please.”

“Mr. Grewgious,” said Mrs. Billickin, bursting into a new candour, “no, sir! You must excuse the Christian name.”

Mr. Grewgious stared at her.

“The door-plate is used as a protection,” said Mrs. Billickin, “and acts as such, and go from it I will not.”

Mr. Grewgious stared at Rosa.

“No, Mr. Grewgious, you must excuse me. So long as this ’ouse is known indefinite as Billickin’s, and so long as it is a doubt with the riff-raff where Billickin may be hidin’, near the street-door or down the airy, and what his weight and size, so long I feel safe. But commit myself to a solitary female statement, no, Miss! Nor would you for a moment wish,” said Mrs. Billickin, injured into emphasis, “to take that advantage of your sex, if you were not brought to it by inconsiderate example.”

Rosa reddened as if she had attempted the most disgraceful overreaching. She begged Mr. Grewgious to be content with any signature, and so, in a baronial manner, the sign-manual BILLICKIN was appended.

They settled details for taking possession on the next day but one, when Miss Twinkleton might reasonably be expected; and then Rosa went back to Furnival’s Inn on her guardian’s arm, through the street’s jangling talk and wheels and cries, longing again for that ordered quiet where even a latch knew how to behave.

Behold Mr. Tartar walking up and down Furnival’s Inn, checking himself when he saw them coming, and advancing with a prompt, respectful ease.

“It occurred to me,” he hinted, “that we might go up the river, the weather being so delicious and the tide serving. I have a boat of my own at the Temple Stairs.”

“I have not been up the river for this many a day,” said Mr. Grewgious, tempted.

“I was never up the river,” added Rosa.

Though Mr. Crisparkle and John Jasper passed each other daily beneath the Cathedral roof—meeting in the same flagged passages where the air always smelt faintly of damp stone and old incense—no word ever again, at any time, was exchanged between them that bore upon Edwin Drood, after that day—more than half a year gone—when Jasper had opened his diary in silence and shown the Minor Canon the cold conclusion, the resolved intention, written there like a judgment.

It could not be that they met so often without the thought of Edwin turning back upon them, each time, like a refrain. Nor could it be that they met without the uneasy sense—quick as the echo of a footstep in a transept—that the other kept a locked chamber in his mind. Jasper, publicly the denouncer and pursuer of Neville Landless; Mr. Crisparkle, steadfast advocate and protector of the accused; their paths crossed in a quiet opposition that should have bred questions. Yet neither broached the theme.

False pretence was not in Mr. Crisparkle. If he longed to revive the subject, he would have shown it. Jasper’s reticence, however, was a shut door with the bolt shot. Impassive, moody, solitary, living as if he kept his own company under arrest, he held himself to one idea and to the fixed purpose attendant on it, sharing it with no creature. That he continued to practise an Art which required him to be in exact mechanical unison with other men—voice with voice, breath with breath, the organ’s measured thunder answered by a choir’s disciplined rise—made it the more curious that his spirit kept moral time with nothing around him. He had confessed as much to his lost nephew long before the present iron stiffness had need to show itself.

That he must know of Rosa’s abrupt flight, and must divine the cause, could scarcely be doubted. Had he frightened her into silence? Or did he suppose she had confided—perhaps to Mr. Crisparkle himself—the particulars of that last interview? The Minor Canon could not settle it in his mind. He could not but admit, as a just man, that it was not in itself a crime to fall in love with Rosa, any more than it was a crime to offer to set love above revenge.

The dreadful suspicion of Jasper, which had entered Rosa’s imagination like a poisonous fume in a close room, found no harbour in Mr. Crisparkle’s. If it haunted Helena’s thoughts or Neville’s, neither gave it one spoken syllable. Mr. Grewgious made no pains to conceal his dislike of Jasper, yet never referred it, however distantly, to such a source. He was reticent as well as eccentric; and he never mentioned a certain evening when he had warmed his hands at the gatehouse fire and looked steadily down upon a heap of torn, miry clothes upon the floor—as if studying a recipe he could not read.

Drowsy Cloisterham, waking at intervals to reconsider a story six months old and already dismissed by the bench, divided itself, as it divided itself on everything, between three opinions: that Edwin had been killed by his treacherously passionate rival; or had died in open struggle; or had, for his own purposes, slipped away. Then it lifted its head to observe that bereaved Jasper still clung to discovery and revenge; and dozed off again. So the town’s mind lay, like bread dough left too long near a cold hearth—sometimes stirring, never rising.

The Cathedral doors close for the night; and the choir-master, on short leave for two or three services, turns his face towards London. He travels thither by the means by which Rosa travelled, and arrives, as Rosa arrived, on a hot dusty evening when the city’s breath tastes of grit and coal-smoke and over-used oil.

His baggage is small enough to carry in one hand. He goes on foot to a hybrid establishment in a little square behind Aldersgate Street, near the General Post Office—a place that is hotel, boarding-house, or lodging-house at the visitor’s option. It is one of those timid new enterprises, just beginning to spring up, that almost apologises for offering a fixed charge instead of inviting a traveller to order a pint of sweet blacking for drinking and throw it away. It insinuates you may black your boots rather than your stomach, and have bed, breakfast, attendance, and a porter up all night, for so much. From such premises many true Britons, in the lowest spirits, deduce that the times are levelling times—except in the high roads, soon to be none at all.

He eats without appetite, food lying dead on his tongue, and goes forth again. Eastward and still eastward through stale streets—where every alley has its own sour note, and every cheap gin-shop throws a thin music of clinking glasses and quarrelling voices into the air—he makes his way until he reaches his destination: a miserable court, specially miserable among many such.

He climbs a broken staircase whose steps complain under him, opens a door, looks into a dark stifling room, and says:

“Are you alone here?”

“Alone, deary; worse luck for me, and better for you,” croaks a voice from within. “Come in, come in, whoever you be. I can’t see you till I light a match, yet I seem to know the sound of your speaking. I’m acquainted with you, ain’t I?”

“Light your match, and try.”

“So I will, deary, so I will; but my hand that shakes, as I can’t lay it on a match all in a moment. And I cough so, that, put my matches where I may, I never find ’em there. They jump and start, as I cough and cough, like live things. Are you off a voyage, deary?”

“No.”

“Not seafaring?”

“No.”

“Well, there’s land customers and there’s water customers. I’m a mother to both. Different from Jack Chinaman t’other side the court. He ain’t a father to neither. It ain’t in him. And he ain’t got the true secret of mixing, though he charges as much as me that has, and more if he can get it. Here’s a match—and now where’s the candle? If my cough takes me, I shall cough out twenty matches afore I gets a light.”

She finds the candle and lights it before the cough comes on. It seizes her in the instant of success, and she sits rocking and gasping, the flame wagging in sympathy with her body:

“O, my lungs is awful bad! my lungs is wore away to cabbage-nets!”

While the fit lasts she has no power of sight, nor any other power not swallowed up in the struggle. As it leaves her, she strains her eyes; and as soon as she can get breath to words, she cries, staring hard:

“Why, it’s you!”

“Are you so surprised to see me?”

“I thought I never should have seen you again, deary. I thought you was dead, and gone to Heaven.”

“Why?”

“I didn’t suppose you could have kept away, alive, so long, from the poor old soul with the real receipt for mixing it. And you are in mourning too! Why didn’t you come and have a pipe or two of comfort? Did they leave you money, perhaps, and so you didn’t want comfort?”

“No.”

“Who was they as died, deary?”

“A relative.”

“Died of what, lovey?”

“Probably, Death.”

“We are short to-night!” she cries, with a propitiatory laugh that ends in a wheeze. “Short and snappish we are! But we’re out of sorts for want of a smoke. We’ve got the all-overs, haven’t us, deary? But this is the place to cure ’em in; this is the place where the all-overs is smoked off.”

“You may make ready, then,” he replies, “as soon as you like.”

He takes off his shoes, loosens his cravat, and lies across the foot of the squalid bed, his head resting on his left hand. The room holds the odour of old breath, damp rags, and the sweetish, resinous promise of the drug—like bruised poppy heads and burnt sugar.

“Now you begin to look like yourself,” says the woman, approvingly. “Now I begin to know my old customer indeed! Been trying to mix for yourself this long time, poppet?”

“I have been taking it now and then in my own way.”

“Never take it your own way. It ain’t good for trade, and it ain’t good for you. Where’s my ink-bottle, and where’s my thimble, and where’s my little spoon? He’s going to take it in a artful form now, my deary dear!”
